Extensional-Projective Order Geometry  
Foundations of Holographic Extensional Mathematics

Working status: Foundation monograph / research scaffold  
Version: Draft 0.1

Purpose of this document

This Google Doc is the working home for the heavier foundation text of Holographic Extensional Mathematics. It is separate from the Practical Guide. The Practical Guide is the accessible, application-facing projection; this work is the source-bearing foundation text for sophisticated readers.

Core posture

This work introduces Extensional-Projective Order Geometry as a geometry-first modelling framework. It is not presented as established mathematics, empirical physics, or a replacement for existing formal systems. It is offered as a structured attempt to recover a geometric mode of reasoning that has often been hidden beneath algebraic, symbolic, computational, and linguistic representations.

The central proposal is simple but demanding: before we calculate, classify, prove, optimise, or interpret, we may ask what kind of extension is being treated, what boundary makes it intelligible, what projection renders it visible, what transformation preserves or changes it, what structure is lost in the rendering, and under what context the resulting view may be used.

This work calls that discipline Holographic Extensional Mathematics, not because it claims that physical reality is literally a hologram, but because many mathematical, cognitive, computational, and observational situations appear to involve lower-order views of richer source structures. A line may be a boundary of a surface. A surface may be a projection of a body. A momentary body may be a slice of a history. A history may be one path through a field of possible variation. A law may govern the admissible transformations of that field. A context may select, render, measure, or interpret what appears.

The word “holographic” is therefore used cautiously. It does not mean that every part literally contains the whole without loss. It means that a lower-order appearance may preserve selected structure from a richer source while losing other structure. The task of the theory is not to mystify this relation, but to discipline it.

Extensional-Projective Order Geometry begins from seven modelling orders:

D0 — point-locality or local mark  
D1 — line-extension or path  
D2 — surface-extension or field  
D3 — form-extension or spatial body  
D4 — duration-extension or world-form  
D5 — variation-extension or possibility field  
D6 — transformation-extension or law-space  
D7 — context-extension or projection governance

These are not asserted to be physical dimensions. They are not coordinate axes, metrics, scores, or metaphysical planes. They are order labels: ways in which a thing may be treated as extended, projected, transformed, sliced, or interpreted.

The framework therefore has two complementary directions.

The first direction is extensional ascent. It asks how a thing is built upward: from locality to line, from line to surface, from surface to form, from form to duration, from duration to variation, from variation to transformation, and from transformation to context.

The second direction is projective descent. It asks how a richer source may appear downward: as a trace, slice, shadow, diagram, equation, record, measurement, dashboard, proof text, interface, or local experience.

These two directions are not the same. Extension is not projection. Projection is not identity. Projection is not reconstruction. A lower-order view may be compatible with many higher-order sources. Therefore, any serious use of this framework must declare not only what is preserved, but also what is lost.

This is the governing discipline of the present work.

The aim is not to replace algebra, set theory, topology, category theory, proof theory, type theory, computation theory, or empirical science. The aim is to provide a geometry-first modelling language that can speak to these domains without pretending to absorb them. Algebra remains powerful, but in this work algebraic expressions are usually treated as renderings, compressions, invariants, transformation calculi, or reconstruction constraints. A coordinate equation is not automatically the geometric object. A proof text is not automatically the proof object. A dashboard is not the system. An AI-generated answer is not proof. A measurement is not the whole state of what is measured.

The practical question of this book is therefore:

What must be declared before a lower-order appearance may be responsibly used as a view of a richer structure?

The theoretical question is:

Can a disciplined geometry-first language help us reason across mathematics, computation, proof, cognition, physics-facing interpretation, and human modelling without collapsing metaphor into proof?

This book answers cautiously. It proposes a framework, not a finished theorem. It offers a modelling calculus, not a universal physics. It invites formalisation, criticism, bridge work, and practical testing.

Its value, if it has value, will not lie in grand claims. It will lie in whether the framework helps a reader see preserved structure, lost structure, hidden projection, false reconstruction, confused context, or premature certainty more clearly than before.

How to read this book

This book is a foundations draft for E7G-T: Extensional-Projective Order Geometry.

It should be read as the conceptual foundation of a geometry-first modelling language and practical calculus. It is not a textbook in established mathematics, not a physics theory, not a proof assistant manual, not a metaphysical doctrine, and not a replacement for mature domain methods.

The central purpose of the book is simple:

make projection visible;  
make loss visible;  
make boundary visible;  
make context visible;  
make reconstruction status visible;  
make bridge strength visible;  
make the next admissible move visible.

The book asks readers to notice a recurring problem: many artefacts we rely on are projections. A formula is a projection of structure into symbolic form. A diagram is a projection of relations onto a surface. A dashboard is a projection of a business system into selected metrics. A proof text is a projection of proof work into readable form. A translation is a projection of a source text into another language under purpose constraints. An AI answer is a projection of generated text into user-facing fluency. A measurement is a projection of an observed system into a recorded value under protocol.

The practical question is therefore not only whether a representation is useful. It is also:

What does it preserve?  
What does it lose?  
What source or candidate source does it point toward?  
What context produced it?  
What can responsibly be reconstructed from it?  
What claim strength does it support?  
What use should be blocked?

Reading mode 1: foundation

In the foundation reading, this book develops the core categories of E7G-T: extension, projection, boundary, incidence, slice, invariant, transformation, reconstruction, temporal regime, bridge mode, and admissible use.

Reading mode 2: practical calculus

In the practical reading, this book becomes a tool for asking what to do next with a concrete object, model, proof, claim, output, system, observation, or representation.

The practical reader should pay special attention to OneLine, MiniCard, FullCard, primitive moves, practical laws, Proof-of-Path, RealityBridgeCard, and failure-mode checks.

Reading mode 3: bridge discipline

In the bridge reading, this book explains how to speak responsibly across domains. It allows philosophical, contemplative, fictional, formal, computational, mathematical, empirical, and operational uses, but it requires each bridge to declare its mode.

The most important bridge rule is:

Do not upgrade claim strength by rhetoric.

A metaphor is not proof.  
A formal analogy is not empirical evidence.  
A useful model is not automatically reality.  
A proof path is not automatically a proof.  
A diagram is not the source.  
A projection is not reconstruction.

What this book does not claim

This book does not claim that E7G-T is established mathematics.

It does not claim that D0–D7 are literal physical dimensions.

It does not claim that temporal regimes are a final theory of time.

It does not claim that holographic reading proves a holographic universe.

It does not claim that E7G-T replaces algebra, topology, category theory, type theory, proof theory, physics, statistics, translation studies, software verification, legal reasoning, medical reasoning, or any other mature discipline.

It does not claim to solve open mathematical or scientific problems merely by reframing them.

What this book does claim

This book claims that many reasoning failures become easier to see when we ask geometry-first questions before accepting a representation, proof, model, measurement, translation, dashboard, or output.

It claims that extension and projection should not be confused.

It claims that every projection deserves preservation/loss accounting.

It claims that reconstruction is usually harder than projection.

It claims that context and boundary must be declared before strong use.

It claims that bridge mode must be declared before cross-domain claims are trusted.

It claims that the next admissible move is often clearer once entity, boundary, projection, loss, and claim strength are made explicit.

How to use the notation

The notation in this book is a recording tool, not a substitute for thought.

A compact expression such as:

X : Dn @ TRi / C

means that X is being treated as order Dn under temporal regime TRi within context C.

The notation does not say what X ultimately is. It says how X is being modelled in the current move.

When notation becomes cramped, prefer readable prose.

A clean OneLine is often better than an overloaded formula.

Relationship to other E7G-T documents

This foundations book is the explanatory source layer.

The kernel is the compact operational rule-set extracted from it.

The practical guide should be the accessible, example-driven version for everyday use.

The Application Atlas should contain disciplined worked examples across domains.

The Reality Bridge Atlas should contain cautious bridge entries for physics-facing, metaphysical, fictional, computational, scientific, and contemplative applications.

These are different projections of the same framework. They should not be confused.

Reader’s compact checklist

When reading any chapter, keep the following questions in mind:

What is the entity of concern?  
What order profile is active?  
What temporal regime is active?  
What boundary is being used?  
What is being extended?  
What is being projected?  
What is preserved?  
What is lost?  
What is being reconstructed, if anything?  
What bridge mode is active?  
What claim strength is justified?  
What overread is blocked?  
What is the next admissible move?

This checklist is the simplest way to read the book.

Chapter 1 — The Under-Geometrised Condition

1.1 The late start problem

Many failures of reasoning begin before reasoning visibly begins. They begin at the moment when a situation is accepted in the wrong form.

A formula appears, and we treat the formula as the thing. A chart appears, and we treat the chart as the state of the system. A proof text appears, and we treat the text as the proof. A measurement appears, and we treat the measurement as the object measured. A dashboard shows a green indicator, and we treat the green indicator as health. An artificial intelligence system produces fluent language, and we treat the fluency as understanding or proof.

In each case, the mistake is not merely that we lack more data. The mistake is structural. We begin from a lower-order appearance and silently grant it the authority of a richer source. We start too late.

By “too late” this work means: after projection has already occurred, after boundary has already been chosen, after a representation scheme has already shaped the object, after a context has already selected what may appear, and after loss has already entered the situation. We then reason from the presented result as if it were primary.

Extensional-Projective Order Geometry begins earlier. It asks what had to be true for the appearance to appear as it does. What was extended? What was bounded? What was projected? What was rendered? What was preserved? What was lost? What source, if any, may responsibly be reconstructed from the view? What context authorized this presentation? What use is admissible?

The under-geometrised condition is the habit of accepting symbols, texts, outputs, diagrams, measurements, interfaces, and formulae as if they arrived without prior geometry. It is the habit of treating representation as if it were not already a spatial, structural, projective, and contextual event.

This condition is not a defect of algebra, computation, language, or formalism. Algebra and formalism are among the most powerful inventions of human thought. The problem is not that we use them. The problem is that we often forget what they have already done to the object by the time they arrive in front of us.

1.2 Representation is not neutral

A representation does not merely show. It selects.

A coordinate system selects axes, origin, units, and allowed transformations. A diagram selects visual salience, scale, and visible relations. A proof text selects a linear path through a proof object. A graph selects nodes, edges, weights, and the meaning of connection. A dashboard selects indicators, aggregation windows, thresholds, and colour rules. A sentence selects grammar, viewpoint, tense, and implied agency. A model output selects one response from a space of possible responses.

None of these selections is automatically wrong. Without selection, nothing becomes usable. But every selection has a cost. Some structure is preserved; other structure is lost, hidden, compressed, or made difficult to recover.

The central error of under-geometrised reasoning is not simplification itself. Simplification is necessary. The error is unpriced simplification: a projection whose losses are not declared.

A map may be excellent for navigation and useless for geology. A medical test may be useful for a diagnostic question and useless for a whole-person account. A profit figure may be useful for one financial comparison and useless for judging organisational health. A theorem statement may be precise and still hide the construction path by which it became intelligible. A translation may preserve legal force while losing rhetorical texture. A philosophical metaphor may reveal a pattern while proving nothing about the physical world.

The responsible question is therefore not “Is this representation true?” in the abstract. The responsible question is: true enough for what use, under what projection, with what loss, and with what source-return condition?

This is why Extensional-Projective Order Geometry treats representation as a D7 matter: context, rendering, measurement, interpretation, projection governance, and admissible use belong to the same family of concerns. They are not afterthoughts appended to a neutral object. They are part of the way the object is made available to thought.

1.3 Algebra as a powerful projection

Modern mathematics often teaches us to meet objects through symbolic form. We encounter the equation, the coordinate tuple, the function, the set, the matrix, the graph, the type signature, the proof script. These forms are extraordinarily useful because they make manipulation possible. They compress structure into a carrier that can be transformed, calculated, checked, transported, and reused.

But compression is not identity.

The equation of a circle is not simply the circle. It is a rendering of circular structure under a coordinate context. It preserves certain invariants with great power. It hides other things: construction history, diagrammatic intuition, physical embodiment, alternative coordinate renderings, and the difference between the formal object and any drawn or measured instance.

Likewise, a function in code is not simply the transformation it describes. It is a textual and computational rendering of an intended transformation, executed under runtime conditions, dependencies, state assumptions, side effects, and environmental constraints. Its documentation is another projection. Its tests are selected traces. Its logs are records of occurrences, not the transformation law itself.

A proof text is not automatically the proof object. A proof assistant script is not merely prose, but neither is it the whole mathematical situation. It is a formal carrier that renders a proof path under a language, kernel, library, tactic system, and accepted formal substrate. Its validity depends on those conditions.

To say this is not to diminish algebra or formal proof. It is to protect them. When algebra is treated as a rendering, its power becomes clearer because its scope becomes clearer. We can ask which structure it preserves, which transformations it supports, which invariants it exposes, and which contexts it requires.

Geometry-first reasoning therefore does not reject algebra. It asks algebra to disclose its geometry.

1.4 The first repair: name the entity of concern

The first repair for under-geometrised reasoning is simple: name the entity of concern.

Are we talking about the geometric object, the equation, the diagram, the measurement, the proof text, the proof object, the computation, the log, the dashboard, the AI answer, the user interface, the underlying system, or the publication that presents it?

This distinction often feels pedantic until it prevents a serious mistake.

Consider the statement: “The dashboard says the business is healthy.” The entity of concern may be the dashboard, the business, the metric system, the underlying transaction records, the reporting process, or the management decision that will rely on the dashboard. Each choice changes the modelling move. If the entity is the dashboard, we inspect layout, metrics, freshness, thresholds, and source links. If the entity is the business, the dashboard is only a projection. If the entity is the decision, we need admissibility, evidence, and risk boundaries.

Consider the statement: “The AI explained the concept correctly.” The entity of concern may be the answer text, the model output event, the source-backed claim, the explanation usefulness, the user’s understanding, or the factual state of the domain. A fluent answer may be a useful projection, but it is not proof of correctness. For factual reliance, the answer must return to sources, evidence, or formal checks.

Consider the statement: “The equation represents the system.” Which system? A physical system, a simulated system, a formal system, a simplified model, a local state, a family of trajectories, or a law-space? The equation may preserve one invariant while losing boundary conditions, parameter uncertainty, measurement error, hidden variables, or context of validity.

The entity-of-concern repair is the first act of intellectual hygiene. It prevents the visible carrier from swallowing the hidden object.

1.5 Boundary before operation

Once the entity of concern is named, the next question is boundary.

What makes this thing this thing rather than a blur? Where does it begin and end for the present use? What belongs inside the object, what remains outside, and what crosses the boundary?

Boundary is not always physical. A line segment has endpoint boundaries. A surface patch has a boundary curve or line-complex. A software module has an interface boundary. A proof has assumptions, rules, allowed transformations, and a formal context. A concept has a bounded context and contrast cases. A measurement has an instrument boundary and a measurement protocol. A business metric has a data boundary, aggregation boundary, and time window.

Without boundary, operation becomes premature. We cannot responsibly calculate, compare, optimise, or reconstruct when we have not yet said what is being operated on.

This is why the practical calculus begins with placement and boundary before projection, reconstruction, or bridge claims. A thing must be placed, at least provisionally, before an operator is applied. The notation may later become compact, but the discipline remains simple: no operation without object placement; no projection without loss; no reconstruction without rival sources; no invariant without transformation class; no empirical claim without empirical bridge.

Boundary also prevents a common inflation of meaning. If a local slice of a larger process is shown, the slice is not the whole. If a text records a transformation, the text is not the transformation occurrence. If a measurement registers one characteristic, the measurement is not the full state. If a metaphor illuminates a structure, the metaphor is not proof that the target domain literally has the source structure.

1.6 Projection as a normal event

Projection is not an exotic idea. It is one of the most ordinary events in reasoning.

A shadow is a projection. A map is a projection. A diagram is a projection. A coordinate tuple is a projection. A screenshot is a projection. A summary is a projection. A translation is a projection. A proof text is a projection. A dashboard is a projection. A local experience of a changing system is a projection of a selected slice under context.

The problem is that ordinary reasoning often treats projection as if it were transparent. It looks through the projection toward the supposed object without asking what the projection did.

Extensional-Projective Order Geometry makes projection explicit. A projection has a source or candidate source, a target view, a rule or rendering method, a context, preserved structure, lost structure, distortion risk, and a source-return condition.

This does not make every use heavy. Many ordinary uses can remain light. A map user does not need a philosophical treatise before walking to the station. But the moment a projection supports a stronger claim, controls a decision, crosses a domain boundary, or becomes evidence, the projection must become accountable.

The practical rule is proportionality: use the smallest modelling level that changes the next move without hiding risk. A quick orientation may need only a OneLine. A repeated modelling situation may need a MiniCard. A publication, formal claim, empirical bridge, or operational reliance may need a FullCard.

1.7 Reconstruction is not projection in reverse

One of the most dangerous mistakes in reasoning is to treat reconstruction as if it were simply projection in reverse.

If a three-dimensional object casts a two-dimensional shadow, many different objects may cast the same shadow. If a system produces one measurement trace, many different internal states may explain it. If a person gives one answer, many different mental processes may have led to it. If a theory predicts one observation, rival theories may also predict it. If a lower-order view resembles a higher-order pattern, the resemblance does not prove the higher-order source.

Projection may be many-to-one. Reconstruction is therefore underdetermined by default.

This is especially important for the holographic reading. A lower-order appearance may preserve structure from a richer whole, but that does not mean the whole is uniquely recoverable from the appearance. The phrase “the part contains the whole” must therefore be repaired. A part or local view may preserve selected structure from a whole under a projection rule. It may also lose structure. It may distort structure. It may be compatible with several possible wholes. It may require source return before any strong claim is made.

This repair does not destroy the holographic intuition. It makes the intuition usable.

1.8 The task of a geometry-first foundation

The under-geometrised condition cannot be solved by adding more terminology. It is solved by changing the first move.

Instead of asking first, “What equation applies?” we ask, “What is being extended, bounded, projected, transformed, or rendered?”

Instead of asking first, “What does this output say?” we ask, “What source, context, and projection produced this output, and what would source return require?”

Instead of asking first, “Can this metaphor explain the domain?” we ask, “What structure is preserved by the metaphor, what structure is lost, and where must the bridge stop?”

Instead of asking first, “Is this true?” we ask, “What kind of claim is being made, for what scope, under what evidence, with what admissible use?”

The aim is not to slow thought for its own sake. The aim is to prevent false speed. A reasoning process that moves quickly from a projection to an identity, from a measurement to a whole state, from a metaphor to an ontology, or from a fluent sentence to proof has not become efficient. It has merely hidden its losses.

Extensional-Projective Order Geometry proposes that serious reasoning becomes more reliable when the hidden geometry of representation is brought back into view.

This is the starting point of the work. The next chapter turns from the problem to the first constructive answer: the orders of extension.

Chapter 2 — Orders of Extension

2.1 Why order comes before dimension

The word dimension is useful, but it is also dangerous.

In ordinary mathematical and scientific use, dimension often suggests coordinate axes, measurable degrees of freedom, vector spaces, physical extent, or the number of independent parameters required to describe a system. Those meanings are legitimate within their own contexts. But they are not the starting point of Extensional-Projective Order Geometry.

This work therefore uses the word order more strongly than the word dimension.

An order of extension is not first a coordinate axis. It is a way in which a thing may be treated as extended, bounded, transformed, projected, sliced, or interpreted. It asks what kind of geometric accountability is active before a coordinate system, equation, representation, or measurement is imposed.

A line is not merely a one-dimensional coordinate object. It is an extension from locality into path, interval, direction, and endpoint boundary. A surface is not merely a two-coordinate set. It is an extension into spread, contour, inside/outside distinction, and field-like exposure. A body is not merely a three-coordinate object. It is an extension into form, depth, interior, exterior, and surface boundary. A history is not merely a parameterised curve. It is a duration-extension whose local states may be sliced and rendered.

The word order also prevents another mistake. It stops D0–D7 from being read as a ladder of metaphysical planes. The orders in this book are not asserted to be literal layers of reality. They are not spiritual densities, hidden substances, occult dimensions, or physical claims. They are modelling categories.

This distinction matters. A modelling order can be powerful without being ontologically inflated. It may help us reason about geometry, proof, computation, measurement, cognition, software, narrative, or physics-facing interpretation without claiming that every use reveals the final structure of the universe.

In this chapter, D0–D7 are introduced as a working grammar. Each order can be read in two ways.

The first reading is extensional. It asks how an object is built upward: what new kind of extension becomes available, what boundary appears, what incidence relations become meaningful, what transformations may act, and what invariants may be preserved.

The second reading is projective. It asks how an object may appear downward: how a lower-order mark, line, surface, form, history, possibility field, law, or context may be a view, slice, trace, rendering, or record of some richer source.

The two readings are not interchangeable. A D0 point may be an extensional primitive, but it may also be a projected trace. A D2 diagram may be a constructed surface, but it may also be a projection of a D3 form, a D4 process, a D6 transformation, or a D7 context. A D4 history may be a built duration-extension, but it may also be one selected path from a D5 variation field.

The aim of this chapter is not to make the reader memorise seven labels. The aim is to train a habit: when a thing appears, ask what order of extension is active, what lower-order accountability supports it, what higher-order source it may project from, and what must not be inferred from it.

2.2 D0 — locality, mark, and projected trace

D0 is locality without extension.

It is the minimal distinction: here rather than not-here, this rather than that, P rather than Q. It is not a tiny ball, not a hidden particle, not a metaphysical atom. It is a mark of distinction before extension has begun.

Extensional reading:

D0 gives us locality. It allows an object to be marked, identified, separated, registered, selected, or named as a local distinction. A point in geometry, a click in an interface, a measurement registration, a vertex mark, a cursor position, a selected cell in a table, or a logged event ID may all be treated as D0-like for a given modelling purpose.

D0 does not yet contain length, area, volume, duration, variation, law, or context as extension. It is the seed of distinction, not the structure built from it.

This makes D0 powerful and misleading at the same time. Because it is simple, it is easy to overread. A point on a diagram may stand for a city, a person, a probability state, a software node, a star, a data value, or a selected outcome. The mark itself does not contain all those structures. The context assigns the role.

Projective reading:

A D0 mark may also be a projected trace of a richer source. An endpoint may be a trace of a line. A vertex may be a trace of a polygonal boundary. A contact point may be a trace of a body interaction. A sensor reading may be a trace of a system state. A measurement outcome may be a trace of a measurement process. A selected answer may be a trace of a larger model state or reasoning process.

This is why D0 must be handled carefully. The fact that something appears as a mark does not prove that the source is simple. A simple output may have a complex source. A single number may compress a long calculation. A single verdict may compress a legal process. A single pixel may belong to a rendered image. A single data point may result from an entire measurement chain.

The D0 repair is therefore:

Do not confuse local registration with source simplicity.

When a point-like mark appears, the responsible question is: is this a primitive mark for the current model, or is it a projected trace of a higher-order source? If it is a trace, what source is known, what source is only hypothesised, and what reconstruction limits remain?

Example:

A point on a sales chart may be treated as D0 within the chart. But if the entity of concern is the business, that point is a projected trace of transactions, dates, accounting rules, product categories, returns, currency conversions, and reporting choices. The point is not the business. It is not even the whole sales process. It is a local mark under a reporting context.

2.3 D1 — line, path, interval, and projected passage

D1 is first-order extension.

It appears when locality is extended into path, interval, direction, sequence, or between-ness. A line segment is the simplest image: two endpoint localities bound a first extension. But D1 should not be restricted to Euclidean drawing. It also appears wherever a relation is treated as path-like, ordered, directional, sequential, or interval-bound.

Extensional reading:

D1 introduces passage from one locality to another. It makes endpoint boundary meaningful. It allows direction, orientation, continuity, adjacency, ordering, and distance-like comparison to appear. A path between two points, a dependency chain, a proof step sequence, a timeline segment, a transition from one software state to another, or a line of argument may be modelled with D1 features.

The first boundary of D1 is endpoint boundary. A line segment is not just “line-ness”; it is a bounded extension. It begins somewhere and ends somewhere for the present use. If those endpoints are not declared, the line-like object remains vague.

D1 also introduces a temptation. Once something appears as a line, we may assume that the relevant structure is linear. But not every path-like rendering means the source is one-dimensional. A line may be a route through a surface, a boundary of a plane, a trace of a motion, a selected proof path through a much larger proof space, or a simplified display of a complex relation.

Projective reading:

A D1 object may be a projected interval from a higher-order structure. A trajectory line may be the trace of a D4 duration-extension. A dependency arrow may be a projection of a more complex transformation relation. A line in a diagram may be the boundary of a D2 surface. A path through a proof may be one selected route through a D6 rule-space. A narrative timeline may be a D1 rendering of a D4 world-form.

The D1 repair is therefore:

Do not confuse a path with the whole path-space.

A line may be admissible for navigation, explanation, or local transformation. But if it is used to infer the source, the model must say what structure the line preserves and what it loses.

Example:

A project roadmap is often drawn as a line: discovery, design, build, launch, review. This D1 rendering can be useful. It preserves sequence and directional intention. It loses parallel work, feedback loops, uncertainty, resource constraints, hidden dependencies, and possible branch points. If the roadmap is treated as the whole project, the projection has become an overread.

2.4 D2 — surface, field, map, and projected face

D2 is second-order extension.

It appears when extension spreads into surface, field, area, map, plane, face, or interface. D2 is the order at which inside/outside distinction becomes visible in a new way. Contour, region, adjacency, coverage, exposure, overlap, and field-like variation become natural.

Extensional reading:

D2 is not merely “two coordinates”. It is spread. A surface patch, map, diagram, page, screen, table, chart, matrix, plane, or interface may all be treated as D2-like when their main function is to expose structure across a field.

D2 introduces boundary as contour or line-complex. A surface is not intelligible without some boundary, even if the boundary is open, implicit, changing, or context-dependent. A page has margins. A map has a frame. A diagram has a visible region and a hidden outside. A dashboard has tiles, filters, date windows, and metric definitions. A screen interface has affordances and exclusions.

D2 is one of the most common orders of representation. Humans often reason through D2 surfaces: pages, whiteboards, charts, diagrams, maps, tables, screens. This makes D2 extraordinarily useful and extraordinarily easy to overtrust.

A surface can make structure visible, but it can also flatten it.

Projective reading:

A D2 surface may be a projected face of a D3 body, a chart of a D4 process, a state-space view, a diagrammatic rendering of a proof path, an interface over a software system, a dashboard over business activity, a map over territory, or a measurement plane selected by context.

The shadow of a cube on a wall is D2. The wall projection may preserve outline, orientation, or relative position. It loses depth, hidden faces, interior, material, and sometimes scale. A business dashboard is also D2. It preserves selected indicators. It loses transaction-level detail, causal explanation, uncertainty, unmeasured work, and context of interpretation unless those are added back.

The D2 repair is therefore:

Do not confuse surface visibility with source completeness.

A D2 view is powerful because it lets us see. It is dangerous because it lets us forget what cannot be seen there.

Example:

A proof diagram may show a relation that a proof text later formalises. The diagram may preserve shape, dependency, symmetry, or intuition. It may lose formal admissibility, hidden assumptions, edge cases, and the exact transformation rules required by a proof system. The diagram is not invalid because it is a projection. But it is not enough, by itself, for proof reliance.

2.5 D3 — form, body, volume, and local object

D3 is third-order extension.

It appears when surface is extended into form, body, volume, depth, interior, exterior, and spatial objecthood. D3 is the familiar order of ordinary physical objects, but in E7G-T it is not limited to physical matter. A rendered 3D object, a body-like model, a volume of design space, or a structured object with inside/outside relations may be treated through D3 features when appropriate.

Extensional reading:

D3 introduces body-form. A D3 object has depth, not merely surface spread. It has an interior that may differ from its exterior. It has a surface-complex boundary rather than only a line boundary. It can contain, enclose, occlude, reveal, hide, deform, rotate, and be viewed from different angles.

D3 is the order at which the difference between appearance and hidden structure becomes especially intuitive. A body can show one face while hiding another. It can preserve identity across viewpoint changes. It can have internal structure that no surface view displays. It can cast different shadows under different projection contexts.

D3 therefore trains a central E7G-T habit: the visible face is not the whole form.

Projective reading:

A D3 body may itself be a local slice or rendering of something richer. A momentary physical configuration may be a slice of a D4 duration-extension. A rendered object in a simulation may be a projection from data, code, and transformation rules. A visible organism may be a present slice of development, metabolism, history, and environment. A product prototype may be one local embodiment of a D5 design variation field and a D6 production method.

The D3 repair is therefore:

Do not confuse present form with full history, law, or possibility.

A body-form may be real and still incomplete as an account. It may need a D4 history to explain how it came to be, a D5 variation field to show how it could differ, a D6 law-space to explain what transformations are admissible, and a D7 context to explain how it is being viewed, measured, named, or used.

Example:

A physical product sample is D3 in the ordinary sense: it has material, form, surface, weight, and use. But as a business object, the sample is also a projection of design choices, supplier constraints, production methods, cost structure, user needs, quality standards, logistics, and market positioning. Treating the sample as “the product” may be useful in a narrow inspection context. It is not enough for manufacturing, pricing, certification, or market strategy.

2.6 D4 — duration, history, world-form, and slice

D4 is duration-extension.

It appears when a D3 form is treated through persistence, change, trajectory, sequence of states, development, history, or world-form. D4 is not merely “time as a line”. It is the geometric treatment of a form extended through duration.

Extensional reading:

D4 introduces history as object. A moving body is not only a D3 body at many moments. It may be treated as a D4 world-form whose D3 states are slices. A proof being constructed may be treated as a D4 process, even if the completed proof object is later treated under timeless completion. A software execution may be treated as a D4 occurrence, even if the code is a D6-like transformation rule rendered in text.

D4 makes slicing central. A local state is not automatically the whole process. A momentary body, a snapshot, a log entry, a single frame of video, a one-day metric, or a current emotional state may all be local slices of a longer extension.

The D4 question is: what persists across slices, what changes, and what selects the slice?

This order also helps repair a common confusion about time. E7G-T does not need to declare what time ultimately is in physical reality in order to model duration. It can say that, in this context, an object is being treated as extended through local-time, shared-time, branching-time, cyclic-time, meta-time, or timeless-completion. The temporal regime belongs to the modelling record.

Projective reading:

A D4 history may be a projected path from a richer source. One experienced sequence may be one path through a D5 field of possible continuations. One simulation run may be one trace generated by a D6 rule. One biography may be a D4 rendering of a person’s life under narrative and documentary context. One experimental time-series may be a projected record of a system under measurement constraints.

The D4 repair is therefore:

Do not confuse a slice with the whole history, and do not confuse one history with all possible histories.

A current state may need source return to earlier states, boundary conditions, transformations, and context. A single trajectory may show what happened, but not everything that could have happened. A history may preserve sequence while losing unrealised branches, hidden causes, and measurement conditions.

Example:

A time-series chart of revenue is a D2 rendering of D4 business activity. It may preserve ordered values over time. It may lose causes, product mix, advertising changes, seasonality, operational constraints, customer behaviour, and accounting choices. If used for decision-making, the chart must return to source records and explanatory structures.

2.7 D5 — variation, possibility, branch-space, and deformation

D5 is variation-extension.

It appears when we do not merely ask what is or what happened, but what could vary, deform, branch, continue, or be otherwise under declared constraints. D5 is the order of possibility fields, design variants, modal neighbourhoods, alternative histories, proof continuations, reachable states, and admissible deformations.

Extensional reading:

D5 introduces the geometry of “could be otherwise”. A product design has possible variants. A proof has possible continuations. A software system has reachable states. A story has possible branches. A physical system, under a declared formal or empirical bridge, may have possible states or outcomes. A business strategy has scenarios. A mathematical object may have a deformation space.

D5 is not fantasy by default. It is disciplined possibility. A D5 field is meaningful only when its constraints are declared. What counts as an admissible variant? What transformation rules generate the variants? What boundaries remain fixed? Which invariants are preserved? Which possibilities are merely imaginable, and which are reachable under the model?

Without constraints, possibility becomes noise. With constraints, it becomes geometry.

Projective reading:

A D5 variation field may itself be projected from D6 transformation rules or D7 context restrictions. The field of possible chess positions is generated by rules of play. A design variant space is generated by materials, manufacturing methods, cost constraints, user needs, and design rules. A proof search space is generated by axioms, inference rules, tactics, and formal substrate. A scenario set is generated by assumptions, uncertainties, and modelling choices.

A lower-order appearance may also be a selected branch from D5. One design, one proof path, one decision, one measurement outcome, one history, or one generated answer may be one selected projection from a larger possibility field.

The D5 repair is therefore:

Do not confuse possibility with actuality, and do not confuse one selected branch with the whole field.

A possibility field does not actualise itself. A branch must be selected, produced, measured, chosen, proved, simulated, or otherwise made actual under a declared context and transformation path. Likewise, a selected branch does not exhaust the possible field unless constraints show that it does.

Example:

An AI system may produce one answer. That answer is a D2/D7 textual projection, but it comes from a larger space of possible completions under prompt, model, policy, tool, and context constraints. The answer does not reveal the whole possibility field. Nor does the existence of other possible answers make the selected answer false. It means that reliance requires source return, evidence, or formal checking according to the claim being made.

2.8 D6 — transformation, law-space, operator, and rule

D6 is transformation-extension.

It appears when the object of concern is not one form, one history, or one possibility field, but the rule-space that governs transformations. D6 includes laws, operators, proof rules, rewrite systems, dynamics, methods, state transition rules, symmetries, construction rules, and generative mechanisms.

Extensional reading:

D6 introduces lawful change as a geometric concern. It asks not only what objects exist, but what transformations are admissible, what invariants are preserved, what inputs and outputs are bounded, and what rule connects one state to another.

A D6 object is not one event. A law is not an occurrence. A method is not the work performed under it. A proof rule is not a proof text. A software function specification is not every runtime execution. A dynamic model is not the measured system. D6 is the order at which transformation patterns become the entity of concern.

This distinction is crucial because modern reasoning often confuses rule, execution, and record. Code text may render a transformation rule. Runtime execution produces transformation occurrences. Logs record selected occurrences. Documentation describes intended use. Tests sample behaviour under selected conditions. These are related, but not identical.

D6 also gives invariance a home. An invariant is not just something that “stays the same”. It stays the same under a declared transformation class. Without the transformation class, the invariant claim is incomplete.

Projective reading:

A D6 transformation regime may be inferred from observed lower-order regularities, but it is not proven by them without reconstruction discipline. Repeated trajectories may suggest a law. Repeated outputs may suggest a rule. Stable behaviour may suggest a mechanism. Symmetry in appearances may suggest an underlying group of transformations. But suggestion is not reconstruction.

Observed regularity is not automatically law.

The D6 repair is therefore:

Do not confuse regularity, rule, occurrence, and record.

If a D6 law-space is claimed, the model must say what transformations are admitted, what substrate is active, what evidence or proof supports the rule, what invariants are preserved, and where the claim stops.

Example:

A software function can be treated as a D6/D4 transformation rule rendered as code. The code text is a carrier. Runtime execution is a bounded occurrence. Unit tests are selected traces. Documentation is a projection for human use. A bug report is a record of a failure or observed mismatch. To reason responsibly, these must not be collapsed into one object.

2.9 D7 — context, projection governance, and admissible use

D7 is context-extension.

It appears when the modelling concern becomes viewpoint, measurement basis, coordinate rendering, formal language, diagrammatic representation, computational interface, proof assistant interface, publication context, admissibility policy, semantic bridge, reconstruction context, or reality-facing interpretation.

D7 is the closure layer of E7G-T because every lower-order object is encountered under some context. We never simply “see the object” without a view, language, representation, measurement, framing, or use. D7 does not mean that context creates everything arbitrarily. It means that context must be declared when a view is used.

Extensional reading:

D7 extends the model into context. It asks: who or what is viewing, measuring, rendering, formalising, publishing, interpreting, using, or bridging the object? What projection rule is active? What representation scheme is used? What use is admissible? What overread is blocked? What source return is required?

D7 includes coordinate systems, but is not reducible to coordinates. It includes observation, but is not reducible to a conscious observer. It includes interpretation, but is not license for arbitrary meaning. It includes publication, but is not the same as truth. It includes formal language, but is not the same as proof. It includes measurement context, but is not the same as empirical validation.

D7 is governance of appearance.

Projective reading:

D7 is also the condition under which projective descent is selected, limited, distorted, or authorised. A D7 context may choose a slice, render a body into a diagram, encode a proof into text, reduce a system into metrics, translate a source into another language, or project a state into a dashboard. In doing so, it preserves some structure and loses other structure.

The D7 repair is therefore:

Do not treat context as invisible.

Whenever a representation is used beyond casual orientation, the context must be named. A measurement basis is not a coordinate rendering. A publication context is not an empirical protocol. A proof assistant interface is not a human diagram. A dashboard context is not the operational system. A contemplative bridge is not an empirical bridge.

Example:

A popular science explanation may render a formal theory into accessible language. Its D7 publication context may preserve intuition, broad structure, and selected examples. It may lose formal definitions, edge cases, mathematical restrictions, evidential density, and controversy. The explanation can be useful. But if the reader uses it for technical or empirical reliance, source return is required.

2.10 Order bands: formation, world, and meta-geometry

The seven orders can be easier to remember in three bands.

D0–D3 form the band of formation geometry: locality, line, surface, and form. These orders concern the emergence of mark, path, field, and body.

D4–D5 form the band of world and possibility geometry: duration and variation. These orders concern history, slice, process, branch, deformation, scenario, and possible continuation.

D6–D7 form the band of meta-geometry: transformation and context. These orders concern law, operator, rule, admissible transformation, projection, interpretation, measurement, formalisation, and use.

These bands are didactic, not ontological. They help the reader navigate the system, but they do not replace D0–D7. A real modelling situation often activates several orders at once.

For example, a proof assistant session may involve:

D0 local marks and symbols;  
D1 proof steps and paths;  
D2 visible text and interface surfaces;  
D4 construction history during proof development;  
D5 possible proof continuations;  
D6 inference rules, tactics, and kernel checking;  
D7 formal language, interface, library context, and admissible proof use.

Calling the whole situation “D6” would hide too much. The better object is an order profile.

2.11 Order profiles rather than single labels

A mature E7G-T account usually does not assign one D-label and stop. It gives an order profile.

A single label may be useful for quick orientation. A dashboard may be called D2/D7. A proof rule may be called D6. A world-line may be called D4. A local mark may be called D0. But if a claim is reused, published, formalised, bridged, or operationally relied on, a single label is usually too thin.

An order profile asks:

What is the primary order for the entity of concern?  
Which orders are active in support?  
What lower-order boundaries make it accountable?  
What higher-order sources may project into it?  
What slices are selected?  
What transformations govern it?  
What invariants are claimed?  
What context renders or interprets it?  
What temporal regime is active?  
What bridge mode, if any, is being used?  
What overread is blocked?

Consider again the dashboard. Its visible surface is D2. Its publication and reporting context are D7. Its time windows belong to temporal regime and D4 records. Its metrics may be D0 or D1 values rendered into tiles and trends. Its source system may include transactions, workflows, customers, stock, staff, and accounting rules. Its decision use may require evidence, assurance, and source return.

The order profile prevents the dashboard from being mistaken for the business.

The same discipline applies to algebra. An equation may be a D1/D2 symbolic rendering, a D6 transformation calculus, a D7 formal-language object, or the EntityOfConcern itself under a formal substrate. The question is not “what D is the equation?” in isolation. The question is: what role is the equation playing in this context?

2.12 The order grammar as a repair discipline

The seven orders are not meant to decorate prose. They are meant to repair reasoning.

When a mark is overread, return to D0 and ask whether it is primitive or a trace.

When a path is overread, return to D1 and ask what path-space, surface, law, or context it may be projecting from.

When a surface is overread, return to D2 and ask what depth, history, uncertainty, source, or context it hides.

When a body is overread, return to D3 and ask what duration, variation, transformation, and view are missing.

When a history is overread, return to D4 and ask what slice, branch, law, and temporal regime are active.

When a possibility field is overread, return to D5 and ask what constraints make the possibilities admissible and what selection process actualises or records one branch.

When a law is overread, return to D6 and ask what evidence, proof, substrate, transformation class, and invariant support the rule.

When a context is hidden, return to D7 and ask what view, rendering, measurement, bridge, publication, or admissible-use boundary governs the appearance.

This is the practical value of the order grammar. It does not tell us everything. It tells us where to look next.

Chapter 1 named the under-geometrised condition: the habit of beginning with representation as if representation had no prior geometry. Chapter 2 gives the first grammar for repair. Chapter 3 now turns to the central movement of the system: extension and projection, and why the two must never be silently identified.

Chapter 3 — Extension and Projection

3.1 The central duality

The previous chapter introduced D0–D7 as orders of extension. This chapter introduces the central movement of the framework: extension and projection.

These two words are easy to understand in ordinary examples. A line may be extended from two points. A surface may be extended from a line. A body may cast a shadow onto a wall. A map may project a territory onto a page. A proof may be written as a sequence of lines. A software system may be represented by a diagram. A business may be shown through a dashboard.

But the ordinary familiarity of these examples hides a serious problem. We often treat extension, projection, identity, and reconstruction as if they were the same act.

They are not.

Extension is the constructive or accountability direction. It asks how a thing is built upward into richer order: what locality, boundary, incidence, continuity, transformation, invariant, and context make the object intelligible.

Projection is the rendering or appearance direction. It asks how a richer source is made available as a lower-order view: what is shown, what is hidden, what is preserved, what is lost, and what use the view may support.

Reconstruction is a third direction. It asks whether a lower-order view can support a candidate account of its higher-order source. Reconstruction is not projection in reverse. A projection may be many-to-one. Many different sources may produce the same view.

This gives the first discipline of the chapter:

Extension builds. Projection renders. Reconstruction hypothesises.

If these three are confused, almost every later mistake becomes easier. A diagram becomes the system. An equation becomes the object. A measurement becomes the full state. A proof text becomes the proof object. A metaphor becomes ontology. A local slice becomes the whole. A compatible source becomes a proven source.

The purpose of this chapter is to slow down that collapse.

3.2 Extension as constructive accountability

To extend is not merely to add more size.

In E7G-T, extension means that a thing receives a richer order of geometric accountability. Something new becomes possible: length, spread, form, duration, variation, law, or context. But something new is also required: boundary, incidence, transformation, invariant, slice relation, projection rule, or admissible-use condition.

A point-pair may support a line segment only when the relation between the points is declared. A line-complex may support a surface only when the surface relation and boundary are meaningful. A surface-complex may support a body only when interior, exterior, and form-boundary are admitted. A body may be extended through duration only when persistence, change, and slice relation are declared. A history may be extended into a variation field only when admissible alternatives are defined. A variation field may be extended into transformation-space only when the rules of possible change are declared. A transformation-space may be extended into context only when the conditions of rendering, interpretation, measurement, formalisation, or use are named.

This is why extension is accountability-oriented.

A higher order is not a prestige label. Calling something D5 or D6 does not make it deeper. It increases its obligations. A D5 possibility field must say what makes a possibility admissible. A D6 transformation law must say what transformations it governs and what invariant it preserves. A D7 context must say what it selects, renders, permits, hides, or blocks.

The practical error is to use higher-order language without paying the higher-order cost.

For example, suppose someone says: “This business dashboard shows the health of the company.” The statement implicitly extends a visible surface into a whole-system judgement. That is a major ascent. To make it accountable, the model would need the entity of concern, the metric definitions, the source records, the time window, the aggregation rules, the missing indicators, the uncertainty, the decision context, and the admissible use. Without that, the statement is not yet a higher-order account. It is a projection being inflated.

Or suppose someone says: “This equation describes the physical system.” That may be true under a declared substrate and scope. But as an E7G-T move, it must declare what has been extended into the equation: which system, which variables, which boundary, which assumptions, which transformations, which invariants, which measurement context, and which empirical or formal bridge. Otherwise the equation is only a rendering whose source has not been made accountable.

Extension therefore asks a sober question:

What must be added to make the object accountable at the claimed order?

This question protects the framework from a common pseudo-depth. It is not enough to say that something “belongs to a higher dimension” or “comes from a higher order”. The higher-order claim must show the boundary, transformation, invariant, projection, reconstruction, and context obligations that come with that order.

3.3 Projection as controlled loss

Projection is not simply “showing”. It is controlled loss.

A projection produces a lower-order view from a richer source under a context. A shadow projects a body into a surface. A map projects a territory into a navigational field. A summary projects a source text into a shorter account. A coordinate tuple projects a geometric object into a formal representation. A dashboard projects selected organisational activity into visible indicators. A proof text projects a proof path into a readable sequence. An AI answer projects a model response event into language.

Every projection preserves something. Otherwise it would be useless.

Every projection loses something. Otherwise it would be identity.

The discipline of projection is to make both facts visible.

The preserved structure may be outline, order, measure, relation, sequence, dependency, invariant, selected state, selected claim, selected evidence, or practical orientation. The lost structure may be depth, interior, history, uncertainty, alternative views, construction path, rival explanations, measurement conditions, formal substrate, or source density.

Projection becomes dangerous when its losses are hidden.

A map may preserve road connectivity while losing elevation, road condition, legal restrictions, local danger, and temporary closures. A graph may preserve selected relations while losing the meaning of relation types. A proof outline may preserve argument direction while losing formal admissibility. A translation may preserve legal force while losing rhythm, ambiguity, or rhetorical texture. A product photo may preserve appearance while losing material feel, durability, manufacturing variability, and user experience.

A projection is therefore not judged by asking whether it is simply “true” or “false”. It is judged by asking whether it preserves the right structure for the intended use and whether its losses are declared.

This gives the second discipline of the chapter:

No projection without preservation and loss.

A projection may be excellent for one use and dangerous for another. A tube map may be excellent for route planning and poor for measuring actual distance. A company profit figure may be useful for financial reporting and poor for judging organisational resilience. A mathematical diagram may be excellent for intuition and insufficient for formal proof. A single observation may be useful as a cue and insufficient as evidence for a law.

Projection is not a defect. Projection is how usable views are made. The defect is treating projection as identity.

3.4 Projection is not identity

The simplest rule is also the easiest to forget:

A projection of X is not X.

A shadow of a body is not the body. A map of a city is not the city. A scan of a document is not the legal source unless the legal context admits it as such. A coordinate rendering of a geometric object is not automatically the object. A dashboard is not the business. A proof text is not automatically the proof object. A model output is not the model. A measurement is not the whole state of what is measured.

This rule does not deny that projections can have authority. In some contexts, a projection may become the legally accepted record, the operational source, the formal object, or the entity of concern. But that status must be declared. It does not arise merely because a representation is visible.

Consider a coordinate equation for a circle. If the entity of concern is the equation as an algebraic object, then the equation may be primary. But if the entity of concern is the circle as geometric extension, the equation is a rendering under a coordinate context. It preserves radial relation and boundary condition. It loses construction history, non-coordinate intuition, drawing instance, and alternative renderings. The same written expression changes status depending on the entity of concern.

Consider a proof assistant script. If the entity of concern is the script as text, then its syntax, dependencies, imports, and tactic sequence matter. If the entity of concern is the proof object accepted by the kernel, the script may be a path that elaborates into a checked object. If the entity of concern is the theorem as a mathematical claim, the proof object belongs to a larger formal substrate. Treating all three as the same thing hides the structure that makes proof reliable.

Consider a dashboard. If the entity of concern is the dashboard interface, then layout, tiles, labels, filters, and data freshness matter. If the entity of concern is the business, the dashboard is a lower-order view. If the entity of concern is a management decision, the dashboard is only one evidence input. Each shift changes the admissible move.

Projection is not identity because representation is selective. It is not identity because the source may contain more structure than the target. It is not identity because the target may introduce distortions that are not present in the source. It is not identity because the target may be reused in contexts for which the projection was never designed.

The practical repair is:

Name the source. Name the view. Name the projection. Name the use.

Without these four, the projection remains underdeclared.

3.5 Projection is not reconstruction

The next rule is more subtle:

Projection is not reconstruction.

Projection moves from source to view. Reconstruction moves from view to candidate source. These moves are not inverses by default.

A three-dimensional object may cast a two-dimensional shadow. But many different three-dimensional objects may cast a similar shadow. A system may produce a single measurement trace. But many different internal states may explain the trace. A person may write one sentence. But many different intentions, contexts, and reasoning paths may produce it. A theory may predict one observation. But rival theories may also predict the same observation.

A projection may therefore be many-to-one. Reconstruction must be treated as underdetermined unless constraints reduce the rival set.

This is one of the most important disciplines for any holographic reading. A lower-order appearance may preserve selected structure from a richer source. That does not mean the richer source is uniquely recoverable from the appearance.

The phrase “the part contains the whole” is therefore too strong if read literally. A safer formulation is:

A local view may preserve selected structure from a wider whole under a projection rule, while losing other structure and remaining compatible with more than one possible source.

This formulation does not destroy the intuition. It makes it usable.

Reconstruction requires additional work. It must declare the observed view, the candidate source order, the projection rule if known, the constraints, the invariants, the rival source hypotheses, and the status of elimination. A single source may be claimed only when the rival set has been bounded and eliminated by formal constraints, empirical evidence, or declared stipulation in fiction or simulation.

Otherwise the correct status is not “source recovered”. It is “source compatible”, “source hypothesised”, “bounded rival set”, “open rival set”, or “reconstruction blocked”.

This distinction matters in mathematics, science, software, translation, business, and AI.

A pattern in data may be compatible with a causal explanation, but not prove it. A symptom may be compatible with a diagnosis, but not prove it. A fluent answer may be compatible with understanding, but not prove it. A geometric metaphor may be compatible with a physical interpretation, but not prove it. A lower-order projection may suggest a higher-order source, but not establish it.

The practical repair is:

Compatible with source does not mean reconstructed from source.

3.6 Extension and projection in the same object

Most real modelling objects involve both extension and projection.

A book is extended as a sequence of chapters, sections, paragraphs, claims, examples, and arguments. It is also a projection of a source idea, research programme, authorial intention, and conceptual kernel into a reader-facing form.

A proof assistant session is extended through marks, syntax, tactics, states, goals, transformations, and kernel checking. It is also a projection of formal proof work into a human-visible interface.

A software product is extended through code, modules, interfaces, runtime behaviour, user flows, deployment environments, data structures, and maintenance processes. It is also projected into screenshots, documentation, dashboards, logs, tickets, architecture diagrams, and user impressions.

A business is extended through people, contracts, money, stock, supply chains, customers, processes, legal entities, obligations, and histories. It is projected into accounts, reports, KPIs, dashboards, brand images, invoices, and investor decks.

A mathematical object may be extended through construction, boundary, transformation, and invariant. It may be projected into algebraic notation, diagrams, coordinate systems, proof texts, computational representations, and teaching examples.

This is why a simple label is often insufficient. A thing may be D3 as present form, D4 as history, D5 as variation field, D6 as governed transformation, and D7 as rendered view. The same entity may have several active orders depending on the modelling purpose.

The practical question is not “which one is it really?” in the abstract. The practical question is:

Which order is primary for the current entity of concern, and which orders are active as support, projection, source, or context?

This is the role of the order profile introduced in Chapter 2\. The order profile prevents the model from flattening a multi-order situation into one convenient label.

It also prevents the opposite error: inflating a simple local use into a full higher-order claim. Sometimes a line is just a line for the present use. Sometimes a dashboard tile is only a local indicator. Sometimes a metaphor is only a teaching aid. Sometimes a OneLine is enough.

The proportionality rule remains: use the smallest modelling level that changes the next move without hiding risk.

3.7 Extensional ascent

Extensional ascent is the upward accountability path.

It begins with what is available locally and asks what must be added to make a higher-order account responsible.

A simplified sequence may look like this:

D0 locality becomes D1 path when relation, interval, direction, or endpoint-bound extension is declared.

D1 path becomes D2 surface when spread, region, contour, or field-like relation is declared.

D2 surface becomes D3 form when depth, interior, exterior, volume, or body-boundary is declared.

D3 form becomes D4 duration-extension when persistence, change, slice relation, or history is declared.

D4 history becomes D5 variation-extension when admissible alternatives, branches, deformations, or possibility fields are declared.

D5 variation becomes D6 transformation-extension when rules, operators, dynamics, methods, or law-spaces are declared.

D6 transformation becomes D7 context-extension when viewing, measuring, rendering, interpreting, formalising, publishing, bridging, or admissible use is declared.

This sequence is not a mechanical recipe. It is a responsibility check.

For example, if a model claims D4, it must be able to say what slices it contains and what persists across them. If it cannot, the D4 claim may be premature. If a model claims D5, it must be able to say what alternatives are admissible and what constraints generate or bound them. If it cannot, the D5 claim may be only imaginative language. If a model claims D6, it must be able to say what transformation class is active and what invariants are preserved. If it cannot, the D6 claim may be only a name for observed regularity.

Extensional ascent therefore prevents hidden leaps.

It asks: what lower-order structures support the higher-order claim? What boundary makes the extension intelligible? What transformations are admitted? What invariants hold? What context governs use?

In ordinary terms, extensional ascent is the discipline of not saying “there is a system” before saying what makes it a system for the present purpose. It is the discipline of not saying “there is a law” before saying what transformations the law governs. It is the discipline of not saying “there is a possibility field” before saying what counts as possible.

3.8 Projective descent

Projective descent is the downward rendering path.

It begins with a source or candidate source and asks how it becomes available as a lower-order view.

A D7 context may render a D6 transformation into a proof rule, code expression, diagram, equation, dashboard, explanation, or interface.

A D6 transformation regime may generate a D5 field of admissible variants.

A D5 variation field may project one selected D4 history or one chosen branch.

A D4 world-form may project a D3 local state or present body-slice.

A D3 body may project a D2 surface view, shadow, drawing, photograph, scan, or diagram.

A D2 surface may project a D1 contour, route, edge, trend line, or reading path.

A D1 path may project D0 endpoints, marks, events, values, or registrations.

Again, this is not a mechanical hierarchy. It is a loss check.

At each descent, the model must ask what survives and what disappears. A D3-to-D2 projection may preserve outline and lose depth. A D4-to-D3 slice may preserve present state and lose history. A D5-to-D4 branch selection may preserve one path and lose unrealised alternatives. A D6-to-D5 generation may preserve admissibility and lose the full rule account. A D7-to-D2 publication may preserve readability and lose formal detail.

Projective descent is normal. It is how things become usable. But the stronger the use, the more explicit the projection must become.

A casual sketch can remain light. A teaching diagram can remain approximate if its limits are obvious. But a diagram used as evidence, a dashboard used for management, a measurement used for diagnosis, a proof text used for formal reliance, or a metaphor used for reality-facing interpretation must expose its projection discipline.

The practical question is:

What lower-order view has been produced, from what source or candidate source, under what context, preserving what, losing what, and for what use?

3.9 Projection stacks

Many real situations involve not one projection, but a stack of projections.

A physical process is measured by an instrument. The instrument produces data. The data is cleaned. The cleaned data is aggregated. The aggregation becomes a chart. The chart appears in a report. The report is summarised in a meeting. The meeting produces a decision.

At each stage, something is preserved and something is lost.

The danger is to treat the final projection as if it still contains everything the first source contained. It does not.

A projection stack cannot silently regain lost structure.

If a measurement protocol loses contextual nuance, a later chart does not automatically restore it. If a summary loses minority details, a later executive dashboard does not contain them unless it returns to source. If a translation resolves an ambiguity, a later legal argument cannot rely on the original ambiguity unless the source is reopened. If a diagram hides assumptions, a later presentation cannot use the diagram as if the assumptions were visible.

Projection loss accumulates unless source return or reconstruction work is performed.

This rule is especially important for AI-assisted work. A user may provide a source document. An AI system may summarise it. Another system may summarise the summary. A human may then make a decision from the second summary. The final answer may be fluent and useful, but it is several projections away from the source. Without source return, it should not carry the same authority as the original.

A projection stack should therefore be handled with a simple discipline:

Name the source chain.  
Name each projection stage that matters.  
Declare preserved structure at each stage.  
Declare lost structure at each stage.  
Return to source when the use becomes technical, legal, empirical, formal, or operationally risky.

The point is not to make every ordinary act heavy. The point is to know when a lightweight projection has become too thin for the claim placed on it.

3.10 Slice and projection need not commute

A further complication appears when slicing and projection interact.

Slicing first and then projecting may not give the same result as projecting first and then slicing.

A full process may be projected into a time-series chart. A single day may then be selected from the chart. That is projection first, slice second.

Alternatively, one may select a single day of source activity and then inspect all records from that day. That is slice first, projection second.

The two views may differ.

The chart may smooth, aggregate, omit, or transform values before the day is selected. The source-day inspection may reveal details that the chart never carried. The same date in the chart and the same date in the source system are not automatically equivalent views.

The same applies to proof, software, science, and narrative.

A proof may be projected into a linear outline and then a step selected from the outline. Or a proof state may be selected during construction and then rendered into text. These are not automatically the same.

A software system may be projected into logs and then an error window selected. Or the runtime state during the error window may be inspected directly and then rendered. These are not automatically the same.

A life story may be projected into a biography and then one chapter selected. Or one period of life may be examined through letters, records, memories, and context, then written as a chapter. These are not automatically the same.

This gives another practical discipline:

Declare whether the model slices the source before projection or slices the projection after rendering.

If the use is casual, this may not matter. If the use is explanatory, evidential, formal, legal, diagnostic, or operational, it may matter greatly.

3.11 Equality, equivalence, and resemblance

Projection also forces care with sameness.

Two views may look the same without having the same source. Two diagrams may share a shape while representing different structures. Two equations may have similar form while belonging to different substrates. Two dashboard tiles may use the same colour while referring to different metrics. Two traditions may use similar metaphors while making different claims. Two scientific models may produce similar predictions while differing in mechanism.

E7G-T therefore avoids a single careless word: same.

When sameness is claimed, the model should ask: same in what respect, under what context, by what bridge, preserving what structure, and allowing what substitution?

There are several possible sameness claims:

visual resemblance;  
structural analogy;  
shared invariant;  
formal equivalence;  
coordinate transformation;  
semantic translation;  
operational substitutability;  
empirical indistinguishability within a measurement window;  
identity of the entity of concern.

These are not the same claim.

A shadow may resemble another shadow without sharing the same source. A diagram may be structurally analogous to a system without being operationally substitutable for it. A model may be empirically indistinguishable under one measurement window but diverge under another. A translation may preserve legal meaning without preserving rhetorical force. A formal analogy may preserve structure without importing ontology.

This is why bridge discipline later becomes necessary. Whenever a claim crosses contexts, the bridge must say what kind of sameness is being asserted and where it stops.

For now, the chapter only needs the repair:

Do not use resemblance as identity.

3.12 Worked example: circle, equation, drawing, and object
