A simulation step is not empirical time. A cycle is not a straight line. A possible branch is not an actual occurrence. A timeless theorem is not a historical publication event. A measurement record is not the whole duration of the measured system.

If these differences are not declared, a model can quietly move between incompatible time treatments.

E7G-T therefore uses temporal regimes.

A temporal regime is not a claim about what time ultimately is. It is a modelling declaration: this object, claim, projection, transformation, or view is being treated under a particular time-like discipline.

The regime tells us whether the object is being treated as atemporal, locally sequential, shared across observers or systems, branching, cyclic, meta-temporal, or completed beyond local sequence.

This is practical, not metaphysical.

The purpose is to prevent a common overread: taking a local slice and treating it as the whole duration, taking a possible branch and treating it as actual, taking a completed formal object and confusing it with the process of constructing it, or taking a timeless mathematical claim and treating it as a physical claim about time.

The guiding rule is:

Do not use time-like language without saying which temporal regime is active when the claim depends on it.

6.2 Temporal regime is part of object placement

In the practical notation, an object may be placed as:

X : Dn @ TRi / C

This means that X is being treated at order Dn, under temporal regime TRi, within context C.

The temporal regime is not a decoration. It affects what operations are admissible.

A D3 object under local-time may be treated as a present state. A D3 object under D4 duration-extension may be treated as one slice of a world-form. A proof text under construction may be treated as a process. The same proof after formal acceptance may be treated as a completed object inside a formal substrate. A design alternative may belong to a D5 variation field, not to actual history. A law may be treated as D6 transformation-extension, not as one occurrence in time.

Without temporal placement, the model may confuse state, process, branch, rule, and view.

Consider a software function.

As code text, it may be treated almost timelessly: a written rule or expression that can be inspected. As execution, it unfolds in runtime. As a logged event, it becomes a record of one occurrence. As a bug report, it becomes a selected projection of observed failure. As a refactor, it becomes a transformation across versions. As a formal specification, it may be treated as a D6 rule under a substrate.

The same “function” can therefore carry several temporal regimes depending on the entity of concern.

The repair is:

When a claim depends on time, place the entity with its temporal regime before applying the operation.

6.3 TR0 — no-time or atemporal treatment

TR0 is no-time or atemporal treatment.

It is used when an object is being considered without duration, sequence, history, branch, or becoming as part of the model. The object is treated as a static formal entity, mark, relation, structure, definition, or local configuration.

TR0 does not claim that time does not exist. It says that, for the present modelling purpose, time is not active in the object account.

A geometric definition may be treated under TR0. A point, line, equation, diagram, type signature, theorem statement, or algebraic expression may be treated atemporally when the question concerns its structure rather than its construction, use, or history.

For example, the equation x² \+ y² \= r² may be studied under TR0 as a formal expression. Its derivation, teaching, discovery, publication, computation, and empirical use are not part of that specific treatment.

TR0 is useful because many objects can be stabilised by ignoring duration. It allows focus.

But it is dangerous when the ignored duration matters.

A product sample treated atemporally may hide manufacturing variation, wear, storage conditions, and supply history. A dashboard screenshot treated atemporally may hide data freshness. A theorem statement treated atemporally may hide proof dependencies and publication context. A medical value treated atemporally may hide trend. A business metric treated atemporally may hide seasonality.

The TR0 repair is:

Atemporal treatment is allowed only when duration is irrelevant or explicitly excluded.

If duration later matters, upgrade the temporal regime rather than pretending the TR0 account was complete.

6.4 TR1 — local-time

TR1 is local-time.

It is used when a process, observer, system, proof construction, computation, experience, or operation unfolds in a local sequence. There is a before and after relative to that local frame.

TR1 is the ordinary regime of doing, observing, reading, writing, executing, measuring, deciding, and experiencing.

A user reads a sentence before the next sentence. A developer runs a test after changing code. A proof assistant displays a new goal after a tactic. A machine logs an event after another event. A customer places an order before shipment. A person observes a measurement outcome after performing a measurement protocol.

TR1 is important because most practical reasoning happens inside local sequence.

But local-time is not the whole time-structure of every object.

A reader experiences a proof sequentially, but the proof object may later be treated as completed. A local measurement occurs at a time, but the measured system may have a longer duration. A dashboard is viewed now, but its data may refer to past windows and delayed updates. A simulation runs step by step, but its model may describe a larger state space.

TR1 therefore requires slice discipline.

A local-time event is often a slice, trace, or occurrence. It should not automatically be treated as the whole object. A current screen state is not the whole software system. A present mood is not the whole person. A current metric is not the whole business. A current proof state is not the whole proof. A current scientific measurement is not the whole system.

The TR1 repair is:

Local sequence gives local order, not whole-state authority.

When using TR1 evidence, ask what larger duration, process, field, or context the local event belongs to.

6.5 TR2 — shared-time

TR2 is shared-time.

It is used when several objects, agents, systems, records, measurements, or processes must be coordinated under a common temporal reference.

TR2 appears in calendars, clocks, version histories, collaborative work, experimental protocols, distributed systems, legal deadlines, business reporting periods, and shared event sequences. It allows more than one local-time process to be compared, synchronised, ordered, or audited.

TR2 is not merely “time for everyone”. It is time under a coordination scheme.

A meeting time belongs to shared-time. A version-control commit timestamp belongs to shared-time. An invoice date, contract deadline, shipping date, lab collection time, and publication date all rely on shared temporal coordination. A multi-site experiment requires shared-time discipline. A distributed software system needs event ordering, clock assumptions, and sometimes causal ordering beyond clock time.

TR2 matters because many errors arise when local-time records are treated as if they already share a stable timeline.

Two logs may show events in different time zones. Two systems may have clock drift. A financial report may use invoice date while a cash report uses payment date. A medical test may be interpreted differently depending on sample time. A project plan may mix planned dates, actual dates, and reporting dates. A historical narrative may mix event time, record time, memory time, and publication time.

The repair is:

When multiple local sequences are compared, declare the shared-time scheme.

Without that scheme, “before”, “after”, “simultaneous”, “late”, “current”, and “historical” may be underdeclared.

TR2 is especially important for source return. If a dashboard value is traced back to records, the time windows must align. If a proof version is cited, the library version and date may matter. If a translation revision is reviewed, version chronology matters. If a scientific result is compared with another, protocol and time context matter.

Shared-time is therefore not only chronology. It is accountability across local sequences.

6.6 TR3 — branching-time

TR3 is branching-time.

It is used when the model concerns possible continuations, alternatives, scenarios, decision paths, proof branches, simulation branches, design options, or modal futures.

TR3 belongs naturally with D5 variation-extension. It treats the object not only as what happened, but as what could continue, diverge, or be otherwise under declared constraints.

A project may have several possible paths. A proof search may branch. A software state machine may have multiple transitions. A business strategy may have scenarios. A game position may have legal moves. A fictional narrative may contain alternate timelines. A scientific model may simulate possible outcomes under parameter variation.

Branching-time is useful because many real decisions concern not only the actual past, but possible futures.

But TR3 is dangerous because possible branches are easily treated as actual histories.

A forecast is not a future fact. A scenario is not a prediction unless given a prediction status. A possible proof continuation is not a proof. A design option is not a manufactured product. A simulated outcome is not an empirical occurrence. A risk model is not the event itself.

TR3 therefore requires admissibility constraints.

What counts as a branch? What transformation rules generate branches? Which branches are possible under the model, plausible under evidence, preferred under value, or actualised by decision? Which branches are merely imaginable but not admissible?

The repair is:

Do not confuse branch with occurrence.

A branch must be marked as possible, simulated, forecast, planned, chosen, actualised, or discarded. These are different statuses.

TR3 also helps prevent retrospective simplification. After one branch becomes actual history, it is tempting to treat it as inevitable. E7G-T keeps the D5/TR3 field visible: the actual path may be one selected or realised branch among alternatives. This does not mean all alternatives were equally real or equally likely. It means the model should not erase variation when variation matters.

6.7 TR4 — cyclic-time

TR4 is cyclic-time.

It is used when repetition, recurrence, rhythm, periodicity, iteration, feedback, seasonal pattern, ritual, review cycle, development cycle, biological cycle, business cycle, or computational loop is central to the object.

A cycle is not merely a line that returns. It has phase, recurrence condition, transformation across iterations, and possible drift.

A weekly meeting, monthly report, annual tax cycle, software sprint, biological rhythm, production batch, customer reorder cycle, learning loop, or proof refinement loop may be treated under TR4.

TR4 is useful because many systems do not behave as one-pass lines. They repeat, update, accumulate, degrade, improve, oscillate, or re-enter earlier states.

But cyclic-time is dangerous when recurrence is mistaken for sameness.

The second cycle is not automatically identical to the first. A monthly sales cycle may repeat while market conditions change. A biological cycle may repeat with variation. A software sprint may repeat but accumulate technical debt. A ritual may repeat but change meaning. A customer reorder may repeat but with altered price, stock, or preference. A proof refinement loop may revisit a goal with changed context.

The invariant question is central:

What is preserved across cycles, and what changes?

A cycle may preserve structure while changing content. It may preserve period while changing amplitude. It may preserve ritual form while changing inner meaning. It may preserve sprint cadence while changing scope. It may preserve review timing while changing evidence.

The TR4 repair is:

Do not treat recurrence as identity. Declare cycle invariant and cycle drift.

This makes TR4 especially important for practical modelling. Businesses, health patterns, learning, software development, translation revision, and proof search often operate cyclically. A straight-line model may hide the feedback structure that actually governs the process.

6.8 TR5 — super-time or meta-time

TR5 is super-time or meta-time.

It is used when the model concerns time about time: revision histories, versioning of rules, changing temporal frames, simulation control, governance of processes, meta-schedules, proof development over formal objects, model evolution, or higher-level ordering of lower-level temporal regimes.

TR5 is not mystical “higher time”. It is meta-temporal modelling.

A calendar system governs events in shared-time. A version-control history governs changes to code that later executes in local-time. A proof assistant development history governs changes to a theorem library whose accepted objects may be treated under timeless-completion. A simulation controller governs simulated time steps. A research programme evolves its own models across revisions. A legal system may change rules that govern future deadlines.

TR5 appears whenever one temporal structure is itself being managed, revised, interpreted, or transformed from another temporal position.

This is important because models often confuse object-time with model-time.

A simulation may model a century in minutes. The simulated time is not the runtime of the simulation. A book may describe years of history but be read in hours. The story time is not the reading time. A proof may concern timeless mathematical objects but be developed over weeks. The proof-development time is not the theorem’s formal status. A business forecast may refer to next year but be revised every week. Forecast target-time is not forecast revision-time.

The repair is:

Separate object-time from model-time.

TR5 is the regime that allows that separation to be named.

It also helps with governance. When a model is updated, when a proof library changes, when a dashboard definition changes, when a legal rule changes, or when a translation revision changes, the object of concern may be the temporal governance layer rather than the object originally described.

6.9 TR6 — timeless-completion

TR6 is timeless-completion.

It is used when an object is treated as complete relative to a formal, narrative, archival, or modelling context. Its construction history may exist, but the present use treats the object as a completed whole.

A proved theorem may be treated under TR6 inside its formal substrate. A completed proof object may be checked as complete. A finished book may be read as a complete text, even though it was written over time. A recorded dataset may be frozen for audit. A closed legal document may be treated as a completed instrument. A fictional world may be treated as a completed canon for interpretation. A final translation may be treated as the delivered target text.

TR6 does not mean “outside reality”. It means completed treatment.

The difference from TR0 is subtle but important. TR0 ignores time for the present purpose. TR6 treats the object as complete relative to a construction or closure process. A theorem statement may be considered under TR0 as a static statement. A theorem with proof accepted in a formal system may be treated under TR6 as completed relative to that proof context.

TR6 is powerful because it allows local sequence to be bracketed. We can use a completed proof without replaying every construction step. We can cite a final document without reopening every draft. We can use a frozen dataset without treating it as still changing.

But TR6 becomes dangerous when closure is overread.

A completed proof inside a formal substrate is not necessarily an explanation for every reader. A completed dataset is not the living system. A final report is not the events it reports. A delivered translation may be final for the client but still a projection of the source. A completed model may still be wrong, limited, or context-bound. A closed legal document may still need interpretation or enforcement.

The repair is:

Completion is relative to a closure context.

Declare what has been completed, under what substrate or context, and what remains outside the completion.

6.10 Atemporal whole and local slice

One recurring E7G-T pattern is the relation between atemporal whole and local slice.

A whole may be treated under TR0 or TR6 while a local observer, user, process, or measurement experiences slices under TR1. This pattern is useful in formal mathematics, proof, computation, narrative, simulation, and cautious philosophical interpretation.

A completed proof object may be treated as a whole under TR6, while a human reader encounters it line by line under TR1.

A finished book may be a complete text under TR6, while the reader experiences one page at a time under TR1.

A simulation model may contain a defined state space, while a run unfolds step by step.

A recorded film may be complete as a file, while the viewer experiences frames sequentially.

A database snapshot may be frozen, while a query returns selected slices.

This pattern should not be inflated into a universal claim that reality itself is necessarily a completed whole. It may be used as a modelling analogy or philosophical interpretation, but stronger claims require stronger bridge discipline.

The safe formulation is:

A source may be treated as whole under one temporal regime while a selected view appears locally under another temporal regime.

This formulation allows the model to speak about local experience without claiming that the local slice exhausts the whole. It also allows the model to speak about completed structures without denying local sequence.

The repair is:

When whole and slice use different temporal regimes, declare both.

For example:

ProofObject : D6 @ TR6 / FormalKernelContext  
ReaderExperience : D1/D2 @ TR1 / HumanReadingContext

This says that the proof object is treated as completed under a formal context, while the reader experiences a sequential textual projection. It does not confuse the two.

6.11 Time, projection, and loss

Temporal rendering is projection.

A timeline projects events into a line. A calendar projects obligations into a shared grid. A chart projects values into ordered visual form. A history projects selected events into narrative order. A log projects runtime events into records. A proof script projects construction into text. A forecast projects possible futures into scenario language.

Each temporal projection preserves something and loses something.

A timeline preserves sequence and loses depth. A calendar preserves scheduled relation and loses lived experience. A chart preserves ordered values and loses causal explanation. A narrative preserves selected meaning and loses unselected events. A log preserves recorded events and loses unlogged state. A proof script preserves a path and loses search space. A forecast preserves scenario structure and loses certainty.

Temporal projections therefore require the same discipline as spatial projections:

What source is rendered?  
What temporal regime is active?  
What selector orders the view?  
What is preserved?  
What is lost?  
What use is admissible?

This matters because time-ordered views often feel more authoritative than they are. A chart with dates may look like an explanation when it is only a sequence. A log may look like causation when it is only recorded order. A story may look inevitable when it is retrospective selection. A forecast may look like future knowledge when it is branch modelling.

The repair is:

Temporal order is not automatically causal explanation.

Before and after are relation claims, not complete source accounts.

6.12 Time and causation

Time-language often smuggles causation.

A happened before B, so A caused B.

Sometimes this is true. Often it is not established.

Temporal order may be necessary for some causal claims, but it is not sufficient. A cause claim requires an incidence relation stronger than sequence. It may need mechanism, intervention, counterfactual support, statistical evidence, formal model, experimental control, or domain-specific reasoning.

E7G-T therefore separates temporal incidence from causal incidence.

A before B is a temporal relation.

A produces B is a causal or generative relation.

A permits B is an enabling relation.

A is correlated with B is a statistical relation.

A is projected as B is a projective relation.

A is represented by B is a rendering relation.

These should not be collapsed.

Consider a dashboard. Advertising spend rises before revenue rises. This may suggest a causal relation, but the time order alone does not prove it. Seasonality, stock, pricing, organic demand, competitor behaviour, platform changes, and reporting delay may be involved.

Consider a medical value. A symptom appears after a medication change. This may be clinically relevant, but temporal order alone does not establish causation.

Consider software. An error appears after a deployment. The deployment may be causal, but logs, dependencies, runtime state, configuration, traffic, and environment must be inspected.

The repair is:

Do not upgrade sequence to cause without relation discipline.

6.13 Time and proof

Proof has several temporal regimes at once.

There is proof discovery: the human or machine process of finding a proof. This belongs to TR1 or TR4 when iterative, sometimes TR5 when managed across revisions.

There is proof construction: the sequence of steps, tactics, lemmas, or transformations by which a proof is built. This belongs to local-time during work.

There is proof object: the formal completed object accepted under a substrate. This may be treated under TR6.

There is theorem status: within a formal system, the theorem may be treated as derivable independently of the reader’s experience.

There is proof explanation: the reader-facing projection, experienced sequentially under TR1.

There is proof publication: a historical event under shared-time.

Confusing these creates errors.

A theorem may be formally proved but not yet understood by a reader. A proof may be readable but not formalised. A proof may be discovered over time but later treated as completed. A published proof may later be corrected. A proof assistant library may change, affecting formal checking. A theorem may be timeless in formal treatment but historically situated in research practice.

E7G-T helps by asking which temporal regime belongs to which entity of concern.

The repair is:

Do not confuse proof discovery, proof construction, proof object, proof explanation, and proof publication.

Each has its own temporal placement.

6.14 Time and computation

Computation also uses several temporal regimes.

Code text may be inspected under TR0-like static treatment.

Program execution unfolds under TR1.

Distributed systems require TR2 or more sophisticated shared-time and causal-ordering discipline.

Loops and retries involve TR4.

Version history and deployment governance involve TR5.

A released build may be treated as completed under TR6 relative to a release context.

A state space or possible execution tree may involve TR3.

The same software object may therefore have many temporal faces.

A bug report may refer to one local occurrence. A test suite may sample behaviour across selected cases. A monitoring dashboard may aggregate runtime slices. A version-control system may record code evolution. A specification may describe expected transformation independent of one run. A release tag may freeze a version.

Errors arise when these are collapsed.

A passing test at one time is not proof of all future behaviour. A log entry is not the whole runtime state. A release tag is not the development history. A specification is not an execution. A possible execution path is not an observed execution. A local bug occurrence may reveal a general defect, but the relation must be established.

The repair is:

Separate code-time, run-time, log-time, version-time, release-time, and possibility-time.

This is not terminology for its own sake. It prevents operational mistakes.

6.15 Time and business modelling

Business reasoning is full of temporal regime confusion.

A current balance is not a trend. A monthly profit figure is not long-term health. A forecast is not revenue. A plan is not execution. A recurring order is not guaranteed future demand. A delayed payout is not missing revenue. A historical sales pattern is not a law. A seasonal cycle is not a permanent structure.

E7G-T can help business modelling precisely because it separates regimes.

TR1 appears in daily operations: orders, shipments, decisions, meetings, messages, payments, incidents.

TR2 appears in reporting periods, accounting dates, deadlines, payroll cycles, shipping timelines, and shared schedules.

TR3 appears in forecasts, scenarios, strategic options, risk branches, market possibilities, and decision trees.

TR4 appears in seasonality, reorder cycles, production cycles, monthly reports, advertising optimisation, and review loops.

TR5 appears in governance: changing KPI definitions, versioned dashboards, strategy revisions, policy changes, and model updates.

TR6 appears in closed reports, final accounts, signed contracts, delivered translations, approved budgets, and frozen datasets.

A mature business claim should say which regime it is using.

“We are profitable” may refer to one month, a rolling period, cash basis, accrual basis, one product line, one marketplace, one legal entity, or a forecast. The sentence is underdeclared until the temporal and boundary conditions are named.

The repair is:

Business time must distinguish actual history, reporting slice, cycle, forecast branch, and closed record.

6.16 Time and narrative

Narrative makes temporal projection visible.

A story may present events in an order different from the order in which they occurred. It may use flashback, foreshadowing, parallel threads, cyclic motifs, alternate timelines, unreliable narration, or retrospective interpretation. Narrative time, event time, reading time, and authoring time are different.

This is not only relevant to literature. Human explanation often works narratively. A business case study, personal biography, scientific discovery story, historical account, spiritual testimony, legal argument, or product origin story may impose narrative order on complex events.

Narrative order can reveal meaning. It can also distort causation and inevitability.

A retrospective story may make a path look necessary after it occurred. A founder story may erase failed branches. A scientific discovery story may simplify collaboration and accident. A spiritual narrative may compress years of ambiguity into a clean turning point. A legal narrative may select events that support an argument.

E7G-T does not reject narrative. It asks narrative to declare its temporal projection.

What event sequence is being rendered?  
What reading sequence is chosen?  
What branches are omitted?  
What cycles or repetitions are emphasised?  
What interpretive closure is imposed?  
What remains outside the narrative boundary?

The repair is:

Narrative time is a projection, not the raw structure of events.

6.17 Physics-facing caution

Temporal regimes may be tempting to read as physics.

This must be resisted unless a proper bridge is declared.

E7G-T can model time-like structure in formal systems, computation, proof, narrative, business, cognition, and speculative interpretation. It can also offer cautious analogy for physics-facing discussions: local slice, measurement context, possibility field, transformation law, and completed model can be useful interpretive terms.

But this does not establish physical time, quantum time, relativity, cosmology, or any empirical theory.

A claim about physical time requires the standards of physics: mathematical formalism, connection to existing theory, measurement map, empirical predictions or constraints, evidence, and falsification or revision conditions.

Therefore, when E7G-T speaks about atemporal whole, local slice, branching possibility, timeless-completion, or projection, the default status is modelling language or formal/philosophical analogy. It becomes empirical only under an empirical-testable bridge.

The repair is:

Do not treat temporal modelling regimes as discoveries about physical time.

They may become useful bridge language, but bridge language must not be confused with established science.

6.18 The temporal regime check

A quick temporal regime check can prevent many errors.

Ask:

Is the object being treated atemporally, locally, jointly, branchingly, cyclically, meta-temporally, or as completed?

Is the claim about state, process, history, branch, cycle, rule, record, or completion?

Is the visible object a slice of a longer duration?

Is a possible branch being treated as an actual occurrence?

Is a completed object being confused with the construction process?

Is a local sequence being treated as shared-time?

Is recurrence being treated as identity?

Is temporal order being treated as causation?

Is model-time being confused with object-time?

Is a temporal metaphor being upgraded into an empirical claim?

These questions are not meant to slow every sentence. They are meant to catch hidden upgrades when time-language supports a claim.

The shorter form is:

What temporal regime is active, what slice is selected, and what overread is blocked?

6.19 From temporal regimes to holographic reading

The temporal regime chapter prepares the next major idea: the holographic reading.

The word holographic can easily become inflated. It can suggest that every part literally contains the whole, that local appearances uniquely reveal hidden sources, or that projection is automatically reversible. Earlier chapters have already blocked those readings. Projection is not identity. Reconstruction is not projection reversed. A slice is not the whole. An invariant is not resemblance. A branch is not an occurrence. A completed model is not empirical reality.

Temporal regimes add another correction.

A local slice may preserve selected structure from a wider source under one regime while being experienced sequentially under another. A completed formal object may be read locally. A possibility field may generate a realised branch. A cyclic pattern may preserve a rhythm without preserving identity. A narrative may project event structure into reading order.

These are the disciplined forms in which holographic intuition may become useful.

Chapter 7 therefore turns to Holographic Reading, Properly Disciplined. Its task is to preserve the insight that lower-order appearances may carry selected structure from richer sources, while refusing the overclaim that the part simply contains the whole without loss.

Chapter 7 — Holographic Reading, Properly Disciplined

7.1 The useful intuition and the dangerous slogan

The word holographic is attractive because it names an intuition many people recognise: a smaller view may carry structure from a larger source.

A shadow carries outline from a body. A map carries navigational structure from a territory. A DNA sample carries information related to an organism. A local measurement may carry selected structure from a system. A proof line may carry a step from a larger proof. A diagram may carry structural relations from a formal object. A dashboard tile may carry a selected trace from business activity. A sentence may carry a compressed projection of a larger argument.

This intuition is useful.

The dangerous slogan is: the part contains the whole.

Read literally, this is too strong. It hides loss. It hides distortion. It hides selection. It hides context. It hides non-uniqueness of reconstruction. It hides the fact that many sources can produce similar local views.

E7G-T therefore does not use holographic reading as a mystical shortcut. It uses it as a disciplined projection claim.

The safer formulation is:

A lower-order view may preserve selected structure from a richer source under a projection context, while losing other structure and remaining subject to reconstruction limits.

This sentence is longer than the slogan because it pays the cost of the slogan.

It says lower-order view, not whole identity.

It says may preserve, not must contain.

It says selected structure, not total structure.

It says richer source, not proven ultimate reality.

It says projection context, not context-free revelation.

It says losing other structure, not perfect containment.

It says reconstruction limits, not automatic recovery of the whole.

This is the governing discipline of holographic reading in E7G-T.

7.2 Holographic reading is a projection discipline

A holographic reading begins when a lower-order appearance is treated as a view of a richer source.

The appearance may be a point, line, surface, body, history, branch, law, context, equation, diagram, measurement, summary, proof text, model output, dashboard, or narrative. The source may be a geometric object, process, possibility field, transformation rule, formal system, empirical system, software system, text, business, cognition, or fictional world.

But the relation is not automatically valid merely because the appearance is suggestive.

A disciplined holographic reading must declare at least six things:

source or candidate source;  
view or projected appearance;  
projection context;  
preserved structure;  
lost structure;  
reconstruction status.

If any of these are missing, the reading may still be an intuition, but it is not yet a mature modelling claim.

For example, a dashboard tile may be read holographically as a local view of business activity. The source is the business process and records. The view is the tile. The projection context is the reporting system with definitions, filters, and time windows. The preserved structure may be a selected value, trend, or threshold. The lost structure may include transactions, causes, uncertainty, operational constraints, and human context. Reconstruction status may be weak unless source records are available.

This is a useful holographic reading.

It does not claim that the tile contains the whole business.

Likewise, a proof diagram may be read as a lower-order view of a proof object or proof strategy. It may preserve incidence, dependency, symmetry, or flow. It may lose formal details, assumptions, edge cases, and kernel-checkable transformations. It can guide understanding without becoming proof by itself.

The practical rule is:

Holographic reading requires preservation and loss accounting.

Without loss accounting, the reading becomes inflation.

7.3 The lower-order view

The lower-order view is what appears.

It may be visible, symbolic, textual, numerical, experiential, computational, or conceptual. It is lower-order not because it is inferior, but because it is more local, compressed, selected, or rendered than the source being considered.

A point on a chart is lower-order relative to the business process it summarises. A sentence is lower-order relative to the whole argument it belongs to. A proof line is lower-order relative to the proof object or strategy. A measurement value is lower-order relative to the measured system. A coordinate equation may be lower-order relative to a geometric construction. A local state may be lower-order relative to a duration-extension. A realised branch may be lower-order relative to a variation field.

The first error is to despise the lower-order view.

A local mark may matter. A measurement may be decisive. A counterexample may refute a universal claim. A proof line may carry a valid inference. A dashboard tile may reveal a serious issue. A symptom may require urgent attention. A single word in a legal translation may change meaning.

Lower-order does not mean unimportant.

The second error is to overread the lower-order view.

A mark is not necessarily the source. A measurement is not the whole state. A counterexample refutes only when the universal claim and context are properly declared. A dashboard tile is not the business. A symptom is not the diagnosis. A word is not the whole text.

Holographic reading therefore requires respect in both directions.

Respect the view because it may preserve structure.

Limit the view because it loses structure.

7.4 The richer source

The richer source is the structure from which the view is taken, rendered, sliced, measured, summarised, or interpreted.

Sometimes the source is known. A map has a known territory. A translation has a source text. A dashboard has source records. A proof assistant script has a formal context. A measurement has an instrument and protocol. A screenshot has an interface state.

Sometimes the source is only a candidate. A pattern in data may suggest a causal mechanism. A shadow may suggest a body. A symptom may suggest a diagnosis. A local mathematical resemblance may suggest a formal structure. A philosophical analogy may suggest a deeper interpretive relation.

The difference matters.

Known source supports stronger projection claims. Candidate source supports weaker reconstruction claims.

A holographic reading becomes unsafe when a candidate source is treated as known merely because the view resembles it.

For example, a spiral drawing may resemble a galaxy, shell, storm, and mathematical curve. The resemblance does not prove which source is active. A business downturn may resemble a seasonal dip, advertising failure, stock issue, or reporting change. The resemblance does not establish cause. A mystical metaphor may resemble an E7G-T pattern. The resemblance does not establish ontology.

The practical rule is:

Name whether the source is known, candidate, hypothesised, fictional, formal, empirical, or unknown.

This one declaration prevents many overclaims.

7.5 Preservation

Preservation is the reason holographic reading is useful.

If nothing were preserved, the lower-order view would not help. A map helps because it preserves selected navigational relations. A diagram helps because it preserves selected structure. A summary helps because it preserves central claims. A proof sketch helps because it preserves strategy. A measurement helps because it preserves a registered property. A dashboard helps because it preserves selected metrics. A metaphor helps because it preserves a pattern of relation.

But preservation is not total.

The model must say what kind of structure is preserved.

Possible preservation types include:

shape;  
order;  
incidence;  
boundary;  
ratio;  
trend;  
measure;  
state;  
relation;  
dependency;  
symmetry;  
constraint;  
meaning;  
legal force;  
proof strategy;  
formal derivability;  
operational behaviour;  
empirical signal;  
reader orientation.

These are not interchangeable.

A diagram may preserve incidence but not metric scale. A translation may preserve legal force but not rhythm. A software refactor may preserve external behaviour but not performance. A summary may preserve conclusion but not evidence density. A proof explanation may preserve intuition but not formal admissibility. A physical model may preserve a relation under ideal conditions but not under real-world noise.

The practical rule is:

Do not say “the structure is preserved” without naming the preserved structure.

Preservation must be typed.

7.6 Loss

Loss is the price of projection.

Every lower-order view leaves something out. It may omit depth, interior, history, variation, context, evidence, uncertainty, mechanism, source density, alternative views, formal substrate, measurement conditions, or admissible-use boundaries.

Loss is not failure by itself. Loss makes usability possible.

A map loses much of the territory so that navigation becomes easier. A summary loses detail so that the main claim becomes visible. A graph loses individual records so that trend becomes visible. A diagram loses formal proof detail so that structure becomes visible. A dashboard loses transaction density so that selected indicators become visible.

The problem is unacknowledged loss.

Unacknowledged loss creates false authority. The user forgets that the view is thinner than the source. The lower-order appearance begins to masquerade as the whole.

E7G-T therefore treats loss accounting as mandatory for strong holographic reading.

A loss account should identify what matters for the use. It does not need to list everything omitted. It needs to list the losses that could affect the claim, decision, proof, interpretation, bridge, or reliance.

For example, if a dashboard is used for quick orientation, it may be enough to know that it loses detail. If it is used to make a major operational decision, the loss account must be more specific: data freshness, exclusions, aggregation rules, causation, uncertainty, reporting delay, and missing indicators.

The practical rule is:

A projection is not mature until its relevant losses are named.

7.7 Distortion

Loss is not the only issue. Projection may also distort.

A map may distort distance or area. A graph may distort perception through scale. A summary may distort emphasis. A translation may distort ambiguity. A dashboard may distort urgency through colour thresholds. A proof diagram may distort dependency. A metaphor may distort by importing unwanted associations. A model output may distort confidence through fluency.

Distortion differs from loss.

Loss leaves structure out.

Distortion changes how preserved structure appears.

A diagram may preserve connection while distorting relative importance. A chart may preserve values while distorting trend perception through axis choice. A narrative may preserve events while distorting causation by order and emphasis. A translation may preserve basic meaning while distorting tone. A model may preserve correlation while distorting mechanism.

Holographic reading must therefore ask not only what is missing, but how the view changes what remains.

The practical rule is:

Declare distortion risk when the form of the view may mislead the use.

This is especially important in visual, rhetorical, and AI-generated outputs. Fluency, symmetry, elegance, and visual clarity can create a feeling of truth that exceeds the projection’s actual support.

7.8 Reconstruction limits

A holographic view may suggest a source. It does not automatically reconstruct it.

This has already been stated in earlier chapters, but it is central here.

Many sources can share a view. Many bodies can cast similar shadows. Many causes can produce similar symptoms. Many mechanisms can fit a pattern. Many arguments can produce the same conclusion. Many models can generate similar predictions. Many histories can explain one record. Many prompts can produce one answer.

A holographic reading must therefore declare reconstruction status.

Useful statuses include:

source known;  
source directly traceable;  
source partially traceable;  
source compatible;  
source hypothesised;  
rival sources open;  
rival sources bounded;  
source reconstructed under assumptions;  
reconstruction blocked;  
source irrelevant for current use.

These statuses protect the reader from false certainty.

For example, a translation has a known source text, so source return is possible. A dashboard tile may have traceable source records, if the system supports traceability. A measurement may have a known protocol, but the full state may remain unreconstructed. A symptom may be compatible with several diagnoses. A philosophical resemblance may be a formal analogy at most. A quantum interpretation may be bridge language unless empirically testable predictions are declared.

The practical rule is:

Do not call a source reconstructed when it is only compatible.

Compatibility is useful. It is not recovery.

7.9 The disciplined holographic statement

A mature holographic statement has a recognisable form.

It does not say:

The part contains the whole.

It says something closer to:

Y is a lower-order view of source X under context C. It preserves P, loses L, may distort R, and supports use U. Reconstruction status is S.

In notation:

X : Dn @ TRi / C  \--π\_C^{n→k}--\>  Y : Dk @ TRj / C'  \[preserves: P; loses: L; distortion risk: R; use: U; reconstruction: S\]

This is not meant to replace prose. It is a compact modelling record.

For example:

BusinessActivity : D4/D6 @ TR2 / ReportingSystem  \--π--\>  DashboardTile : D0/D2 @ TR1 / ManagerView  \[preserves: selected metric value; loses: transaction detail, causation, uncertainty; distortion risk: colour threshold overconfidence; use: orientation; reconstruction: source-traceable if records available\]

The line is longer than the dashboard number because it describes what the number hides.

The point is not to make every conversation formal. The point is to have a discipline available when the claim matters.

7.10 Holographic reading and the body-shadow example

The body-shadow example remains useful because it is simple.

A D3 body casts a D2 shadow under a light source and projection surface. The shadow may preserve outline, relative orientation, or certain contours. It loses depth, hidden faces, interior, material, mass, texture, and sometimes scale. It may distort shape depending on light angle and surface. Many bodies may cast similar shadows.

Therefore, the shadow is a lower-order view of the body, not the body itself.

A disciplined statement would say:

Body : D3 @ TR1 / LightingContext  \--π--\>  Shadow : D2 @ TR1 / WallSurface  \[preserves: projected outline; loses: depth, interior, hidden faces, material; distortion risk: angle and surface; reconstruction: underdetermined\]

This example trains the general habit.

A chart is a shadow of activity.

A proof text is a shadow of formal construction.

A summary is a shadow of source text.

A measurement is a shadow of a measurement interaction.

A model output is a shadow of a generative process and context.

The analogy is useful as long as we do not forget its limits. Not every projection is optical. Not every source is physical. Not every view has a known projection rule. The body-shadow case teaches loss, distortion, and underdetermination; it does not define all projection.

7.11 Holographic reading and mathematics

Mathematics frequently uses lower-order views of richer structures.

A diagram may show a relation that a formal proof later establishes. An equation may render a geometric object. A coordinate chart may display part of a manifold. A graph may render a network. A proof outline may render strategy. A theorem statement may compress a field of definitions, lemmas, and proof obligations.

These are not failures of mathematics. They are working projections.

The danger begins when a projection carries more authority than its role allows.

A diagram may support intuition but not proof. A coordinate rendering may support calculation but not coordinate-free understanding. A theorem statement may be true but hide the machinery required to understand it. A proof sketch may be convincing to experts but not formalised. A symbolic analogy may preserve structure but not establish equivalence.

E7G-T’s holographic reading can therefore help mathematics by asking:

What is the source object?  
What is the representational view?  
What structure is preserved?  
What is lost?  
What formal substrate governs reconstruction?  
What use is admissible?

This does not replace existing mathematical standards. It clarifies the movement between intuition, representation, formal proof, and explanation.

7.12 Holographic reading and proof assistants

Proof assistants make holographic discipline practical.

The user sees text, goals, tactic states, messages, and interface surfaces. These are lower-order views of formal objects and transformations. The proof assistant environment contains definitions, libraries, tactics, elaboration mechanisms, type checking, kernel rules, and proof objects.

The visible script may preserve proof path, intent, and human-editable structure. It may lose tactic expansion, implicit arguments, library dependencies, elaborated terms, and kernel-level proof object structure.

The checked proof object may preserve formal admissibility. It may lose human readability. The explanation may preserve intuition. It may lose kernel-checkable detail.

A disciplined proof-assistant reading therefore avoids two opposite errors.

The first error is treating visible script as the whole proof situation.

The second error is treating kernel acceptance as the whole human meaning of proof.

Both are projections. Both matter. They serve different uses.

An E7G-T proof-of-path interface, if developed, would make these projection layers visible: source theorem, formal substrate, tactic path, proof state, elaborated object, kernel check, explanation, and reliance status.

The practical rule is:

A proof assistant view is holographic when it preserves selected formal structure for a user while exposing what has been hidden by the interface.

7.13 Holographic reading and computation

Computation is full of holographic views.

A log is a lower-order view of runtime occurrence. A stack trace is a lower-order view of failure context. A UI screen is a lower-order view of application state. A dashboard is a lower-order view of system behaviour. A type signature is a lower-order view of input-output boundary. A test result is a lower-order view of behaviour under selected conditions. A repository is a lower-order view of software history and structure.

These views preserve selected structure. They also lose much.

A log may preserve event order but lose unlogged state. A stack trace may preserve call path but lose user intent. A UI may preserve current state but lose backend conditions. A test result may preserve pass/fail status but lose untested behaviours. A type signature may preserve interface boundary but lose performance, side effects, and domain meaning.

Software failures often come from overreading one view.

A test passes, so the system is safe.

A log shows no error, so no error occurred.

The UI looks correct, so the state is correct.

The type checks, so the behaviour is correct.

The dashboard is green, so the service is healthy.

Each sentence may be useful as an orientation cue. None is safe as a whole-system claim without source return.

The repair is:

Treat computational artefacts as projected views with declared source, preservation, loss, and operational use.

7.14 Holographic reading and empirical science

Empirical science also works through projections.

Instruments produce readings. Protocols produce records. Models render relations. Graphs render data. Equations render laws or approximations. Experiments produce constrained observations. Papers summarise evidence. Review articles summarise papers. Public explanations summarise review articles.

This is not a defect. Science cannot operate without projection.

But empirical projection has strict standards. A measurement must connect to instrument, calibration, protocol, uncertainty, and domain of validity. A model must connect to evidence, prediction, constraint, and revision. A graph must connect to data and method. A conclusion must connect to study design and inference limits.

E7G-T can describe this as disciplined holographic projection, but it cannot replace scientific method.

A lower-order measurement may preserve selected structure from a system. It does not contain the whole system. A model may preserve selected relations. It does not become reality. A theory may organise observations. It must still face evidence.

The practical rule is:

Empirical holographic reading requires empirical bridge.

Without measurement map, evidence path, and falsification or revision condition, the claim remains formal analogy, philosophical interpretation, or modelling proposal.

This is especially important for physics-facing speculation. Holographic language may resonate with real scientific terms, but resonance is not bridge. A modelling analogy is not evidence. A diagram is not data. A metaphor is not a measurement.

7.15 Holographic reading and business

Business dashboards, reports, invoices, forecasts, customer reviews, product photos, and financial summaries are all lower-order views.

They are necessary. A business cannot be managed by staring directly at every transaction, customer, supplier, process, and human interaction all at once. It needs projections.

But a business is often damaged by overtrusting projections.

A revenue chart may preserve sales timing but lose margin. A profit figure may preserve an accounting relation but lose cash timing. A product photo may preserve appearance but lose durability. A customer review may preserve one experience but lose wider pattern. A forecast may preserve scenario assumptions but lose uncertainty. A brand image may preserve positioning but lose operational reality.

Holographic reading helps by treating each business artefact as a partial view of a richer source.

A disciplined business statement might say:

MonthlyRevenueChart : D2 @ TR2 / ReportingContext projects TransactionHistory : D4 @ TR2 / AccountingContext; preserves ordered revenue totals; loses margin, returns, causation, customer mix, and cash timing; use: orientation, not full health assessment.

This is not academic fussiness. It changes decisions.

If the use is advertising optimisation, the chart may need channel incidence. If the use is cash planning, it needs payment timing. If the use is product strategy, it needs product mix and margin. If the use is investor reporting, it needs definitions and auditability.

The repair is:

Do not let one business projection become the business.

7.16 Holographic reading and translation

Translation is one of the clearest everyday forms of controlled projection.

A source text is rendered into a target text under language, purpose, audience, legal, cultural, and formatting constraints. The target may preserve meaning, legal force, names, dates, register, function, tone, rhythm, ambiguity, layout, or terminology depending on the task.

It will not preserve everything equally.

A certified legal translation may preserve legal function and document structure while losing stylistic texture. A literary translation may preserve voice and rhythm while shifting literal structure. A technical translation may preserve terminology and instructions while sacrificing rhetorical elegance. A summary translation may preserve information while losing exact wording.

The phrase “same text in another language” is therefore too strong.

A better statement is:

A translation is a target-language projection of a source text under declared purpose, preserving selected invariants and losing or transforming others.

This is a holographic reading in disciplined form.

The target text may carry enough of the source for a given use. But if the use changes, source return may be necessary. A translation prepared for administrative understanding may not support literary analysis. A translated excerpt may not support whole-document terminology judgement. A machine translation may not support legal reliance without human review.

The repair is:

Translation adequacy is use-relative and invariant-specific.

7.17 Holographic reading and AI outputs

AI outputs make projection discipline urgent.

A model answer is a lower-order textual output produced under prompt, model, system constraints, tool use, policies, retrieved sources if any, and conversational context. It may preserve useful structure: explanation, synthesis, pattern recognition, draft language, comparison, or procedural guidance.

It may also lose source grounding, uncertainty, rival views, hidden assumptions, data freshness, formal validity, and evidence density.

Fluency creates a major distortion risk.

A fluent answer may feel like a whole account. It is not. It is a generated view. Its reliability depends on task, sources, tools, verification, and claim type.

E7G-T therefore gives a clean way to read AI output:

AIAnswer : D2/D7 @ TR1 / ConversationContext is a projection of model state, prompt context, possible completions, tool results if any, and language constraints; it may preserve useful explanation but loses much of the source unless cited or checked; reconstruction of truth requires source return, formal verification, empirical evidence, or domain review.

This does not make AI useless. It makes AI usable.

For brainstorming, a lighter projection may be enough. For legal, medical, financial, scientific, formal, or operational reliance, source return is necessary.

The repair is:

Treat AI fluency as projection quality, not proof of source truth.

7.18 Holographic reading and contemplative use

E7G-T may also be used contemplatively.

A person may use the framework to think about life, meaning, spiritual traditions, metaphors, or personal experience. Holographic language can feel especially resonant here because human experience often does seem to contain local views of wider patterns.

A single event may carry a pattern from a life story. A relationship may reveal a recurring structure. A symbol may concentrate a worldview. A ritual may enact a larger cosmology. A phrase may carry a whole tradition. A memory may project a period of life into one image.

These contemplative uses can be meaningful.

But they must not be confused with proof.

A resonance is not an identity. A symbolic pattern is not empirical evidence. A spiritual metaphor is not a formal theorem. A personal insight is not automatically a universal law. A local experience may be meaningful without reconstructing the whole of reality.

The disciplined contemplative statement is:

This local experience may be read as preserving a pattern from a wider life-context or interpretive framework, under a contemplative bridge. It supports reflection, not empirical or formal proof.

This allows E7G-T to be useful in a life layer without becoming grandiose.

The repair is:

Contemplative holographic reading should produce humility, not inflated certainty.

7.19 The “part contains the whole” repair

The central repair of this chapter can be stated directly.

Instead of:

The part contains the whole.

Use:

The part may preserve selected structure from the whole under a projection rule or context.

Instead of:

The whole can be reconstructed from the part.

Use:

The part may constrain candidate wholes, but reconstruction depends on rival sources, projection rule, and additional evidence or formal constraints.

Instead of:

The local view proves the higher source.

Use:

The local view may be compatible with a higher source; proof or evidence requires bridge discipline.

Instead of:

Everything is holographic.

Use:

Some modelling situations are fruitfully read as lower-order views of richer sources, provided preservation, loss, distortion, and reconstruction status are declared.

Instead of:

Holography explains reality.

Use:

Holographic reading is a modelling discipline and may become reality-facing only under an appropriate bridge.

These repairs do not weaken the framework. They make it less fragile.

A disciplined claim survives criticism better than a beautiful slogan.

7.20 The holographic reading check

A quick holographic reading check can be used whenever a local view is treated as carrying a wider source.

Ask:

What is the lower-order view?

What is the source or candidate source?

Is the source known, traceable, hypothesised, formal, empirical, fictional, or unknown?

What projection context produced the view?

What structure is preserved?

What structure is lost?

What distortion risk exists?

What temporal regime is active for source and view?

What reconstruction status is justified?

What use is admissible?

What overread is blocked?

The short version is:

View, source, context, preservation, loss, reconstruction, use.

If those cannot be named, the holographic reading should remain intuition, not reliance.

7.21 From holographic reading to practical calculus

The previous chapters built the foundation:

Chapter 1 named the under-geometrised condition.

Chapter 2 introduced orders of extension.

Chapter 3 separated extension, projection, and reconstruction.

Chapter 4 introduced boundary, incidence, slice, and invariant.

Chapter 5 treated algebra as rendering.

Chapter 6 disciplined temporal regimes.

This chapter has now repaired the holographic intuition.

What remains is to turn the whole framework into a repeatable practice.

A reader should not need to remember every chapter in order to use E7G-T. The framework needs a small calculus of moves: place, bound, relate, extend, slice, project, preserve and lose, transform, reconstruct, bridge, and stop. These moves should work at several levels: a quick OneLine, a MiniCard for modelling, and a FullCard for reliance.

Chapter 8 therefore turns to Practical Calculus.

It asks the operational question:

What do I do next with a concrete object, claim, problem, model, proof, system, or observation?

Chapter 8 — Practical Calculus

8.1 From foundation to movement

The previous chapters built the conceptual discipline. They named the under-geometrised condition, introduced orders of extension, separated extension from projection, declared the four hinges, treated algebra as rendering, disciplined temporal regimes, and repaired the holographic intuition.

Those chapters answer the question:

What must be understood before E7G-T can be used responsibly?

This chapter answers a different question:

What do I do next?

A modelling framework becomes practical only when it changes the next move. It must help the user inspect, repair, formalise, compare, test, bridge, or stop a claim that would otherwise remain vague. If it merely adds new vocabulary to an unchanged habit of reasoning, it has not yet become a calculus.

The practical calculus layer is therefore the operational heart of E7G-T. It converts the foundation into repeatable moves.

It does not replace mathematical proof, empirical method, software testing, legal review, medical diagnosis, business accounting, or domain expertise. It tells the user how to place a claim before such methods are invoked, how to avoid confusing representation with source, and how to decide whether the next move is lightweight orientation or stronger reliance.

The calculus question is:

What do I do next with a concrete object, claim, problem, model, proof, system, or observation?

The answer is not always “build a larger model”. Sometimes the next move is to mark a boundary. Sometimes it is to name a projection. Sometimes it is to return to source. Sometimes it is to downgrade the claim. Sometimes it is to stop.

8.2 The three practical levels

The calculus has three practical levels.

Level 1 is Marking. Its output is a OneLine.

Level 2 is Modelling. Its output is a MiniCard.

Level 3 is Reliance. Its output is a FullCard.

These levels exist because not every situation deserves the same weight.

A casual idea, quick caution, or local repair may need only a OneLine. A repeated reasoning problem, design review, model comparison, translation issue, dashboard interpretation, proof-assistant interface note, or AI-output evaluation may need a MiniCard. A claim prepared for publication, formalisation, empirical bridge, operational reliance, safety-sensitive use, or external review may need a FullCard.

The rule is:

Use the smallest level that changes the next modelling move without hiding risk.

This rule prevents two opposite failures.

The first failure is under-modelling. A strong claim is left as ordinary prose, so its boundary, projection, evidence, and loss remain hidden.

The second failure is over-modelling. A simple orientation cue is inflated into a heavy formal record, wasting attention and making the framework feel ceremonial.

E7G-T should not become bureaucracy. It should become movement discipline.

8.3 Level 1: OneLine

A OneLine is used for quick orientation.

It gives an object or claim an initial E7G-T position and blocks one dangerous overread. It should be short enough to write in ordinary work but precise enough to change the next move.

Template:

E7-OneLine:  
X is treated as \[order/role\] under \[context\]; admissible move: \[next move\]; blocked overread: \[one phrase\].

Example:

A dashboard is treated as a D2/D7 projection of business activity under a reporting context; admissible move: source return before reliance; blocked overread: dashboard is not the business.

Example:

A proof text is treated as a D2/D1 rendering of proof work under a formal-language context; admissible move: identify proof substrate; blocked overread: readable text is not automatically proof validity.

Example:

An AI answer is treated as a D2/D7 textual projection under conversation context; admissible move: source check for factual reliance; blocked overread: fluency is not evidence.

Example:

A metaphorical equation is treated as a bridge cue under interpretive context; admissible move: declare bridge mode; blocked overread: resemblance is not identity.

A OneLine is enough when it changes behaviour. If the user now knows to check source, declare boundary, identify context, or stop an overclaim, the OneLine has done its job.

A OneLine is not enough when the claim will be reused, published, formalised, bridged, or relied upon.

8.4 Level 2: MiniCard

A MiniCard is used when a reasoning pattern needs to be reusable.

It is still compact, but it records the main elements needed to prevent projection confusion: entity, placement, boundary, extension, projection, preservation, loss, invariant, reconstruction status, bridge mode, next move, and blocked overread.

Template:

E7MiniCard:  
  id: ""  
  entity: ""  
  plainMeaning: ""  
  placement: "X : Dn @ TRi / C"  
  boundary: ""  
  extensionAccount: ""  
  projectionAccount: ""  
  preserved: \[\]  
  lost: \[\]  
  invariant: ""  
  reconstructionStatus: "known | hypothesis | underdetermined | not-claimed"  
  bridgeMode: "none | contemplative-what-if | philosophical-interpretation | formal-analogy | formal-compatibility | empirical-testable"  
  nextMove: "inspect | repair | formalize | test | compare | stop"  
  blockedOverread: \[\]

A MiniCard is not meant to be beautiful. It is meant to be inspectable.

It should answer enough questions for another reader to understand what the model is doing and where the claim stops.

Consider a sales dashboard.

E7MiniCard:  
  id: "dashboard-as-projection"  
  entity: "Sales dashboard"  
  plainMeaning: "A reporting surface showing selected business indicators."  
  placement: "Dashboard : D2/D7 @ TR2 / BusinessReportingContext"  
  boundary: "Visible tiles, filters, metric definitions, data freshness, and source links."  
  extensionAccount: "Built from records, aggregation rules, time windows, and presentation layout."  
  projectionAccount: "Projects selected business activity into a low-order visual field."  
  preserved:  
    \- "selected metric values"  
    \- "declared aggregation window"  
  lost:  
    \- "individual transactions unless drilldown exists"  
    \- "causal explanation"  
    \- "data-quality uncertainty unless shown"  
  invariant: "Metric definitions only if version-pinned."  
  reconstructionStatus: "underdetermined without source return"  
  bridgeMode: "none"  
  nextMove: "source-return before operational reliance"  
  blockedOverread:  
    \- "dashboard is not the business"  
    \- "green tile is not proof of health"

The MiniCard is useful because it changes the next move. It tells the user not only what the dashboard is, but also what should happen before relying on it.

8.5 Level 3: FullCard

A FullCard is used when a claim must support reliance.

Reliance includes publication, formalisation, empirical bridge work, operational decisions, safety-sensitive contexts, repeated project governance, or any situation where a weak projection could cause serious error.

A FullCard expands the MiniCard into a full record. It should include entity of concern, claim text, bounded context, order profile, temporal regime, boundary, extension account, projective account, transformation account, reconstruction account, bridge account, maturity profile, admissible use, non-admissible use, stop condition, and next admissible move.

A FullCard is not needed for every idea. But when a claim crosses domain boundaries or rises in authority, a FullCard may be the difference between disciplined modelling and rhetorical inflation.

The core FullCard questions are:

What exactly is the entity of concern?

What claim is being made about it?

At what order and temporal regime is it placed?

What is its boundary?

What lower-order accountability supports it?

What projection account is active?

What is preserved and lost?

What transformation class and invariant are claimed?

What reconstruction status is justified?

What bridge mode is being used?

What is the weakest link?

What use is admissible?

Where must the claim stop?

A FullCard is the right output when a claim is no longer merely useful for thought, but must be accountable to other people, systems, disciplines, or decisions.

8.6 The ten primitive moves

The practical calculus uses ten primitive moves.

They are intentionally few.

1\. Place.

What kind of thing is this in E7G-T?

Minimal output:

X : Dn @ TRi / C

2\. Bound.

What makes it this thing rather than a blur?

Minimal output:

∂X \= ...

3\. Incide.

What is it structurally incident with?

Minimal output:

A ⋈\_C B

4\. Extend.

What higher-order extension is being built?

Minimal output:

X ↑ n

5\. Slice.

What local slice is being selected?

Minimal output:

S\_C^s(X)

6\. Project.

What lower-order view is being produced?

Minimal output:

π\_C^{n→k}(X) \= Y

7\. Preserve/Lose.

What survives and what disappears?

Minimal output:

Preserve: ...  
Lose: ...  
Risk: ...

8\. Transform.

What rule changes it?

Minimal output:

τ\_L(X) \= Y

9\. Reconstruct.

What source hypotheses explain the view?

Minimal output:

ρ(Y) ⇒ {X\_i}

10\. Bridge/Stop.

May this claim cross contexts, or must it stop?

Minimal output:

β\_M^{A↔B}(...)

or:

Stop(...)

The moves can be used lightly or heavily. A OneLine may use only Place and Stop. A MiniCard may use most moves in plain language. A FullCard may record all of them.

8.7 Move 1: Place

Place is the first move.

Before asking what something means, proves, predicts, or explains, E7G-T asks where it is being placed as an entity of concern.

Placement has the practical form:

X : Dn @ TRi / C

This says:

X is being treated at order Dn, under temporal regime TRi, in context C.

Placement does not need to be final. A provisional placement is allowed. But unplaced objects should not carry strong claims.

Examples:

Dashboard : D2/D7 @ TR2 / BusinessReportingContext

ProofText : D2/D1 @ TR6 / FormalPublicationContext

AIAnswer : D2/D7 @ TR1 / ConversationContext

RuntimeLog : D1/D2 @ TR2 / SystemMonitoringContext

ProductSample : D3 @ TR1 / QualityInspectionContext

ScenarioSet : D5 @ TR3 / StrategyPlanningContext

A placement is good if it changes the next question. If the dashboard is D2/D7, ask for source and projection loss. If the scenario set is D5/TR3, ask for admissible branches. If the product sample is D3/TR1, do not infer manufacturing history without source return.

The law is:

Typing before operation.

No operator may be applied until the object has a declared placement or provisional placement.

8.8 Move 2: Bound

Bound declares the object boundary.

The practical symbol is:

∂X

Boundary answers:

What makes this object this object for the present use?

A boundary may be physical, formal, contextual, temporal, operational, semantic, legal, computational, or evidential.

Examples:

∂LineSegment \= {endpointA, endpointB}

∂SurfacePatch \= boundaryLineComplex

∂SoftwareModule \= declared interface boundary, dependencies, and runtime assumptions

∂Dashboard \= visible tiles, filters, metric definitions, source links, and reporting window

∂Translation \= source text, target text, purpose, language pair, certification context, and recipient use

∂Proof \= assumptions, definitions, formal substrate, inference rules, and theorem statement

Boundary is contextual but not arbitrary. The same object may have different boundaries for different uses, but the boundary must match the claim.

If the boundary changes, the claim changes.

A sales figure under one reporting boundary cannot silently become a whole-business health claim. A proof sketch boundary cannot silently become a formal proof boundary. A translated excerpt boundary cannot silently become a whole-document translation judgement.

8.9 Move 3: Incide

Incide declares accountable relation.

The practical symbol is:

A ⋈\_C B

This means:

A and B are incident under context C.

In expanded form:

Incide(A, B | relationKind, context, admissibleUse)

The important word is relationKind.

A relation may be boundary, dependency, causation, containment, transformation, projection, evidence, semantic reference, temporal order, formal derivation, interface call, data source, translation alignment, or operational support.

Do not use vague relation words when the relation matters.

Instead of:

This metric is connected to profit.

Ask:

Is the metric a component of profit, a proxy for profit, a cause of profit, a correlate of profit, a forecast input, a reporting artefact, or merely a visual neighbour on a dashboard?

Instead of:

This concept is linked to quantum measurement.

Ask:

Is the relation contemplative resonance, philosophical interpretation, formal analogy, formal compatibility, or empirical-testable bridge?

Incidence prevents the word connected from doing too much work.

8.10 Move 4: Extend

Extend builds higher-order accountability.

The practical symbol is:

X ↑ n

This means:

Extend X into order Dn, declaring the obligations required at that order.

Extension is not decoration. A higher-order claim costs more.

To extend a local mark into a path, relation and endpoint boundary must be declared. To extend a path into a surface, spread and contour must be declared. To extend a surface into a body, interior, exterior, and form-boundary must be declared. To extend a body into duration, slice relation and persistence must be declared. To extend a history into variation, admissible alternatives must be declared. To extend variation into law, transformation class and invariant must be declared. To extend law into context, rendering, interpretation, bridge, and use must be declared.

Example:

CurrentRevenuePoint ↑ D4 \= RevenueHistory

This is admissible only if the point is supported by a time series, reporting windows, source records, and temporal context. Otherwise the point remains a local mark.

Example:

ProofSketch ↑ D6 \= FormalProofClaim

This is admissible only if a formal substrate, inference rules, definitions, and proof object or proof path are supplied. Otherwise the sketch remains a projection of possible proof work.

The law is:

Extension is not projection.

Extension builds an account upward. Projection renders a source downward.

8.11 Move 5: Slice

Slice selects a local view from a richer structure.

The practical symbol is:

S\_C^s(X)

This means:

The slice of X selected by context C and selector s.

The selector may be time, location, coordinate, filter, query, view, role, paragraph, proof state, branch, measurement window, interface state, or narrative focus.

Examples:

S\_C^t(WorldForm\_D4) \= BodyState\_D3\_at\_t

S\_Report^May(TransactionHistory) \= MayRevenueSlice

S\_Interface^s(SoftwareSystem) \= ScreenState\_s

S\_Proof^goal(ProofConstruction) \= CurrentGoalState

Slice discipline prevents whole-state inflation.

A slice may be important. It may even be decisive under the right relation. But it is not automatically the whole. A counterexample may refute a universal claim, but a single sample may not establish a population. A screenshot may demonstrate a visible bug, but not its cause. A dashboard month may show a period, but not business health.

The law is:

State the slice relation before making the whole claim.

8.12 Move 6: Project

Project produces a lower-order view from a richer source.

The practical symbol is:

π\_C^{n→k}(X) \= Y

This means:

Y is a Dk view, trace, shadow, rendering, record, diagram, summary, measurement, or interface projected from Dn source X under context C.

Examples:

π\_Report^{D4→D2}(BusinessActivity) \= RevenueChart

π\_Formal^{D6→D2}(ProofObject) \= ProofText

π\_UI^{D4/D6→D2}(SoftwareSystem) \= Dashboard

π\_Measure^{Source→D0}(SystemState) \= MeasurementValue

Projection is normal. It is how usable views are made.

But every projection must declare preservation and loss when the claim is stronger than casual orientation.

Projection is not identity. Projection is not reconstruction. Projection loss accumulates in stacks.

If X projects to Y, it does not follow that Y is X. It also does not follow that X can be uniquely recovered from Y.

The practical projection record is:

Y \= π\_C(X)  
Preserve: \[...\]  
Lose: \[...\]  
Risk: \[...\]  
Return if: \[...\]  
Do not infer: \[...\]

8.13 Move 7: Preserve/Lose

Preserve/Lose prices projection.

The move asks:

What survives the projection, transformation, slice, translation, summary, rendering, measurement, or bridge?

What disappears?

What distortion risk is introduced?

A projection without preserved structure is useless. A projection without loss is identity. Since projection is not identity, loss must be expected.

Examples:

A map preserves navigational connectivity and loses full terrain.

A summary preserves central claims and loses wording, nuance, minority detail, and evidential density.

A dashboard preserves selected metric values and loses transaction detail, uncertainty, and causation.

A proof text preserves proof path and loses hidden formal expansion unless supplied.

A translation preserves selected meaning or legal force and loses other language-specific structure.

An AI answer preserves a useful synthesis and may lose source grounding unless checked.

The practical record is:

Preserve: \[typed list\]  
Lose: \[typed list\]  
Risk: \[distortion list\]

The risk line matters because some views not only omit structure but change apparent emphasis. A chart scale can distort trend. A fluent AI answer can distort confidence. A metaphor can import unwanted associations. A translation can resolve ambiguity that the source left open.

8.14 Move 8: Transform

Transform declares rule-governed change.

The practical symbol is:

τ\_L(X) \= Y

This means:

X transforms into Y under law, rule, method, operator, or procedure L.

Transformation belongs naturally to D6, but transformation events may occur under local-time, shared-time, cyclic-time, or other temporal regimes.

Examples:

τ\_rewrite(ExpressionA) \= ExpressionB

τ\_refactor(CodeV1) \= CodeV2

τ\_translation(SourceText) \= TargetText

τ\_tactic(ProofStateA) \= ProofStateB

τ\_accountingRules(TransactionRecords) \= FinancialReport

A transformation is not just a change. It is a change under a rule.

The model must declare:

input boundary;  
output boundary;  
rule or method;  
preserved invariant;  
lost structure;  
context;  
validation or checking method when needed.
