A rewrite may preserve formal equivalence. A refactor may preserve external behaviour. A translation may preserve legal force. A tactic may preserve proof obligation under formal rules. An accounting transformation may preserve defined totals under reporting standards.

The key question is:

What is preserved under this transformation class?

If no transformation class is declared, no invariant claim is complete.

8.15 Move 9: Reconstruct

Reconstruct works from lower-order view to candidate source.

The practical symbol is:

ρ\_C^{k→n}(Y | P, K, I) ⇒ {X₁, X₂, ...}

This means:

From lower-order view Y, projection rule P, constraints K, and invariants I, construct a set of candidate higher-order source hypotheses.

The default is set-valued.

This is important. Reconstruction is underdetermined by default because many sources may produce the same or similar view.

A shadow may fit several bodies. A symptom may fit several diagnoses. A dashboard pattern may fit several causes. A data trend may fit several mechanisms. A proof outline may fit several formal proof paths. A fluent answer may fit several hidden processes. A philosophical analogy may fit several interpretations.

The reconstruction statuses are:

source known;  
source traceable;  
compatible;  
constrained;  
bounded rival set;  
unique under assumptions;  
reconstructed;  
underdetermined;  
blocked.

The repair is:

Compatible with source does not mean reconstructed from source.

Only declare reconstruction when rival sources have been bounded and eliminated by formal constraint, evidence, source traceability, or declared stipulation in fiction or simulation.

8.16 Move 10: Bridge/Stop

Bridge/Stop decides whether a claim may cross contexts.

The practical bridge symbol is:

β\_M^{A↔B}(X) \= Y

This means:

X in context or substrate A is bridged to Y in context or substrate B under bridge mode M.

Bridge modes include:

contemplative-what-if;  
philosophical-interpretation;  
formal-analogy;  
formal-compatibility;  
empirical-testable.

The stop move is equally important.

Stop(...)

A strong framework must be able to stop itself.

Stop when the entity of concern is unnamed.

Stop when boundary is missing and the claim needs one.

Stop when a projection has no loss account.

Stop when reconstruction is asserted from a lower-order view without rival sources.

Stop when a metaphor is treated as proof.

Stop when formal analogy is treated as formal equivalence.

Stop when philosophical interpretation is treated as empirical evidence.

Stop when empirical language appears without empirical bridge.

Bridge claims inherit the weakest link. A chain containing a philosophical interpretation cannot end as an empirical conclusion unless a separate empirical-testable bridge is supplied.

The practical rule is:

No stage may be skipped by rhetoric.

8.17 Minimal derivation block

A practical E7G-T derivation can be written as a readable block.

Template:

E7-Derivation:  
  1\. Entity: X  
  2\. Placement: X : Dn @ TRi / C  
  3\. Boundary: ∂X \= ...  
  4\. Extension account: ...  
  5\. Projection account: ...  
  6\. Transformation/invariant: ...  
  7\. Preserved structure: ...  
  8\. Lost structure: ...  
  9\. Reconstruction status: known | hypothesis | underdetermined | blocked  
  10\. Bridge mode: none | what-if | interpretation | analogy | compatibility | empirical-testable  
  11\. Next admissible move: inspect | repair | formalize | test | compare | stop

This block is useful because it shows where the model is incomplete.

If items 1–3 cannot be completed, the work is not yet an E7G-T modelling move. It is only a cue.

If items 7–9 cannot be completed, the projection may still be useful, but it must not carry a strong source claim.

If item 10 cannot be completed, the claim must not cross into physics, empirical science, legal authority, medical judgement, or other external domain reliance.

The derivation block is not a proof. It is a modelling record that makes the next move accountable.

8.18 Recipe: Algebra-to-Geometry

Use this recipe when the input is an equation, symbolic expression, coordinate tuple, matrix, graph, type signature, or formula.

Recipe: AlgebraToGeometry

1\. Identify the algebraic carrier.

Is it an equation, coordinate rendering, invariant notation, transformation calculus, data formula, type signature, or formal object?

2\. Declare the EntityOfConcern.

Is the entity the algebraic object itself, or the geometric object, system, process, or claim it renders?

3\. Declare the rendering context.

Which coordinate system, basis, formal language, notation, substrate, or domain convention is active?

4\. Recover the geometric account.

What locality, extension, boundary, incidence, transformation, and invariant does the algebra express?

5\. Declare projection loss.

What does the algebra hide: construction history, context, measurement basis, domain restriction, diagrammatic structure, semantic use, or empirical assumptions?

6\. Decide the next move.

Keep as algebra, lift to geometric account, compare with another rendering, require source return, formalise, test, or stop.

Example:

Input:  
x² \+ y² \= r²

E7 repair:  
The equation is a D1/D2 algebraic rendering of circle-as-geometric-extension under Cartesian coordinate context. It preserves radial invariance and boundary relation. It loses construction history, drawing scale, physical realisation, and non-Cartesian renderings.

Blocked overread:  
The equation is not the circle unless the EntityOfConcern is explicitly the equation-object.

8.19 Recipe: Projection-Loss

Use this recipe for diagrams, dashboards, summaries, translations, AI outputs, screenshots, maps, measurements, reports, simplified explanations, and popularisations.

Recipe: ProjectionLoss

1\. Name the source or candidate source.

2\. Name the view.

3\. Declare the projection rule or rendering method.

4\. List preserved structure.

5\. List lost structure.

6\. List distortion risks.

7\. Declare whether source return is needed.

8\. State the blocked overread.

Short form:

Y \= π\_C(X)  
Preserve: \[...\]  
Lose: \[...\]  
Risk: \[...\]  
Return if: \[...\]  
Do not infer: \[...\]

Example:

Summary \= π\_editorial(SourceDocument)  
Preserve: main claims, selected order, key terminology if faithful.  
Lose: wording, nuance, minority details, layout, evidential density.  
Risk: emphasis distortion.  
Return if: legal, technical, or evidential reliance is required.  
Do not infer: summary is the source.

This recipe is the everyday core of the practical calculus.

8.20 Recipe: Source-Reconstruction

Use this recipe when a lower-order trace, view, measurement, symptom, output, or pattern is used to infer a higher-order source.

Recipe: SourceReconstruction

1\. Register the observed view Y.

2\. Declare the candidate source order Dn.

3\. Declare the projection rule P, if known.

4\. Declare constraints K.

5\. Declare invariants I.

6\. Generate rival source hypotheses {X\_i}.

7\. Eliminate rivals only by formal constraint, observation, evidence, or source traceability.

8\. Declare reconstruction status.

9\. Block source inflation.

Source inflation is the error of treating a compatible source as a proven source.

The governing repair is:

Compatible-with-source \!= reconstructed-from-source

Example:

A revenue dip is observed in a monthly chart. Candidate sources include seasonality, stockout, advertising change, reporting delay, product mix shift, marketplace issue, or demand change. The chart alone does not reconstruct the cause. Source return is required.

8.21 Recipe: Temporal-Slice

Use this recipe when time, no-time, flow, sequence, possibility, timelessness, history, future, cyclicity, or branching appears.

Recipe: TemporalSlice

1\. Identify the time-like wording.

2\. Choose temporal regime: TR0–TR6.

3\. Declare whether the object is a state, process, world-form, possibility field, transformation law, record, or completed invariant.

4\. If local flow appears, declare the local slice selector.

5\. If whole/slice appears, mark source regime and view regime separately.

6\. If possibilities appear, place them in D5 variation-extension unless another substrate is declared.

7\. Block overread into empirical physics unless bridge mode is empirical-testable.

Example:

ObservedMoment \= S\_C^t(WorldForm)  
WorldForm : D4 @ TR6 if treated as complete for the model  
ObserverSequence : D3/D4 slices @ TR1 / LocalObservationContext  
PossibilityField : D5 @ TR3 if alternatives are active

Blocked overread:  
This does not prove block universe, consciousness collapse, or arbitrary reality creation.

8.22 Recipe: Bridge

Use this recipe when E7G-T is applied to another domain.

Recipe: Bridge

1\. Name the E7G-T source structure.

2\. Name the target domain or substrate.

3\. Choose bridge mode.

4\. Declare preserved structure.

5\. Declare lost structure.

6\. Declare bridge congruence level.

7\. Declare weakest-link scope.

8\. Declare admissible and non-admissible use.

9\. Decide whether the next move is interpretation, formal comparison, compatibility proof, empirical test, or stop.

Bridge escalation is:

what-if → interpretation → analogy → compatibility → empirical-testable → validated

No stage may be skipped by rhetoric.

Example:

E7G-T measurement-as-projection may be bridged to quantum measurement only as formal-analogy or philosophical interpretation unless a formal substrate, measurement map, prediction or constraint, rival comparison, and empirical evidence path are declared.

Blocked overread:  
A bridge analogy is not a physics result.

8.23 Practical calculus laws

The calculus is governed by ten laws.

Law 1 — Typing Before Operation.

No operator may be applied until the object has a declared placement or provisional placement.

Law 2 — Extension Is Not Projection.

X ↑ n is not the same as π\_C^{n→k}(X).

Law 3 — Projection Is Not Reconstruction.

π\_C^{n→k}(X)=Y does not imply ρ\_C^{k→n}(Y)=X.

Law 4 — Projection Loss Accumulates.

A projection stack cannot silently regain lost structure.

Law 5 — Slice and Projection Need Not Commute.

Projecting first and then slicing may produce a different result from slicing first and then projecting.

Law 6 — Boundary Is Contextual But Not Arbitrary.

∂\_C(X) may vary by context, but each boundary declaration must state its admissible use.

Law 7 — Invariance Requires a Transformation Class.

Inv(X) is incomplete. Inv\_T(X) is admissible.

Law 8 — Bridge Claims Inherit the Weakest Link.

The strength of a bridge stack cannot exceed its weakest declared bridge link.

Law 9 — Empirical Language Requires Empirical Bridge.

No empirical conclusion is admissible without empirical-testable bridge mode, measurement map, baseline, rival explanations, and evidence path.

Law 10 — The Next Move Is the Practical Test.

If the calculus does not change the next inspection, repair, formalisation, test, comparison, or stop decision, do not use E7G-T language.

The tenth law is especially important. E7G-T should not be used because it sounds deeper. It should be used because it changes what a responsible modeller does next.

8.24 Worked example: AI answer

Entity:  
AI answer to a factual question.

OneLine:

An AI answer is treated as a D2/D7 textual projection under conversation context; admissible move: source return for factual reliance; blocked overread: fluency is not evidence.

MiniCard:

E7MiniCard:  
  id: "ai-answer-as-projection"  
  entity: "AI answer"  
  plainMeaning: "A generated textual response produced under prompt, model, tool, policy, and context constraints."  
  placement: "AIAnswer : D2/D7 @ TR1 / ConversationContext"  
  boundary: "Visible answer text, prompt context, tool results if any, and stated citations or lack thereof."  
  extensionAccount: "Built from model response process, context, and any retrieved sources."  
  projectionAccount: "Projects a possible answer into readable language."  
  preserved:  
    \- "explanation structure"  
    \- "synthesis if sources and reasoning are adequate"  
    \- "draft wording"  
  lost:  
    \- "full source corpus unless cited"  
    \- "uncertainty unless declared"  
    \- "rival views unless included"  
    \- "formal or empirical validation unless performed"  
  invariant: "None unless source-checked, formally verified, or domain-reviewed."  
  reconstructionStatus: "not-claimed for truth without source return"  
  bridgeMode: "none unless domain bridge is declared"  
  nextMove: "verify, cite, formalise, or downgrade depending on use"  
  blockedOverread:  
    \- "fluent answer is not proof"  
    \- "confidence is not source truth"

This example shows the practical value of the calculus. It neither dismisses AI nor overtrusts it. It says what must happen next.

8.25 Worked example: proof assistant interface

Entity:  
Proof assistant session.

OneLine:

A proof assistant session is treated as a D2/D7 interface projection of D6 formal proof work under kernel and library context; admissible move: separate visible script, proof state, and proof object; blocked overread: script text alone is not the whole proof situation.

MiniCard:

E7MiniCard:  
  id: "proof-assistant-session"  
  entity: "Proof assistant session"  
  plainMeaning: "A human-visible interface over formal proof construction and checking."  
  placement: "Session : D2/D7 @ TR1/TR6 / FormalKernelContext"  
  boundary: "Visible script, current goals, imports, library versions, kernel, tactics, and proof state."  
  extensionAccount: "Extends local syntax and tactic steps into formal proof obligations and proof object construction."  
  projectionAccount: "Projects formal proof states and transformations into interface text for human use."  
  preserved:  
    \- "current goals"  
    \- "visible proof path"  
    \- "kernel acceptance status when checked"  
  lost:  
    \- "implicit elaboration unless inspected"  
    \- "library dependency complexity"  
    \- "human explanation unless supplied"  
  invariant: "Theorem derivability under the declared kernel and library versions."  
  reconstructionStatus: "traceable if proof object and environment are available"  
  bridgeMode: "formal-compatibility inside declared substrate"  
  nextMove: "expose proof object, dependency graph, or explanation layer as needed"  
  blockedOverread:  
    \- "green check is not full pedagogy"  
    \- "human-readable script is not identical with proof object"

This example points toward later chapters on proof, proof assistants, and Proof-of-Path.

8.26 Worked example: speculative physics bridge

Entity:  
Claim that quantum measurement can be read as projection.

OneLine:

Quantum measurement as projection is treated as a formal-analogy or philosophical-interpretation bridge unless empirical-testable structure is declared; admissible move: state bridge mode; blocked overread: analogy is not new physics.

MiniCard:

E7MiniCard:  
  id: "measurement-as-projection-bridge"  
  entity: "Quantum measurement interpreted through projection language"  
  plainMeaning: "A cautious bridge reading in which measurement output is treated as a context-conditioned lower-order appearance."  
  placement: "MeasurementReading : D0/D7 @ TR1 / PhysicsInterpretationContext"  
  boundary: "Interpretive claim only, unless formal and empirical substrate are supplied."  
  extensionAccount: "No new physical extension claimed. E7G-T provides modelling vocabulary only."  
  projectionAccount: "Measurement outcome is read as lower-order output of a richer state/process under measurement context."  
  preserved:  
    \- "structural analogy: context-conditioned appearance"  
    \- "projection-loss intuition"  
  lost:  
    \- "formal quantum substrate unless supplied"  
    \- "empirical predictions unless supplied"  
    \- "experimental evidence unless supplied"  
  invariant: "None beyond analogy unless formalised."  
  reconstructionStatus: "not-claimed"  
  bridgeMode: "formal-analogy or philosophical-interpretation by default"  
  nextMove: "formalise against standard quantum formalism or stop before empirical claim"  
  blockedOverread:  
    \- "not a Theory of Everything"  
    \- "not empirical physics without empirical bridge"  
    \- "not proof that observation creates reality arbitrarily"

This example is important because it shows the Stop function. E7G-T may inspire interpretation, but it must not smuggle empirical authority.

8.27 Worked example: book as projection of kernel

Entity:  
A practical book based on E7G-T.

OneLine:

The book is treated as a D7 publication projection of the E7G-T kernel under reader-commercial context; admissible move: preserve intuition while marking limits; blocked overread: book is not the governing source.

MiniCard:

E7MiniCard:  
  id: "book-as-kernel-projection"  
  entity: "Practical E7G-T book"  
  plainMeaning: "An accessible reader-facing projection of the fuller kernel and practical calculus."  
  placement: "Book : D7 publication projection @ TR6 / ReaderCommercialContext"  
  boundary: "Published chapters, examples, reader promise, market positioning, and declared limits."  
  extensionAccount: "Extends selected kernel ideas into examples, explanations, and practical applications."  
  projectionAccount: "Projects the kernel into accessible prose for non-specialist use."  
  preserved:  
    \- "core intuition"  
    \- "extension/projection distinction"  
    \- "selected examples"  
    \- "safe non-ToE posture"  
  lost:  
    \- "full formal records"  
    \- "complete linter detail"  
    \- "all bridge constraints"  
    \- "technical maturity profile"  
  invariant: "Core stop rules if consistently preserved."  
  reconstructionStatus: "source-return required for technical claims"  
  bridgeMode: "none or philosophical-interpretation depending on chapter"  
  nextMove: "write as controlled projection and point back to kernel for strong claims"  
  blockedOverread:  
    \- "accessible book is not the source kernel"  
    \- "holographic metaphor is not empirical claim"

This example also defines how the present monograph should be treated if later projected into a popular guide.

8.28 The practical calculus prompt

A user can invoke the practical calculus with a simple prompt:

Apply the E7G-T Practical Calculus Layer to the following object or claim.

Return:

1\. OneLine placement.

2\. MiniCard if useful.

3\. Projection-loss account if a view/source relation is present.

4\. Reconstruction status if a source is inferred.

5\. Bridge mode if another domain is involved.

6\. Next admissible move.

7\. Blocked overread.

This prompt is intentionally practical. It does not ask for grand theory. It asks for the next responsible move.

Example input:

This chart proves our product strategy is working.

Possible output:

OneLine:  
The chart is treated as a D2 projection of selected business records under reporting context; admissible move: source return and causal analysis; blocked overread: chart trend is not proof of strategy success.

Next admissible move:  
Inspect source data, product mix, time window, advertising changes, stock status, margin, and rival explanations.

This is the calculus doing its work.

8.29 The linter habit

The practical calculus also trains a linter habit.

A linter is not a judge of truth. It is a tool that detects missing declarations and risky patterns.

An E7G-T linter would ask:

Is the entity of concern named?

Is placement declared or marked provisional?

Is boundary declared?

Is context visible?

Is projection being treated as identity?

Is reconstruction asserted from a lower-order view?

Is preservation typed?

Is loss named?

Is invariant declared with transformation class?

Is a slice being treated as the whole?

Is temporal regime declared where time matters?

Is bridge mode declared where domains are crossed?

Is empirical language used without empirical bridge?

Is the next admissible move stated?

If three or more important declarations are missing, the claim should be downgraded to ordinary prose, cue, intuition, or OneLine until repaired.

This linter habit may later become software, proof-assistant interface support, AI prompting pattern, writing checklist, or research-governance tool.

8.30 From practical calculus to formal neighbourhoods

The practical calculus is deliberately not a full formal system.

It is a disciplined modelling practice that can be used before formalisation, alongside formalisation, or after projection into applied contexts. It tells the user what to declare, what to preserve, what to lose, what to bridge, and when to stop.

But once the calculus is stable enough, a natural question appears:

What established formal neighbourhoods does E7G-T resemble, borrow from, or need to respect?

It touches geometry, topology, category theory, type theory, proof theory, model theory, systems theory, diagrammatic reasoning, formal verification, computation, semantics, measurement theory, and philosophy of modelling. It should not pretend to replace these fields. It should learn how to stand near them without stealing their authority.

Chapter 9 therefore turns to Formal Neighbourhoods.

Its task is to locate E7G-T near existing formal traditions, clarify what is analogy and what might later become formal compatibility, and prevent the framework from claiming novelty where established mathematics already has stronger tools.

Chapter 9 — Formal Neighbourhoods

9.1 Why formal neighbourhoods matter

E7G-T is not being introduced into an empty landscape.

Many mature fields already study structure, transformation, relation, representation, proof, model, type, space, boundary, diagram, measurement, computation, and interpretation. Any serious foundation for E7G-T must therefore locate the framework near existing traditions without pretending to have invented what those traditions already do well.

This chapter is a map of neighbourhoods, not a claim of conquest.

A formal neighbourhood is a region of established mathematical, logical, computational, or philosophical work that resembles, supports, constrains, or challenges part of E7G-T.

The purpose is threefold.

First, to avoid false novelty. If an established field already has a precise tool, E7G-T should not rename it and claim ownership.

Second, to avoid false authority. If E7G-T resembles a formal field, resemblance does not make it a theorem inside that field.

Third, to find development paths. Some parts of E7G-T may remain practical modelling grammar. Some may become formal analogy. Some may later become formal compatibility with an existing substrate. Some may produce interface, proof-assistant, or modelling-language tools.

The governing rule is:

Neighbourhood is not identity.

To say that E7G-T is near topology, category theory, type theory, proof theory, or systems modelling is not to say that it is already any of those things.

9.2 The neighbourhood posture

The posture of this chapter is deliberately modest.

E7G-T is currently a geometry-first modelling language and practical calculus. It is not yet a new branch of mathematics with proved theorems, standard definitions, peer review, and formal adoption. It may help organise reasoning, reveal projection loss, discipline cross-domain claims, and design proof or modelling interfaces. But it must not borrow the maturity of neighbouring fields without doing the work those fields require.

The correct verbs are:

resembles;  
borrows intuition from;  
can be compared with;  
may be formalised using;  
can be tested against;  
may be implemented near;  
requires discipline from;  
should not replace.

The incorrect verbs are:

solves;  
subsumes;  
proves;  
replaces;  
unifies;  
explains everything;  
shows that established mathematics is wrong.

This distinction protects the project.

A framework that knows where it is weak can become stronger. A framework that overclaims too early becomes brittle.

9.3 Geometry and synthetic reasoning

The most obvious neighbourhood is geometry itself.

E7G-T begins from a geometry-first instinct: point, line, surface, body, duration, variation, transformation, and context are treated as primary modelling categories. It prefers placement, boundary, incidence, projection, and transformation before symbolic compression.

This resonates with synthetic geometry, diagrammatic reasoning, projective geometry, affine geometry, differential geometry, and other traditions where spatial or structural relations are not merely decorative.

But resemblance is not identity.

E7G-T’s D-orders are not standard geometric dimensions. D0 through D7 are modelling orders of extension, not literal axes in Euclidean space. The framework uses geometric language to discipline modelling, not to replace established geometry.

Therefore, when E7G-T says D2 surface-extension or D3 form-extension, it should not be read as a theorem of classical geometry. It is a modelling placement. When it says D7 context-extension, it is not claiming a standard seventh dimension. It is declaring that context, interpretation, measurement, and projection governance are being treated as higher-order modelling concerns.

The useful bridge to geometry is formal analogy at first.

Potential development path:

Identify specific E7G-T primitives that correspond to established geometric notions: boundary, incidence, projection, transformation, invariant.

Then, where possible, replace informal resemblance with formal compatibility inside a chosen geometric substrate.

Stop condition:

Do not claim geometric theoremhood for E7G-T expressions until they are formalised inside a recognised mathematical setting.

9.4 Projective geometry

Projective geometry is an important neighbour because E7G-T repeatedly speaks of projection.

In projective geometry, projection is not merely a metaphor. It is a disciplined mathematical relation involving points, lines, planes, incidence, perspective, and transformations that preserve projective structure.

E7G-T borrows a broad intuition: lower-order views may be produced from richer structures under a projection context, preserving some relations and losing others.

But E7G-T projection is wider and less formal by default.

A dashboard projection, translation projection, AI-output projection, proof-interface projection, or narrative projection is not a projective-geometric projection unless explicitly formalised as such. The word projection in E7G-T is a general modelling primitive. It must not be allowed to inherit all the precision of projective geometry automatically.

The bridge is therefore:

Projective geometry provides a mature example of disciplined projection; E7G-T generalises projection as a modelling move across domains, but this generalisation begins as analogy, not as theorem.

Useful lessons from projective geometry include:

incidence can be preserved while metric structure changes;  
apparent form depends on projection context;  
projection can make hidden relations visible;  
reconstruction from projection may be underdetermined;  
multiple views can constrain a source more strongly than one view.

These lessons support E7G-T’s practical rules. They do not prove the entire framework.

9.5 Topology

Topology is a neighbour wherever E7G-T speaks of continuity, boundary, connectedness, neighbourhood, deformation, and invariance under transformation.

Topology studies properties preserved under continuous transformation. It gives mature language for spaces, open sets, continuity, compactness, connectedness, holes, boundaries, and invariants.

E7G-T can learn from topology in at least three ways.

First, topology teaches that preservation must be tied to a transformation class. A property is not simply “invariant”; it is invariant under specified transformations.

Second, topology shows that geometry need not be metric-first. Relations of nearness, continuity, and connectedness can matter before distance or coordinates.

Third, topology warns against vague use of words such as connected, boundary, hole, and continuous. These words have precise meanings in formal contexts and looser meanings in modelling contexts.

The E7G-T relation to topology should therefore be careful.

When E7G-T says boundary, it does not automatically mean topological boundary. When it says connected, it must specify relation kind. When it says invariant, it must declare transformation class. When it says extension, it must not pretend that all extensions are topological constructions.

Potential development path:

Some E7G-T objects could be given topological interpretations in specific formal models. For example, a space of possible states, a graph of model relations, or a neighbourhood of transformations may be formalised topologically.

Stop condition:

Do not use topological language as decoration. If a topological claim is intended, define the space and topology.

9.6 Category theory

Category theory is a close formal neighbour because E7G-T cares about objects, arrows, transformations, composition, structure-preserving maps, functor-like movement between contexts, and diagrams.

Category theory is powerful because it studies relations and transformations at high abstraction. It often asks not only what objects are, but how they map, compose, and preserve structure.

This resonates strongly with E7G-T.

Projection, transformation, reconstruction, bridge, and context shift may all invite category-theoretic comparison. One might imagine categories of models, projections as morphism-like structures, functor-like translations between substrates, and diagrams representing preservation or loss.

But again, resemblance is not identity.

An E7G-T arrow is not automatically a morphism. A bridge is not automatically a functor. A projection stack is not automatically a commutative diagram. A transformation class is not automatically a category. A context shift is not automatically a natural transformation.

Category theory demands formal definitions: objects, morphisms, identity morphisms, composition, associativity, functors, naturality, limits, colimits, adjunctions, and more depending on the claim.

E7G-T should not use category theory as an aura.

The useful posture is:

Category theory may be a candidate formal substrate for parts of E7G-T, especially projection stacks, bridges, and transformation-preservation accounts. But formal compatibility requires explicit definitions and proof obligations.

Potential development path:

Start with a small category of representations or model states. Define morphisms as admissible projections or transformations. Then ask which E7G-T laws become categorical properties and which do not.

Stop condition:

Do not call a diagram categorical unless the categorical structure is actually defined.

9.7 Type theory

Type theory is a neighbour because E7G-T begins with typing before operation.

The practical law says:

No operator may be applied until the object has a declared placement or provisional placement.

This resembles the type-theoretic discipline that expressions and operations must be well-typed. Type theory prevents many errors by making the admissibility of terms explicit. It also underlies many proof assistants and programming-language semantics.

E7G-T placement is not the same as a formal type.

X : Dn @ TRi / C is a modelling placement. It says how X is being treated for the present account. A formal type in type theory has stricter syntax, inference rules, judgement forms, and computation behaviour.

Still, the analogy is valuable.

E7G-T can use type-theoretic thinking to improve its own discipline:

objects should be placed before operators are applied;  
operators should declare input and output placements;  
projection should not be confused with reconstruction;  
bridge mode should constrain claim strength;  
contexts should be explicit;  
proof-carrying claims should expose substrate.

Potential development path:

A typed E7G-T modelling language could define formal or semi-formal types for Entity, Order, TemporalRegime, Context, Projection, BridgeMode, Invariant, ReconstructionStatus, and ClaimTier. This could later support a linter or proof-assistant interface.

Stop condition:

Do not confuse E7G-T placement syntax with formal type theory unless an actual formal grammar and rules are supplied.

9.8 Proof theory and formal proof

Proof theory is a neighbour because E7G-T cares about proof as transformation, proof object, proof text, proof path, and proof interface.

Formal proof distinguishes theorem statements, assumptions, inference rules, derivations, proof objects, proof systems, and meta-theoretic properties. It does not rely merely on persuasive prose.

E7G-T can learn from proof theory in several ways.

First, proof is not just conclusion. It is path, rule, admissibility, and substrate.

Second, representation of proof is layered. A human explanation, proof sketch, formal derivation, proof term, proof assistant script, and kernel-checked object are not identical.

Third, proof strength is context-bound. A theorem is proved inside a system or under assumptions, not in a vacuum.

E7G-T’s Proof-of-Path idea belongs near this neighbourhood. It asks whether the route from object placement to claim output can be made visible, checkable, and interface-friendly.

But proof theory also sets a boundary.

E7G-T cannot call its modelling records proofs merely because they are disciplined. A FullCard may be an accountability record. It is not a formal proof unless embedded in a proof substrate and checked according to rules.

Potential development path:

Map E7G-T reasoning steps into proof-assistant-adjacent records: claim, assumptions, context, transformations, bridge mode, proof obligations, unresolved gaps, and stop conditions.

Stop condition:

Do not use proof language for interpretive or modelling claims unless proof conditions are met.

9.9 Model theory

Model theory is a neighbour because E7G-T speaks about models, interpretations, structures, languages, and satisfaction-like relations between statements and worlds or structures.

Model theory studies the relation between formal languages and structures that make sentences true or false. It is a mature field with precise machinery.

E7G-T resembles this neighbourhood when it asks:

What is the language?  
What is the substrate?  
What is the entity of concern?  
What makes the claim admissible?  
What context interprets the symbols?  
What source structure supports the view?

But E7G-T is not model theory.

Its “model” is often broader: business model, AI model output, proof model, software model, philosophical model, narrative model, or geometric representation. It uses model in the practical sense unless a formal model-theoretic substrate is declared.

The useful lesson from model theory is interpretation discipline.

A symbol does not mean by itself. It is interpreted in a structure under rules. A sentence does not float free. It belongs to a language and receives meaning through an interpretation.

This supports E7G-T’s algebra-as-rendering chapter. Equations, arrows, variables, diagrams, and formulas need substrate and interpretation.

Potential development path:

Formalise a small E7G-T language and define structures in which its placement, projection, bridge, and reconstruction claims can be interpreted.

Stop condition:

Do not call an E7G-T practical model a model-theoretic structure unless the formal language and satisfaction relation are defined.

9.10 Graphs, networks, and incidence structures

Graphs and networks are close neighbours because E7G-T repeatedly asks what is incident with what.

A graph can represent nodes and edges. A network can represent entities and relations. Incidence structures can formalise relations between points, lines, blocks, or other objects.

This is useful for E7G-T because many practical objects are relation-heavy:

proof dependencies;  
software modules;  
data pipelines;  
business processes;  
translation terminology networks;  
AI reasoning paths;  
scientific citation chains;  
bridge stacks;  
projection stacks.

Graph language can make relation visible.

But graph representation also loses structure.

An edge may hide relation type. A node may hide internal structure. A graph may preserve adjacency while losing direction, weight, causation, time, context, or transformation law. A network diagram may look explanatory even when it is only descriptive.

E7G-T can use graph methods if it respects projection-loss discipline.

A graph is often a D1/D2 rendering of a richer relational structure. It may support navigation, dependency inspection, and local reasoning. It should not automatically become the source.

Potential development path:

Represent MiniCards or FullCards as typed graphs: entities, projections, transformations, contexts, bridge modes, invariants, losses, and stop conditions.

Stop condition:

Do not treat graph connectedness as causal, semantic, or formal relation unless relation type is declared.

9.11 Diagrammatic reasoning

Diagrammatic reasoning is a neighbour because E7G-T is geometry-first and representation-aware.

Diagrams can carry structure that prose and algebra hide. They can show incidence, containment, direction, dependency, hierarchy, projection, transformation, feedback, and boundary. They can support discovery and explanation.

But diagrams are projections.

A diagram may preserve topology but distort metric. It may preserve dependency but distort chronology. It may preserve hierarchy but hide feedback. It may preserve conceptual relation but hide formal proof. It may make a weak analogy look strong by visual neatness.

E7G-T can treat diagrams as first-class modelling artefacts while refusing to overread them.

A diagram should declare:

what entities its marks represent;  
what relation types its lines represent;  
what spatial placement means and does not mean;  
what is intentionally omitted;  
what use is admissible;  
whether the diagram is explanatory, formal, pedagogical, speculative, or operational.

Potential development path:

Develop E7G-T diagram standards for projection stacks, bridge modes, loss accounting, and reconstruction statuses.

Stop condition:

Do not let visual clarity stand in for formal, empirical, or operational support.

9.12 Systems theory and cybernetics

Systems theory and cybernetics are neighbours because E7G-T often models entities as structured wholes with boundaries, inputs, outputs, feedback, control, context, and levels.

These traditions provide mature ways to think about systems, environment, feedback loops, regulation, emergence, and observation. They are especially relevant to business, software, biology, cognition, AI, and organisation.

E7G-T resonates with systems thinking when it asks:

What is the system boundary?  
What is inside or outside?  
What is observed?  
What feedback loop is active?  
What projection gives the dashboard or record?  
What level of description is being used?  
What transformation governs state change?

But E7G-T should not simply relabel systems theory.

Its distinctive emphasis is the extension/projection account, order placement, temporal regime, bridge discipline, and reconstruction stop rule. These may complement systems thinking, not replace it.

Potential development path:

Use E7G-T as a systems-modelling checklist: boundary, temporal regime, slice, projection loss, feedback, invariant, bridge mode, next move.

Stop condition:

Do not treat “system” as a magic word. Declare boundary, environment, feedback, and observer context.

9.13 Formal verification and program semantics

Formal verification and program semantics are neighbours because E7G-T cares about computation, proof, transformation, invariant, and operational behaviour.

Formal verification asks whether a system satisfies a specification under a formal model. Program semantics studies the meaning of programs: how code relates to computation, state, effects, types, and behaviour.

This neighbourhood can discipline E7G-T in software contexts.

A code expression is not simply a mathematical expression. It belongs to a language, type system, runtime, memory model, compiler, libraries, and environment. A test is a projection of behaviour under selected conditions. A log is a projection of runtime events. A type check preserves some properties and loses others. A formal proof preserves properties under a model that may or may not match production reality.

E7G-T can help by making these projection layers explicit.

But it must not replace verification.

If correctness is required, formal specification, model checking, theorem proving, testing, review, and operational monitoring remain necessary.

Potential development path:

Build an E7G-T linter for software reasoning: distinguish code text, specification, runtime, logs, tests, proofs, deployment, and user-visible behaviour.

Stop condition:

Do not call a system verified because its E7G-T account is coherent. Verification requires the relevant formal or empirical method.

9.14 Measurement theory and empirical modelling

Measurement theory is a neighbour because E7G-T often treats measurement as projection from source to record.

In empirical work, measurement is not merely reading off reality. It involves operational definitions, instruments, calibration, units, protocols, error, uncertainty, sampling, and interpretation.

This resonates with E7G-T’s insistence that empirical-facing claims require bridge discipline.

A measurement value is a lower-order record produced under context. It may preserve a property of the system. It may lose state richness, causal explanation, hidden variables, uncertainty, or context. It may distort through instrument error, sampling bias, or protocol mismatch.

E7G-T can provide a geometric vocabulary for this: source, measurement context, projection, preserved signal, loss, reconstruction status, empirical bridge.

But measurement theory and domain science set the standards.

E7G-T cannot decide by itself whether a measurement is valid. It can ask whether the validity conditions have been declared.

Potential development path:

For empirical applications, attach E7G-T FullCards to standard measurement records: entity, observable, instrument, protocol, units, uncertainty, model, evidence path, falsification or revision condition.

Stop condition:

Do not use measurement-as-projection language to bypass empirical validation.

9.15 Statistics and data science

Statistics and data science are neighbours because E7G-T often works with dashboards, projections, samples, slices, models, and reconstruction from data.

Statistics already has mature tools for uncertainty, inference, sampling, estimation, model comparison, error, correlation, causation, and prediction. Data science has practical pipelines for collecting, transforming, modelling, evaluating, and deploying data products.

E7G-T should not pretend to replace these tools.

Its contribution is pre-statistical and meta-modelling: it asks what the data view is, what source it projects, what slice and boundary are active, what loss occurs, whether reconstruction is justified, and what bridge mode supports the claim.

This helps prevent common errors:

chart equals explanation;  
correlation equals causation;  
sample equals population;  
model output equals truth;  
forecast equals future;  
metric equals reality;  
clean dashboard equals clean data.

Potential development path:

Use E7G-T cards as metadata around data products: source boundary, slice, projection chain, transformations, invariants, uncertainty, reconstruction status, decision use, and stop conditions.

Stop condition:

When statistical inference is needed, use statistics. E7G-T can frame the problem, not replace inference.

9.16 Semantics, linguistics, and translation studies

Semantics, linguistics, and translation studies are neighbours because E7G-T treats language as projection, rendering, transformation, and invariant management.

Words, sentences, texts, and translations do not simply transfer meaning as a physical object moves from one place to another. They operate through context, grammar, reference, pragmatics, genre, audience, legal force, cultural convention, and purpose.

Translation especially shows controlled projection.

A target text preserves selected invariants from the source under a declared use. It may preserve legal force, information, tone, rhythm, terminology, ambiguity, formatting, or reader function. It cannot preserve all of these equally in every case.

This resonates with E7G-T’s practical formula:

Translation \= target-language projection of source text under purpose context, preserving selected invariants and transforming or losing others.

But translation studies already has its own mature debates and concepts. E7G-T should not flatten them.

Potential development path:

Develop an E7G-T translation QA card: source boundary, target boundary, purpose, invariant hierarchy, tolerated loss, distortion risk, recipient use, certification or reliance level.

Stop condition:

Do not treat translation adequacy as absolute. It is use-relative and invariant-specific.

9.17 Philosophy of mathematics

Philosophy of mathematics is a neighbour because E7G-T asks what mathematics is doing when it represents, compresses, proves, renders, constructs, and abstracts.

The framework is geometry-first, but it does not need to claim that all mathematics is literally geometry. A safer claim is that many mathematical practices can be made clearer by asking for geometric-style accountability: object, boundary, relation, transformation, projection, invariant, and context.

This places E7G-T near debates about formalism, structuralism, intuition, diagrammatic reasoning, mathematical practice, representation, abstraction, and proof.

E7G-T’s public philosophical stance should remain modest:

It is not a final philosophy of mathematics.

It is a geometry-first modelling lens for inspecting how mathematical objects and presentations are built, rendered, transformed, and relied upon.

Potential development path:

Write focused essays on under-geometrised layers: algebra as rendering, proof as transformation path, diagram as controlled projection, proof assistant as layered interface.

Stop condition:

Do not make metaphysical claims about mathematical reality unless the work explicitly enters philosophy and marks the claim as such.

9.18 Cognitive science and perception

Cognitive science and perception are neighbours because E7G-T often speaks about local views, context-conditioned appearance, rendering, and reconstruction from partial data.

Human cognition operates through attention, perception, memory, categorisation, inference, narrative, embodiment, and context. It often reconstructs larger structures from partial cues. It also projects simplified models onto complex reality.

This resonates with E7G-T’s view/source discipline.

But cognitive science is empirical. E7G-T cannot make claims about cognition merely by analogy.

The framework may be useful as a modelling language for cognitive tasks:

What is the perceived view?  
What source is inferred?  
What context conditions perception?  
What is preserved?  
What is lost?  
What reconstruction errors are possible?  
What bridge mode supports the claim?

Potential development path:

Use E7G-T as a conceptual scaffold for studying explanation, bias, attention, and reconstruction errors, but bridge to actual cognitive science only through empirical literature and methods.

Stop condition:

Do not treat introspective resonance as cognitive science.

9.19 Information theory and compression

Information theory is a neighbour because E7G-T repeatedly speaks about compression, rendering, loss, and reconstruction.

A projection can be understood informally as a compression or transformation that preserves some information and discards or distorts other information. Summaries, diagrams, measurements, encodings, and dashboards all involve information selection.

This creates a tempting bridge.

However, information theory is mathematically precise. It defines quantities, channels, entropy, coding, noise, capacity, and related concepts in specific ways. E7G-T’s use of compression is usually qualitative unless formalised.

The safe posture is:

Information theory provides formal tools that may later strengthen E7G-T projection-loss accounts, but E7G-T’s current compression language is modelling language unless a formal information-theoretic substrate is declared.

Potential development path:

Investigate whether projection loss can be partially formalised using information measures in constrained settings: data summaries, model compression, dimensionality reduction, encoding, and reconstruction.

Stop condition:

Do not use information-theoretic terms technically without definitions and measures.

9.20 Dimensionality reduction and data representation

Dimensionality reduction is a neighbour because E7G-T often describes descent from richer structure to lower-order representation.

In data science, dimensionality reduction methods map high-dimensional data into lower-dimensional representations for analysis, visualisation, compression, or modelling. These methods can preserve some structure while losing other structure.

This strongly resembles E7G-T projection language.

But the bridge must be precise.

A mathematical dimensionality reduction method has data, metric or similarity assumptions, optimisation criteria, loss functions, and validation concerns. E7G-T’s D-orders are not automatically data dimensions. D5, D6, and D7 are modelling orders, not numerical feature counts.

The useful analogy is:

Dimensionality reduction provides concrete examples of projection with preservation/loss trade-offs. E7G-T generalises the caution: lower-order representation must declare what it preserves and what it loses.

Potential development path:

Use E7G-T vocabulary to explain dimensionality reduction to non-specialists, and use formal dimensionality reduction as a technical bridge for specific data applications.

Stop condition:

Do not claim Dn → Dk projection is the same as a specific dimensionality-reduction algorithm unless formally mapped.

9.21 Holography and physical theories

Holography is a neighbourhood only under strict bridge discipline.

The word holographic has technical meanings in physics and optics, and looser meanings in philosophy, metaphor, spirituality, and systems thinking. E7G-T uses holographic reading primarily as disciplined projection language: lower-order views may preserve selected structure from richer sources under projection context.

This does not make E7G-T a physical holographic theory.

If E7G-T is compared with physical holography, simulation hypotheses, quantum information, or holographic universe proposals, the bridge mode must be declared. The default is philosophical interpretation or formal analogy. Empirical-testable claims require formal physics, measurement map, predictions or constraints, rival explanations, and evidence.

The safe statement is:

E7G-T can provide a modelling grammar for discussing holographic intuitions, but it does not establish physical holography.

Potential development path:

Build a separate Reality Bridge Atlas that carefully separates contemplative, philosophical, formal, and empirical uses.

Stop condition:

Do not use the word holographic to smuggle physics into a modelling framework.

9.22 Category of claim strength

One of the most important formal neighbourhoods is not a field but a discipline of claim strength.

E7G-T needs a stable ladder:

intuition;  
metaphor;  
contemplative what-if;  
philosophical interpretation;  
formal analogy;  
formal compatibility;  
formal proof;  
empirical-testable proposal;  
empirically supported model;  
validated operational method.

These are not interchangeable.

Many weak projects fail because they move from intuition to universal conclusion too quickly. E7G-T’s bridge discipline exists to prevent that.

A claim may be useful as a metaphor. It may be interesting as philosophical interpretation. It may be promising as formal analogy. It may later be formalised. It may or may not become empirical.

The maturity ladder should be visible whenever E7G-T touches established fields.

A formal neighbourhood map should therefore include not only “what field is nearby?” but “what claim strength is currently justified?”

The practical rule is:

Nearness to a formal field does not raise claim strength by itself.

9.23 The formal neighbourhood card

A Formal Neighbourhood Card can be used whenever E7G-T is compared with an established field.

Template:

FormalNeighbourhoodCard:  
  field: ""  
  E7GT-element: ""  
  resemblance: ""  
  established-tool: ""  
  current-bridge-mode: "intuition | metaphor | formal-analogy | formal-compatibility | proof | empirical-testable"  
  what-E7GT-can-learn: \[\]  
  what-E7GT-must-not-claim: \[\]  
  possible-formalisation-path: ""  
  stop-condition: ""

Example:

FormalNeighbourhoodCard:  
  field: "Category theory"  
  E7GT-element: "Projection stacks and bridge maps"  
  resemblance: "Objects and arrow-like transformations between structured contexts."  
  established-tool: "Categories, functors, natural transformations, diagrams."  
  current-bridge-mode: "formal-analogy"  
  what-E7GT-can-learn:  
    \- "composition discipline"  
    \- "diagrammatic constraints"  
    \- "structure preservation"  
  what-E7GT-must-not-claim:  
    \- "E7G-T arrows are morphisms without definition"  
    \- "bridge maps are functors without proof"  
  possible-formalisation-path: "Define a small category of model views and admissible projections."  
  stop-condition: "No categorical claim until objects, morphisms, identities, and composition are defined."

This card keeps the relationship honest.

9.24 Formalisation paths

Several formalisation paths are possible, but they should be staged.

Path 1: Controlled vocabulary.

Define the terms: EntityOfConcern, Order, TemporalRegime, Context, Boundary, Incidence, Projection, Preservation, Loss, Transformation, Invariant, ReconstructionStatus, BridgeMode, ClaimTier, StopCondition.

Path 2: Typed modelling schema.

Create a machine-readable schema for OneLine, MiniCard, and FullCard records. This can support linting, comparison, retrieval, and review.

Path 3: Diagram grammar.

Define visual notation for extension, projection, bridge, slice, reconstruction, and stop. This may support teaching and interface design.

Path 4: Graph model.

Represent E7G-T records as typed graphs. Nodes could be entities, contexts, views, sources, invariants, bridge modes, and losses. Edges could be projection, transformation, incidence, reconstruction, or bridge relation.

Path 5: Type-theoretic prototype.

Define a toy formal language where E7G-T placements and operations become typed terms. Use it to test whether the basic laws can be encoded.

Path 6: Proof-assistant experiment.

Implement a minimal E7G-T linter or proof-of-path interface around a proof assistant environment. The goal is not to prove E7G-T, but to see whether the modelling language improves navigation and accountability.

Path 7: Domain bridge atlas.

Create disciplined bridge records for mathematics, proof assistants, software, AI, data science, physics, cognition, business, translation, and narrative.

Each path has a different maturity target. They should not be collapsed.

9.25 The anti-replacement rule

The anti-replacement rule is simple:

E7G-T does not replace a mature formal or empirical discipline where that discipline already has stronger tools.

It may serve as:

a pre-formal placement discipline;  
a projection-loss account;  
a bridge-mode classifier;  
a modelling-language layer;  
a teaching scaffold;  
a linter for overclaims;  
a proof-interface design aid;  
a cross-domain translation layer;  
a source-return reminder;  
a research-programme organiser.

It should not pretend to be:

a replacement for topology;  
a replacement for category theory;  
a replacement for proof theory;  
a replacement for statistics;  
a replacement for empirical physics;  
a replacement for translation theory;  
a replacement for formal verification;  
a replacement for domain expertise.

The anti-replacement rule is not humility for style. It is structural safety.

A modelling language becomes more useful when it sends the user to the right mature tool at the right moment.

9.26 From formal neighbourhoods to proof and Proof-of-Path

This chapter has located E7G-T near several formal and methodological traditions. It has also insisted that nearness is not identity and that formal authority must be earned.

The next chapter narrows the focus to proof.

Proof is where many E7G-T concerns meet: object, boundary, transformation, invariant, formal substrate, projection, explanation, interface, reconstruction, and reliance. It is also where the framework may have a practical contribution: not as a new proof system, but as a Proof-of-Path discipline and proof-assistant interface layer.

Chapter 10 therefore turns to Proof, Proof Assistants, and Proof-of-Path.

Its question is:

How can E7G-T make the path from claim to admissible proof, explanation, or stop condition more visible?

Chapter 10 — Proof, Proof Assistants, and Proof-of-Path

10.1 Why proof is the stress test

Proof is one of the best stress tests for E7G-T because proof exposes nearly every distinction the framework has introduced.

A proof has an entity of concern. It has a boundary. It has assumptions. It has a formal or informal substrate. It moves through transformations. It preserves validity under rules. It may be rendered as prose, diagram, algebra, proof script, proof object, or interface state. It may be discovered in time, written in sequence, checked by a kernel, explained to a reader, and published as a finished artefact.

The word proof therefore hides many layers.

A proof can mean:

a persuasive explanation;  
a formal derivation;  
a sequence of algebraic transformations;  
a proof assistant script;  
a proof term;  
a kernel-checked object;  
a published argument;  
a diagrammatic construction;  
a verification certificate;  
a social acceptance by a mathematical community.

These are related, but they are not identical.

E7G-T does not replace proof. It asks which layer is being called proof and what path connects that layer to the claim.

The central question of this chapter is:

How can the path from claim to admissible proof, explanation, or stop condition be made visible?

This is the Proof-of-Path question.

10.2 Proof as more than conclusion

A conclusion is not a proof.

A theorem statement is not a proof.

A diagram is not a proof by itself.

A convincing paragraph is not always a proof.

A proof assistant script is not the same thing as a human explanation.

A kernel-checked proof object is not the same thing as a readable argument.

A published proof is not necessarily the same thing as a formally verified proof.

Each may be useful. Each may be strong in its proper context. But none should silently inherit the strength of the others.

E7G-T treats proof as a path of admissible transformations from a bounded claim under a declared substrate to an accepted status.

In compact form:

Claim \+ Context \+ Assumptions \+ Rules \+ Transformations ⇒ AcceptedStatus

But even this is underdeclared unless the substrate is known.

In informal mathematics, the substrate may include accepted definitions, prior theorems, conventions, diagrams, expert judgement, and community norms. In a proof assistant, the substrate includes kernel, logic, library, definitions, tactics, elaboration, and proof object. In legal or empirical contexts, the word proof has different standards again.

This chapter concerns mathematical and formal proof, but the layer discipline can help in other domains too.

The repair is:

Do not ask only whether the conclusion is true. Ask what path supports it and under what substrate.

10.3 The proof stack

A proof stack is the layered structure through which a claim becomes accepted, explained, checked, or used.

A typical formal-mathematical proof stack may include:

problem intuition;  
definitions;  
claim statement;  
assumptions;  
notation;  
proof strategy;  
lemmas;  
transformations;  
diagrams;  
algebraic derivations;  
formal language;  
proof script;  
elaboration;  
proof object;  
kernel checking;  
human explanation;  
publication;  
reuse.

Not every proof uses all layers. But every proof relies on some stack.

The practical E7G-T move is to identify which layer is visible and which layers are hidden.

A textbook proof may show definitions, strategy, and transformations but hide formal proof-object detail.

A proof assistant may show goals and script but hide elaborated terms unless inspected.

A diagram may show incidence but hide formal justification.

A published theorem may show claim and proof but hide discovery path.

A citation may show only the accepted result and hide almost everything else.

Proof stack discipline asks:

What layer am I seeing?  
What layer carries validity?  
What layer carries explanation?  
What layer carries reuse?  
What layer requires source return?

This is not pedantry. It prevents misusing proof artefacts.

10.4 Proof text as projection

A proof text is a projection.

It renders proof work into readable language, symbols, diagrams, and ordered steps. It may preserve strategy, assumptions, core transformations, and enough detail for a competent reader. It may lose search path, failed attempts, formal expansion, implicit lemmas, library dependencies, type details, and machine-checkable structure.

This is normal. Proof texts are designed for human use.

But proof text should not be overread.

A readable proof may be incomplete for formal verification. A formal proof may be unreadable without explanation. A short proof may rely on heavy background. A diagrammatic proof may be persuasive but need formal repair. A proof sketch may convey strategy without discharging all obligations.

The disciplined statement is:

ProofText : D2/D1 @ TR1/TR6 / ReaderMathematicalContext projects ProofWork : D6 @ TR6 / MathematicalSubstrate; preserves selected transformations and explanation; loses formal expansion and hidden obligations unless supplied.

The practical rule is:

When proof text supports reliance, ask what obligations are hidden by prose.

Common hidden obligations include:

definitions;  
domain restrictions;  
nonzero conditions;  
choice of branch;  
existence claims;  
uniqueness claims;  
continuity assumptions;  
implicit lemmas;  
side conditions;  
case distinctions;  
use of prior theorems;  
translation from diagram to formal statement.

A proof text is strong when it makes enough of these visible for its intended context.

10.5 Proof diagram as projection

A proof diagram may be very powerful.

It can show incidence, symmetry, containment, commutation, dependency, transformation, or construction more directly than prose. It can guide discovery and explanation. In some formal traditions, diagrams can even participate in proof under controlled rules.

But a diagram is still a projection unless its formal role is declared.

A diagram may preserve relation but lose metric. It may preserve shape but lose generality. It may show one case but not all cases. It may imply symmetry that the formal object lacks. It may hide assumptions in visual placement. It may make a noncommuting square look commutative by drawing it neatly.

The E7G-T discipline is:

What does the diagram preserve?  
What does it lose?  
What formal substrate, if any, gives it proof status?  
What must be translated into prose or formal notation?

A proof diagram may be used as:

intuition;  
construction guide;  
explanatory projection;  
formal diagram inside a defined diagrammatic calculus;  
proof assistant visualisation;  
dependency graph;  
projection stack map.

These roles are different.

The repair is:

Do not let visual force exceed declared formal force.

10.6 Proof state

A proof state is a local configuration in an ongoing proof process.

In a proof assistant, the proof state may include current goals, hypotheses, context, metavariables, constraints, and expected terms. It is a slice of proof construction under local-time.

Placement:

ProofState : D2/D6 @ TR1 / ProofAssistantContext

A proof state is not the whole proof. It is a local slice of proof work. It may show what remains to be proved, what assumptions are available, and what transformations are currently admissible. It may hide search history, tactic expansion, global library dependencies, and final proof object.

This is exactly the kind of object E7G-T can clarify.

The proof state has:

boundary: current goals, hypotheses, local context, imports, and metavariables;  
incidence: relation to previous states, tactics, theorem statement, and formal environment;  
slice selector: current cursor, tactic position, or proof construction stage;  
projection: interface rendering of formal obligations;  
reconstruction status: traceable if state history and environment are available.

A useful proof interface should help the user see not only the current goal, but also where that goal came from and what kind of move is admissible next.

10.7 Proof object

A proof object is a formal object that witnesses the derivability or construction of a theorem inside a formal substrate.

Placement:

ProofObject : D6 @ TR6 / FormalKernelContext

The proof object is not merely text. It is a structured formal entity accepted by rules. Its authority comes from the substrate: logic, definitions, kernel, type system, and accepted dependencies.

A proof object may preserve formal validity while losing human readability.

This loss is not a defect. It is a trade-off. The object carries machine-checkable structure. Human explanation may need another projection.

E7G-T separates:

ProofObject : formal validity carrier;  
ProofScript : human-editable construction path;  
ProofState : local construction slice;  
ProofText : reader-facing explanation;  
ProofDiagram : visual projection;  
ProofPublication : shared-time record;  
ProofExplanation : pedagogical projection.

A mature proof environment should not collapse these.

The repair is:

Kernel acceptance gives formal status under a declared substrate, not automatic explanatory adequacy.

10.8 Proof script

A proof script is a textual or symbolic sequence used to construct or guide a proof in a proof assistant.

Placement:

ProofScript : D1/D2 @ TR1/TR6 / ProofAssistantLanguageContext

The script may preserve a path of tactics or terms. It may lose elaborated structure, implicit arguments, generated obligations, automation internals, and proof object detail.

A short script can hide enormous structure. A single tactic may solve many goals using library search, rewriting, simplification, automation, or decision procedures. This is powerful, but it creates projection loss.

The user sees:

a line of script;  
a changed proof state;  
a success or error message.

The system may perform:

parsing;  
elaboration;  
typeclass inference;  
rewriting;  
unification;  
search;  
normalisation;  
proof-term construction;  
kernel checking.

A Proof-of-Path interface should make selected hidden layers inspectable when needed.

The repair is:

A proof script is a path projection, not the whole formal proof object.

10.9 Tactic as transformation

A tactic is a transformation of proof state.

In E7G-T notation:

τ\_tactic(ProofStateA) \= ProofStateB

or, when it closes the goal:

τ\_tactic(ProofStateA) ⇒ ClosedGoal

A tactic is admissible only under the proof assistant’s rules and environment. It may preserve theorem validity if the kernel later accepts the constructed proof object. It may also introduce subgoals, rewrite expressions, apply lemmas, split cases, simplify hypotheses, or call automation.

The E7G-T transformation account asks:

What was the input proof state?  
What tactic was applied?  
What rule-space allowed it?  
What proof obligations were preserved, transformed, created, or discharged?  
What hidden automation occurred?  
What proof object fragment was produced?  
What did the user learn from the move?

This is useful for teaching and debugging.

Many proof-assistant beginners do not only need the next tactic. They need to understand what kind of transformation the tactic performed. E7G-T can help classify tactics as projection, extension, boundary introduction, incidence creation, reconstruction, transformation, or stop.

For example:

intro may change a theorem boundary by moving an implication premise into local context.

apply may create incidence between current goal and a known theorem.

rw may transform an expression under equality.

cases may slice or branch the proof state.

exact may close a goal by supplying a term.

simp may perform a stack of transformations under simplification rules.

This vocabulary can support proof pedagogy without replacing the proof assistant’s own semantics.

10.10 Proof-of-Path

Proof-of-Path is not a new proof system.

It is an accountability layer showing how a claim moved from initial placement to accepted, rejected, downgraded, or stopped status.

In E7G-T, a Proof-of-Path record asks:

What was the claim?  
What was the entity of concern?  
What substrate was declared?  
What boundary and assumptions were used?  
What transformations were applied?  
What obligations appeared?  
What was preserved?  
What was lost or hidden by projections?  
What bridge mode, if any, was used?  
What accepted the result?  
What remains unresolved?  
What is the admissible use of the result?

For formal proof, Proof-of-Path may record the route from theorem statement through proof states, tactics, proof object, and kernel acceptance.

For informal proof, it may record definitions, lemmas, transformations, diagrams, and gaps.

For speculative modelling, it may record where proof stops and interpretation begins.

The key distinction is:

Proof proves within a substrate. Proof-of-Path shows the route taken and the status of each step.

A Proof-of-Path can support trust, pedagogy, review, debugging, collaboration, and auditability. It does not replace proof validity.

10.11 Proof-of-Path record

A minimal Proof-of-Path record may have the following structure:

ProofOfPathCard:  
  claim: ""  
  entityOfConcern: ""  
  substrate: "informal | formal | proof-assistant | empirical | mixed"  
  placement: "X : Dn @ TRi / C"  
  boundary: ""  
  assumptions: \[\]  
  definitions: \[\]  
  knownDependencies: \[\]  
  pathSteps:  
    \- step: 1  
      visibleMove: ""  
      moveType: "place | bound | incide | extend | slice | project | transform | reconstruct | bridge | stop"  
      inputState: ""  
      outputState: ""  
      preserved: \[\]  
      obligationsCreated: \[\]  
      obligationsDischarged: \[\]  
      hiddenStructure: \[\]  
  proofStatus: "sketch | plausible | formalised | kernel-checked | blocked | invalid | unknown"  
  explanationStatus: "none | partial | adequate-for-reader | pedagogical | expert-only"  
  reconstructionStatus: "traceable | partially-traceable | underdetermined | not-needed"  
  bridgeMode: "none | formal-analogy | formal-compatibility | empirical-testable | other"  
  admissibleUse: ""  
  stopCondition: ""  
  nextMove: ""

This record is intentionally more detailed than a proof text. It is not always needed. It becomes useful when proof work must be taught, reviewed, debugged, ported, translated, formalised, or trusted by people who did not produce it.

10.12 Proof layers and temporal regimes

Proof involves several temporal regimes.

Proof discovery occurs under TR1 and often TR4. It is local, iterative, and sometimes cyclic.

Proof construction occurs under TR1 as the writer or system creates steps.

Proof search may involve TR3 because several possible paths branch.

Proof assistant development may involve TR5 because libraries, tactics, and definitions evolve over version history.

Proof object acceptance may be treated under TR6 once kernel-checked relative to a substrate.

Proof publication occurs under TR2 as a shared-time record.

Proof explanation is experienced under TR1 by a reader.

These regimes should not be collapsed.

A theorem may be completed under a formal context while a student still struggles locally to understand it. A proof may be discovered historically but treated timelessly once formalised. A proof assistant library may change over time, affecting reproducibility. A proof script may be stable in one version and break in another.

The practical rule is:

Separate proof discovery, proof construction, proof object, proof explanation, proof publication, and proof reuse.

10.13 Proof and reconstruction

Proof often requires reconstruction.

A reader reconstructs the argument from proof text. A proof assistant reconstructs terms from script. A reviewer reconstructs missing steps. A student reconstructs why a tactic worked. A researcher reconstructs an old proof in a new formal substrate. A machine reconstructs a proof object from elaborated code.

Reconstruction can fail.

The proof text may omit a needed lemma. The diagram may hide a false assumption. The script may depend on a library version. The automation may be opaque. The informal argument may not survive formalisation. The theorem statement may be slightly different from the claimed result.

E7G-T’s reconstruction statuses apply:

source known;  
source traceable;  
compatible;  
constrained;  
bounded rival set;  
unique under assumptions;  
reconstructed;  
underdetermined;  
blocked.

In proof work, the important warning is:

A proof sketch compatible with a theorem is not yet a proof of the theorem.

A proof assistant script that compiles may reconstruct a formal proof object, but the human explanation may still be missing.

10.14 Proof and bridge modes

Proof often crosses contexts.

An informal idea may be bridged to formal mathematics.

A diagram may be bridged to algebra.

A paper proof may be bridged to a proof assistant.

A proof assistant theorem may be bridged to software verification.

A mathematical result may be bridged to empirical modelling.

Each bridge needs a mode.

Informal-to-formal may be formalisation.

Diagram-to-proof may be formal analogy unless the diagrammatic rules are defined.

Proof-assistant-to-software may be formal compatibility only if the specification matches the implementation model.

Mathematical-to-empirical requires empirical-testable bridge if empirical claims are made.

The proof itself may be strong inside its substrate, while the bridge to application remains weak.

This is crucial.

A formally proved theorem does not automatically validate a physical model, business decision, software implementation, or philosophical conclusion. It validates a formal claim under a formal substrate. Application requires bridge discipline.

The repair is:

Proof strength does not automatically cross substrates.

10.15 Proof assistant as projection interface

A proof assistant is not only a prover. It is also an interface between human reasoning and formal substrate.

It projects formal obligations into visible goals. It projects tactics into proof-state changes. It projects kernel status into success or error. It projects library structure into searchable theorems. It projects formal objects into readable terms and messages.

The interface is therefore a D7 context layer over D6 formal rule-space and D2/D1 textual surfaces.

Placement:

ProofAssistantInterface : D7/D2 @ TR1 / FormalInteractionContext

The interface preserves selected formal structure for user action. It loses or hides other structure for usability.

A good interface manages this projection honestly.

It should help users know:

what the current goal is;  
what assumptions are available;  
what move types are admissible;  
what hidden obligations exist;  
what tactic did;  
what proof object or term was generated;  
what dependency was used;  
what changed after the step;  
what remains unproved;  
what is kernel-checked;  
what is merely suggested.

E7G-T’s practical contribution may lie here: not in replacing proof assistants, but in designing better explanatory surfaces around them.

10.16 A Lean-facing interpretation

Lean is one example of a modern proof assistant ecosystem. The details of any specific Lean version belong to Lean documentation and practice, not to E7G-T. But E7G-T can provide a modelling lens for Lean-like work.

A Lean theorem statement can be placed as a formal claim under a type-theoretic substrate.

A tactic state can be treated as a local slice of proof construction.

A tactic can be treated as a transformation of proof state.

An error message can be treated as a projected view of failed elaboration, type mismatch, missing instance, unsolved goal, or syntax issue.

A proof term can be treated as a formal object.

A human explanation can be treated as a projection of proof strategy for reader understanding.

A library import can be treated as an incidence relation with dependency context.

This gives practical questions for a Lean-facing interface:

What is the entity of concern: theorem, term, proof state, tactic, error, dependency, or explanation?

What is the current proof-state boundary?

What transformation did the tactic attempt?

What obligation did it create or discharge?

What hidden structure did elaboration supply?

What does kernel acceptance prove and what does it not prove?

What explanation is still needed for a human?

The point is not to teach Lean in this chapter. The point is to show how E7G-T could support proof-assistant usability.

10.17 Proof pedagogy

Proof learning often fails because students see the projection but not the path.

They see a polished proof text, but not the search process. They see an algebraic transformation, but not the admissibility condition. They see a diagram, but not the hidden assumption. They see a proof assistant tactic, but not the state transformation. They see the final theorem, but not the boundary of use.

E7G-T can support proof pedagogy by making path layers explicit.

For each proof step, ask:

What is the current object?  
What is its boundary?  
What move is being made?  
What relation or transformation justifies the move?  
What invariant is preserved?  
What obligation remains?  
What did the step hide?  
What would make the step invalid?

This turns proof reading into active modelling.

A teaching proof could include margin labels:

Place: define the object.

Bound: state the domain.

Incide: connect to known lemma.

Transform: rewrite under equality.

Slice: split cases.

Extend: generalise from local construction.

Project: draw diagram or introduce notation.

Reconstruct: infer source from condition.

Bridge: connect informal intuition to formal step.

Stop: note where analogy ends.

These labels should be used sparingly. Their purpose is not to decorate every proof, but to reveal hidden moves when learners need them.

10.18 Proof debugging

Proof debugging is another natural application.

When a proof attempt fails, the failure may occur at several layers:

claim boundary is wrong;  
ndefinition mismatch;  
missing assumption;  
wrong domain;  
invalid transformation;  
hidden side condition;  
projection overread from diagram;  
incorrect reconstruction from example;  
bridge from intuition to formal statement failed;  
tactic does not apply;  
elaboration fails;  
library dependency changed;  
proof object cannot be constructed.
