Source: not shown.

Bridge mode: missing.

Projection risk: answer fluency may hide evidence gap.

Next move: source search and rival explanation review.

Blocked overread: AI fluency is not proof.

Pilot test:

Compare ordinary AI review with E7G-T-linted review across factual, legal, technical, scientific, and business answers.

Outcome:

Fewer unsupported strong claims, clearer source-return prompts, better downgrade language.

13.9 Pilot 2: dashboard interpretation

A dashboard pilot fits E7G-T well because dashboards are clear projections.

The problem is common: a chart or KPI tile is treated as direct business reality.

An E7G-T dashboard checklist could ask:

What is the metric?  
What source records feed it?  
What time window is active?  
What filters are applied?  
What aggregation rule is used?  
What does the metric preserve?  
What does it lose?  
What rival explanations exist?  
What decision is being considered?  
Is source return required?

Pilot test:

Use the checklist in a business review or simulated business case. Compare decisions made with and without projection-loss accounting.

Outcome:

Better distinction between signal, cause, source, slice, forecast, and decision.

This pilot may be especially useful because it speaks to practical business users without requiring advanced mathematics.

13.10 Pilot 3: translation QA card

Translation is another strong pilot because translation is visibly projection-like but professionally constrained.

An E7G-T translation QA card could include:

source boundary;  
target boundary;  
purpose;  
recipient;  
legal or administrative use;  
invariant hierarchy;  
terminology constraints;  
format constraints;  
allowed loss;  
non-allowed loss;  
review status;  
uncertainty markings;  
certification requirements;  
stop condition.

Example invariant hierarchy:

identity data;  
dates;  
official document structure;  
legal force;  
terminology;  
layout cues;  
style;  
readability.

Pilot test:

Use the card on a small set of administrative or legal translations and compare reviewer comments, defect rates, and rework clarity.

Outcome:

More consistent QA and clearer explanation of translation choices.

13.11 Pilot 4: proof-assistant learning

Proof assistants are powerful but difficult for learners.

A proof-assistant pilot could use E7G-T labels to annotate proof states and tactic moves:

Place;  
Bound;  
Incide;  
Transform;  
Slice;  
Extend;  
Project;  
Reconstruct;  
Bridge;  
Stop.

The goal is not to replace Lean, Coq, Isabelle, or other tools. The goal is to help learners understand what kind of move they are making.

Pilot test:

Take a beginner proof-assistant exercise and annotate each step with E7G-T move labels, hidden obligations, and proof-state changes.

Compare learner understanding before and after.

Outcome:

Better debugging language, improved recognition of assumptions, fewer tactic-as-magic misunderstandings.

This pilot links directly to Proof-of-Path.

13.12 Pilot 5: software bug triage

Software bug triage often suffers from projection confusion.

A user report is not the bug.

A screenshot is not the cause.

A log is not the whole runtime.

A failing test is a slice.

A stack trace is a projection of execution path.

A patch is a transformation that may preserve or break invariants.

An E7G-T bug card could include:

visible symptom;  
reported context;  
source artefacts;  
runtime environment;  
reproduction path;  
projection views: log, screenshot, test, trace;  
preserved information;  
missing information;  
rival causes;  
next diagnostic move;  
stop condition.

Pilot test:

Use the card for real or simulated bugs and compare triage quality, time-to-root-cause, and false cause assignments.

Outcome:

Cleaner separation of symptom, trace, source, cause, and fix.

13.13 Pilot 6: research-note Proof-of-Path

Research notes often contain promising but underdeclared claims.

An E7G-T Proof-of-Path note can help separate conjecture, analogy, proof, source, bridge, and next obligation.

A minimal research note could include:

claim;  
intuition;  
source structure;  
visible projection;  
preserved pattern;  
loss/risk;  
reconstruction status;  
formal neighbourhood;  
next proof obligation;  
bridge mode;  
stop condition.

Pilot test:

Use the template for several E7G-T research ideas, proof-assistant ideas, or geometry-first mathematics essays. Review whether the note makes next steps clearer.

Outcome:

Fewer vague ideas, clearer formalisation paths, better distinction between insight and proof.

13.14 Phase 4: Create semi-formal artefacts

Before full formalisation, E7G-T should produce semi-formal artefacts.

These include:

controlled glossary;  
operator notation guide;  
claim-strength ladder;  
bridge-mode schema;  
MiniCard schema;  
FullCard schema;  
Proof-of-Path schema;  
RealityBridgeCard schema;  
FailureMode checklist;  
FormalNeighbourhoodCard;  
projection-loss ledger;  
application pilot templates.

These artefacts should be machine-readable where useful.

YAML, JSON, Markdown tables, or simple typed records may be sufficient at first.

The goal is not elegance. The goal is repeatability.

If two people apply the same MiniCard template to the same object, their answers should be comparable. If they differ, the template should show where they differ: boundary, context, preservation, loss, bridge mode, or next move.

This is a necessary step before formalisation.

13.15 Phase 5: Formal experiments

Formal experiments should be small.

Do not attempt to formalise all of E7G-T at once.

Start with toy models.

Experiment 1: Typed claim records.

Define types for Entity, Context, Order, TemporalRegime, BridgeMode, Projection, Loss, ReconstructionStatus, and ClaimTier.

Experiment 2: Projection-loss ledger.

Define a record that requires preserved and lost structure before a projection claim can be raised above a certain tier.

Experiment 3: Bridge-mode linter.

Define rules that prevent empirical verbs unless empirical-testable fields are present.

Experiment 4: Proof-of-Path graph.

Represent proof steps as nodes and transformations as edges, with obligations created and discharged.

Experiment 5: Category-theoretic toy model.

Define a small category of views and admissible projections only if identities and composition can be specified.

Experiment 6: Proof assistant annotation.

Create an external annotation schema for Lean-like proof states without modifying the proof assistant.

Formal experiments should have failure criteria.

If the formalisation becomes too awkward, the vocabulary may need revision. If the schema does not catch real errors, the linter is weak. If users cannot understand the output, the interface projection is poor.

13.16 Phase 6: Proof-assistant and interface prototypes

A proof-assistant interface prototype is one of the most promising technical paths.

It does not need to prove E7G-T. It needs to show that E7G-T can improve proof navigation.

Possible prototype levels:

Level A: manual annotation guide.

A document showing how to label proof steps with E7G-T move types.

Level B: structured proof note template.

A Proof-of-Path card filled alongside a proof assistant session.

Level C: external parser or notebook.

A tool that records theorem statement, goals, tactic steps, errors, and explanation notes.

Level D: plugin mock-up.

A visual interface concept showing entity, boundary, move, obligations, hidden structure, and status.

Level E: working plugin.

An actual integration with a proof assistant environment, if feasible.

The research question is practical:

Does this help users understand proof movement better?

If yes, E7G-T has found a real technical niche.

13.17 Phase 7: Reality Bridge Atlas

The Reality Bridge Atlas should be built slowly.

Each entry should be a disciplined bridge card, not a grand claim.

Initial entries might include:

AI output as projection.

Dashboard as business projection.

Translation as invariant-preserving projection.

Proof assistant as projection interface.

Quantum measurement as formal analogy.

Nested projection stack as fictional/contemplative model.

Dimensionality reduction as technical analogy.

Software bug trace as projection.

Medical measurement as empirical projection with domain constraints.

Legal document translation as administrative bridge.

Each entry should state:

source model;  
target domain;  
bridge mode;  
preserved structure;  
lost structure;  
distortion risk;  
non-admissible use;  
next move;  
stop condition.

The atlas will become a safety mechanism. It will show users how to apply the framework without upgrading claims too far.

13.18 Public writing path

Public writing should be staged.

Stage 1: accessible essays.

Topics:

The chart is not the business.

AI fluency is not evidence.

A proof sketch is not a proof object.

Translation is controlled projection.

Why metaphors need bridge modes.

Stage 2: practical guide.

A book or course for general readers showing how to use OneLine and MiniCard reasoning.

Stage 3: foundations monograph.

The present work, revised, source-backed where needed, and positioned for serious readers.

Stage 4: technical notes.

Proof-of-Path, linter schemas, bridge cards, proof-assistant interface ideas.

Stage 5: pilot reports.

Short case studies showing what was tested, what improved, and what failed.

This sequence avoids leading with the heaviest or grandest claim.

The public entry point should be practical repair.

13.19 Source-backed development

Where E7G-T discusses established fields, it should cite public sources and avoid pretending to originate established ideas.

Potential source-backed areas include:

philosophy of mathematical practice;  
diagrammatic reasoning;  
projective geometry;  
topology;  
category theory;  
type theory;  
proof assistants;  
formal verification;  
model theory;  
measurement theory;  
statistics;  
translation studies;  
information theory;  
cognitive science;  
systems theory.

The project may use internal reasoning scaffolds while drafting, but public-facing academic or semi-academic writing should rely on public sources.

This protects credibility.

The rule is:

Use internal frameworks to reason; use public sources to support public claims.

13.20 Naming and positioning

The name E7G-T is compact for the kernel but may be difficult for general readers.

Different public layers may need different names.

Possible positioning layers:

E7G-T: technical kernel.

Holographic Extensional Mathematics: broader philosophical/foundational name.

Practical Projection Calculus: applied tool name.

Proof-of-Path: proof/interface product name.

Reality Bridge Atlas: bridge-discipline resource.

Projection-Loss Method: business/AI/translation-friendly name.

The public name should match the audience.

For mathematicians, avoid sounding like a grand metaphysical system.

For business users, avoid heavy D-order notation.

For proof-assistant users, emphasise proof path, state, obligations, and interface.

For fiction readers, the geometry-first civilisation canon can be more imaginative.

The rule is:

One kernel, several controlled projections.

13.21 Community and review

E7G-T should seek review from different communities for different parts.

Mathematicians can challenge formal language, novelty, and misuse of mathematical terms.

Proof-assistant users can test Proof-of-Path and interface claims.

Software engineers can test bug-triage and architecture cards.

Data practitioners can test dashboard and model-output cards.

Translators can test invariant-preserving translation QA cards.

Philosophers can challenge the conceptual claims.

Physicists can warn against physics overreach.

Business users can test whether the method actually improves decisions.

Each reviewer should be asked a narrow question.

Not:

Is E7G-T true?

But:

Does this card expose a real error?  
Does this linter improve review?  
Does this proof-path label help understanding?  
Is this bridge claim overstated?  
Is this terminology misleading in your field?  
What mature tool already does this better?

Narrow review produces useful criticism.

13.22 Versioning and change control

A project like E7G-T needs versioning.

Each public kernel should have:

version number;  
date;  
status;  
change log;  
added concepts;  
removed concepts;  
renamed concepts;  
known weaknesses;  
compatibility notes;  
recommended public claim posture.

This is especially important because early versions may contain speculative language that later needs discipline.

A reader should know whether a chapter, card, or pilot refers to kernel 0.8, 0.9, 1.0, or later.

Versioning prevents conceptual drift.

The rule is:

If the kernel changes, projections must declare which kernel they project.

13.23 The v0.9 target

A plausible v0.9 target is an Application Atlas version.

Kernel v0.9 should not try to be final. It should add tested application entries.

Possible v0.9 deliverables:

stable v0.8 definitions cleaned;  
application atlas structure;  
AI-output linter entry;  
dashboard projection entry;  
translation QA entry;  
proof-assistant entry;  
software bug-triage entry;  
reality bridge entry for quantum measurement marked as formal analogy;  
fictional nested projection stack entry marked as what-if;  
updated examples;  
reduced notation clutter;  
clear public status statement.

The v0.9 question is:

Can users apply E7G-T correctly in several domains without overclaiming?

If yes, the project is ready for wider testing.

13.24 The v1.0 target

A v1.0 stable public kernel should be conservative.

It should include only concepts that survived use, review, and revision.

Possible v1.0 criteria:

core terms stable;  
primitive moves stable;  
bridge modes stable;  
notation readable;  
Practical Calculus usable;  
Reality Bridge Discipline integrated;  
Failure Modes integrated;  
application examples tested;  
anti-replacement rule prominent;  
public posture clear;  
no uncontrolled ToE language;  
no empirical physics claims without bridge;  
source-backed references for formal neighbourhoods;  
roadmap for future work.

The v1.0 question is:

Can the kernel be shared publicly without inviting the most damaging misunderstandings?

A stable public kernel does not mean the research is complete. It means the project can be responsibly discussed.

13.25 Research outputs

The programme may produce several kinds of outputs.

1\. Kernel documents.

Versioned source of definitions and laws.

2\. Practical guide.

Accessible book or course for users.

3\. Foundations monograph.

The deeper conceptual work.

4\. Application atlas.

Reusable domain entries.

5\. Bridge atlas.

Reality-facing claim discipline.

6\. Proof-of-Path toolkit.

Templates, annotations, interface concepts.

7\. Linter schemas.

Machine-readable records for AI, dashboards, proof notes, and bridge claims.

8\. Pilot reports.

Evidence of practical usefulness or failure.

9\. Fictional canon.

Geometry-first mathematics for science-fiction worldbuilding, clearly bounded as fiction.

10\. Essays and talks.

Public projections for different audiences.

Different outputs carry different claim strength.

The kernel governs. The guide teaches. The atlas applies. The pilots test. The monograph reflects. The fictional canon imagines.

13.26 Success criteria

The programme should define success modestly.

Early success is not proving new physics or solving unsolved mathematics.

Early success could be:

A user stops treating a dashboard as direct business reality.

A translator uses invariant hierarchy to resolve a QA dispute.

A proof-assistant learner better understands tactic movement.

An AI answer is downgraded before unsupported reliance.

A software engineer separates symptom, trace, and cause more cleanly.

A research note distinguishes analogy from proof.

A speculative bridge is marked as what-if instead of empirical claim.

A public reader understands projection-loss accounting.

These are real successes.

Medium-term success could be:

Reusable templates;  
working linting prompts;  
small pilot evidence;  
review by domain experts;  
proof-assistant annotation prototype;  
application atlas entries;  
source-backed papers or essays.

Long-term success could be:

A recognised modelling-language layer;  
a useful proof-interface tool;  
a practical projection-loss method;  
a stable bridge atlas;  
a community of users applying the framework responsibly.

13.27 Failure criteria

The programme should also define failure.

E7G-T fails if it becomes mostly vocabulary without operational consequence.

It fails if users overclaim it as physics, metaphysics, or replacement mathematics.

It fails if notation creates more confusion than clarity.

It fails if application pilots do not improve real tasks.

It fails if mature field experts consistently say the terminology is misleading and the project refuses to adapt.

It fails if commercial versions remove stop rules.

It fails if AI-generated expansions become authority without review.

It fails if no public artefact can be used by another person without the author explaining it live.

It fails if it cannot downgrade claims.

Failure criteria are useful because they keep the project honest.

13.28 The next immediate actions

After this foundations draft, the next immediate actions should be practical.

1\. Review the document as a whole.

Check repetition, tone, claim discipline, notation consistency, and chapter flow.

2\. Extract the kernel-facing definitions.

Update the v0.8 kernel toward v0.9 with any stable improvements from the monograph.

3\. Extract the practical guide outline.

Turn the accessible parts into a shorter reader-facing book plan.

4\. Create the first Application Atlas entries.

Start with AI output, dashboard, translation QA, proof-assistant session, and speculative physics bridge.

5\. Create machine-readable card templates.

MiniCard, FullCard, ProofOfPathCard, RealityBridgeCard, FailureMode checklist.

6\. Run one small pilot.

Choose the easiest: AI-output linter or translation QA card.

7\. Prepare a short public positioning statement.

Explain E7G-T without grand claims.

These actions are enough. The programme should not begin by trying to solve everything.

13.29 A possible public positioning statement

A concise public statement might be:

E7G-T is a geometry-first modelling language for reasoning about extension, projection, context, and claim strength. It helps users distinguish source from view, slice from whole, metaphor from proof, formal analogy from empirical claim, and model from reality. Its practical calculus provides lightweight templates for inspecting dashboards, AI outputs, translations, proof paths, software traces, and speculative bridges. It is not a replacement for established mathematics, science, or domain expertise; it is a discipline for knowing when those tools are needed.

This statement is deliberately modest.

It makes the project easier to test and harder to misunderstand.

13.30 Closing: the next move

This book began from the under-geometrised condition: the tendency to begin reasoning after structure has already been compressed into algebra, prose, dashboard, diagram, measurement, proof sketch, AI answer, or narrative.

E7G-T responds by asking the modeller to return to extension, boundary, incidence, slice, projection, transformation, invariant, reconstruction, context, and bridge.

Its deepest practical question is not:

What grand theory explains everything?

It is:

What am I looking at, what has been preserved, what has been lost, and what may I responsibly do next?

If the framework can keep that question alive, it has value.

If it can help users stop one overclaim, repair one proof path, check one AI answer, clarify one translation, avoid one dashboard mistake, or mark one speculative bridge honestly, it has already begun to function.

The research programme is therefore not a demand for finality. It is a path of disciplined continuation.

The next move is to test the calculus where projection errors actually happen.

Editorial Review Notes — First Pass

ER.1 Overall verdict

The foundations draft now has a complete conceptual arc.

It begins with the under-geometrised condition, establishes orders of extension, separates extension from projection, introduces boundary/incidence/slice/invariant discipline, treats algebra as rendering, clarifies temporal regimes, disciplines holographic reading, operationalises the system through practical calculus, positions the framework near formal traditions, develops Proof-of-Path, defines Reality Bridge Discipline, names failure modes, and closes with a research programme.

The strongest feature of the draft is not any single metaphor. It is the repeated accounting discipline:

What is the entity of concern?  
What is the boundary?  
What is the projection?  
What is preserved?  
What is lost?  
What reconstruction status is justified?  
What bridge mode is active?  
What is the next admissible move?  
What overread is blocked?

This gives the volume coherence.

The draft is now ready for revision, not expansion.

ER.2 Structural strengths

The book has several strong structural assets.

First, it has a clear movement from diagnosis to method. The opening problem is not abstract: reasoning often begins after structure has already been compressed. The later chapters repeatedly show how to recover accountability from compressed artefacts.

Second, the distinction between source and view is stable throughout. This becomes the main thread linking algebra, diagrams, dashboards, AI answers, proof texts, translations, measurements, and speculative bridges.

Third, the anti-overclaim discipline is now strong. The book repeatedly blocks the most dangerous readings: E7G-T is not empirical physics, not established mathematics, not a proof system, not a replacement for mature tools, and not a Theory of Everything.

Fourth, the practical calculus layer gives the foundation operational value. OneLine, MiniCard, FullCard, Proof-of-Path, RealityBridgeCard, and FailureMode checks can become reusable artefacts.

Fifth, the final chapters prevent the book from sounding like a closed doctrine. Formal Neighbourhoods, Reality Bridge Discipline, Objections, and Research Programme all make the framework more credible by limiting it.

ER.3 Main revision risks

The main risks are repetition, density, and defensive tone.

Several core warnings recur many times:

projection is not identity;  
projection is not reconstruction;  
model is not reality;  
metaphor is not proof;  
formal analogy is not empirical support;  
D-orders are not literal physical dimensions;  
E7G-T does not replace mature tools.

These repetitions are useful as governing laws, but in revision they should be made more deliberate. Some should become boxed axioms, chapter epigraphs, or cross-referenced laws rather than being restated in full each time.

The second risk is list density. Many chapters contain long lists of questions, examples, and templates. These are useful but may fatigue the reader. Revision should decide which lists belong in the main body and which should move to appendices.

The third risk is that the book sometimes defines itself by what it is not. This is necessary for claim discipline, but too much negation can make the tone defensive. Revision should balance “not a replacement” with positive use cases: what the framework actually helps users do.

ER.4 Priority revision tasks

Before the document becomes a public draft, the following tasks are high priority.

1\. Add a “How to read this book” note near the beginning.

This should tell readers that the book is a foundation draft, not a textbook in standard mathematics, not empirical physics, and not a practical guide. It should explain that the practical guide and bridge atlas are separate projections.

2\. Add a compact glossary.

Terms such as EntityOfConcern, extension, projection, boundary, incidence, slice, invariant, reconstruction, bridge mode, D-order, temporal regime, and admissible use should be defined in one place.

3\. Add a notation table.

The notation introduced across the chapters should be gathered and normalised.

4\. Add a claim-status statement.

The public status of the work should be visible early: E7G-T is a geometry-first modelling language and practical calculus under development.

5\. Add source-backed references later.

The current draft is conceptually coherent, but formal neighbourhood chapters need public sources before academic-facing release.

6\. Reduce repeated warnings.

Keep the warnings, but convert some repetitions into references to named laws.

7\. Decide what belongs in appendices.

Card templates, long checklists, and formalisation schemas may be cleaner as appendices.

ER.5 Repetition consolidation targets

The following repeated ideas should become named recurring laws or cross-references.

Projection is not identity.

Projection is not reconstruction.

Projection loss accumulates.

Boundary changes claim.

No invariant without transformation class.

Bridge claims inherit the weakest link.

Empirical language requires empirical bridge.

No mature tool replacement.

Notation records discipline; it does not create discipline.

Contemplative value does not require claim inflation.

These should remain visible, but not every chapter needs to reargue them from scratch.

ER.6 Claim-discipline check

The draft is currently strong on claim discipline.

The most important protective claims are present:

E7G-T is not established mathematics.

E7G-T is not empirical physics.

E7G-T is not a replacement for mature disciplines.

D0–D7 are modelling orders, not literal physical dimensions by default.

Temporal regimes are modelling regimes, not a theory of time by default.

Holographic reading is projection discipline, not proof of a holographic universe.

Proof-of-Path is an accountability layer, not a proof system.

Reality Bridge Discipline is required before reality-facing claims.

These should be preserved in every public projection.

The biggest revision task is to make the positive purpose equally memorable:

E7G-T helps users inspect how views, models, proofs, dashboards, translations, measurements, AI outputs, and speculative claims preserve some structure while losing other structure.

ER.7 Notation consistency check

The notation is usable but needs normalisation.

Current core placement form:

X : Dn @ TRi / C

This should consistently mean:

X is treated as order Dn under temporal regime TRi within context C.

Projection notation appears in several forms:

π\_C^{n→k}(X) \= Y

X : Dn @ TRi / C  \--π\_C^{n→k}--\>  Y : Dk @ TRj / C'

Both are useful. Revision should define the compact form and the readable arrow form separately.

Reconstruction notation should be standardised. The draft uses variants such as:

ρ\_C^{k→n}(Y) ⇝ X?

ρ(Y) ⇒ {X\_i}

The first is useful for tentative reconstruction toward a candidate source. The second is useful for set-valued reconstruction. The notation guide should explain both.

Bridge notation should be stabilised:

β\_M^{A↔B}

This should consistently mean a bridge between domains or contexts A and B under mode M.

The notation guide should also warn that notation is optional in practical use. Plain-language OneLines are often enough.

ER.8 Source and citation needs

The draft is not yet source-backed enough for academic-facing release.

The source plan should prioritise public references for the formal-neighbourhood chapter and any claim touching established disciplines.

Needed source areas include:

projective geometry;  
topology;  
category theory;  
type theory;  
proof theory;  
proof assistants;  
formal verification;  
model theory;  
diagrammatic reasoning;  
systems theory;  
measurement theory;  
statistics and data science;  
information theory;  
translation studies;  
philosophy of mathematical practice;  
cognitive science where cognition is discussed.

The physics-facing sections should remain deliberately conservative. They do not need to prove physics claims if they are framed as bridge-discipline examples, but any references to quantum mechanics, holography, or dimensionality reduction should be source-backed if the work becomes public.

ER.9 Tone and audience notes

The current tone is generally sober and usable, but it has three audience layers mixed together.

Layer 1: foundation readers.

They need definitions, distinctions, objections, and formal-neighbourhood discipline.

Layer 2: practical users.

They need examples, cards, checklists, and next-move prompts.

Layer 3: speculative/fictional/metaphysical users.

They need bridge modes and stop rules so imaginative use does not become overclaim.

The monograph can contain all three, but revision should signal which layer is active in each section.

Possible editorial device:

Foundation note.

Practical note.

Bridge caution.

Worked example.

This would make the text easier to navigate.

ER.10 Extraction targets for v0.9

The foundations draft contains enough material to extract a v0.9 kernel.

v0.9 should not copy the full monograph. It should extract the stable operational core.

Kernel v0.9 extraction targets:

status statement;  
core definitions;  
D0–D7 order table;  
TR0–TR6 temporal regime table;  
primitive moves;  
practical laws;  
bridge modes;  
claim-strength ladder;  
notation guide;  
OneLine template;  
MiniCard template;  
FullCard template;  
ProofOfPathCard;  
RealityBridgeCard;  
FormalNeighbourhoodCard;  
FailureMode checklist;  
anti-replacement rule;  
public-use cautions;  
example set.

This kernel should be shorter, stricter, and more reference-like than the monograph.

ER.11 Extraction targets for the practical guide

The practical guide should not begin with D0–D7.

It should begin with everyday projection errors.

Suggested guide entry path:

The chart is not the business.

The AI answer is not evidence.

The proof sketch is not the proof.

The translation is not the source.

The measurement is not the whole system.

The model is not reality.

Then introduce the repair:

What am I looking at?  
What is the source?  
What is preserved?  
What is lost?  
What context is active?  
What may I responsibly do next?

Only after that should the guide introduce OneLine, MiniCard, D-orders, temporal regimes, and bridge modes.

The practical guide should be shorter, more example-driven, and less defensive than the foundations monograph.

ER.12 Extraction targets for the Application Atlas

The first Application Atlas should contain a small number of disciplined entries.

Recommended first entries:

1\. AI output as projection.

2\. Business dashboard as projection.

3\. Translation as invariant-preserving projection.

4\. Proof assistant session as projection interface.

5\. Software bug trace as projection.

6\. Quantum measurement as formal analogy only.

7\. Nested projection stack as fictional/contemplative model.

Each entry should include:

entity;  
source;  
view;  
projection context;  
preserved structure;  
lost structure;  
distortion risk;  
bridge mode;  
admissible use;  
non-admissible use;  
next move;  
stop condition.

This atlas should be the first practical bridge between the foundations text and real users.

ER.13 Suggested appendices

The monograph would benefit from appendices.

Appendix A: Glossary.

Appendix B: Notation guide.

Appendix C: Card templates.

Appendix D: Practical laws.

Appendix E: Failure-mode checklist.

Appendix F: Bridge modes and claim-strength ladder.

Appendix G: First Application Atlas entries.

Appendix H: Source bibliography.

Moving templates and checklists into appendices would reduce list density in the main chapters while preserving practical value.

ER.14 Immediate next editing pass

The next editing pass should not add more theory.

It should perform cleanup and extraction.

Recommended order:

1\. Add “How to read this book”.

2\. Add status and claim posture note.

3\. Add glossary draft.

4\. Add notation table.

5\. Review TOC and section numbering.

6\. Mark repeated laws for consolidation.

7\. Identify appendix material.

8\. Extract v0.9 kernel draft.

9\. Extract practical guide outline.

10\. Create first Application Atlas entry.

The first concrete next move should be either:

Add the front-matter “How to read this book” note.

or:

Extract Kernel v0.9 from the completed draft.

For continuity, the front-matter note should come first because it will govern how the whole document is read.

Appendix A — Glossary Draft

A.1 Purpose

This glossary gathers the core terms used across the foundations draft so that later revisions can avoid redefining the same ideas repeatedly. These are working definitions for E7G-T as a geometry-first modelling language and practical calculus, not final mathematical definitions.

A.2 Core terms

Admissible claim posture

The declared strength and permitted use of a claim. A claim may be intuitive, metaphorical, contemplative, interpretive, formally analogous, formally compatible, formally proved, empirically testable, empirically supported, or operationally validated.

Admissible use

The use a representation, model, proof, bridge, or output can responsibly support. A dashboard may support review but not final diagnosis. A metaphor may support thinking but not proof.

Algebra as rendering

The treatment of algebra as a compressed, symbolic, coordinate, transformational, or invariant-preserving view of a richer structure. E7G-T does not reject algebra. It asks what geometry, boundary, projection, loss, and transformation the algebra is rendering.

Application Atlas

A collection of disciplined worked entries showing how E7G-T applies to specific domains. Each entry should declare entity, source, view, projection context, preservation, loss, bridge mode, admissible use, non-admissible use, next move, and stop condition.

Atemporal whole / local slice

A modelling pattern in which a source is treated as complete or whole under one temporal regime while an observer or user experiences local ordered slices under another regime.

Boundary

The declared limit of the entity of concern for the current operation. Boundary answers what is inside the object of analysis, what is outside it, and why that boundary is legitimate for the claim being made.

Bridge mode

The declared mode by which language, structure, analogy, or method moves between domains. Bridge modes include none, contemplative what-if, philosophical interpretation, formal analogy, formal compatibility, and empirical-testable.

Claim-strength ladder

The ordered set of possible claim postures. It prevents intuition, metaphor, formal analogy, empirical testability, formal proof, and operational validation from being confused.

Context

The governing frame under which an entity is placed, bounded, sliced, projected, transformed, interpreted, or used. Context includes purpose, observer, measurement protocol, formal substrate, user need, publication setting, or operational environment.

D-order

A modelling order of extension from D0 to D7. D-orders are not literal physical dimensions by default. They identify the role an entity plays in the current model: mark, path, surface, form, history, variation, law, or context.

D0

Point-locality, local mark, scalar output, or projected trace.

D1

Line-extension, path, sequence, interval, or directed passage.

D2

Surface-extension, field, map, diagram, screen, text surface, or projected face.

D3

Form-extension, body, volume, object, artefact, or local form.

D4

Duration-extension, history, world-form, record, process through time, or local sequence.

D5

Variation-extension, possibility field, branch-space, design space, option set, or deformation space.

D6

Transformation-extension, operator, law-space, rule-space, dynamic, method, procedure, or permitted transformation class.

D7

Context-extension, interpretive frame, measurement context, projection governance, user purpose, publication frame, or admissible-use context.

Entity of concern

The primary object, claim, proof, model, output, system, artefact, observation, or question currently being analysed.

Extension

The act or structure by which an entity gains order, relation, boundary, path, surface, form, history, variation, law, or context. Extension builds or discloses richer structure. Extension is not projection.

Failure mode

A predictable way a model, proof, representation, or claim goes wrong. Common failure modes include false identity, false reconstruction, hidden boundary shift, bridge inflation, temporal overread, and interface fluency.

Formal compatibility

A bridge mode in which a structure in E7G-T can be mapped into or compared with a recognised formal system under declared definitions.

Formal neighbourhood

An established mathematical, logical, computational, scientific, or philosophical field near which an E7G-T concept may be compared. Neighbourhood is not identity.

FullCard

A Level 3 practical calculus record containing enough information for reliance: entity, claim, order profile, temporal regime, boundary, projection path, preservation/loss, transformation class, reconstruction status, bridge mode, weakest link, admissible use, and stop condition.

Holographic reading

A disciplined way of reading a lower-order view as possibly preserving selected structure from a richer source under a projection context. It must name preservation, loss, distortion risk, reconstruction status, and admissible use.

Incidence

A declared relation between entities. Incidence prevents vague connectedness from doing too much work.

Invariant

A structure, relation, quantity, boundary, meaning, or rule that survives a declared transformation class. No invariant claim is complete without a transformation class.

Kernel

The compact operational core of E7G-T. The kernel contains stable definitions, orders, temporal regimes, primitive moves, laws, bridge modes, notation, templates, and stop rules.

MiniCard

A Level 2 practical calculus record for modelling a claim or artefact before relying on it.

Notation

A compact recording device for E7G-T discipline. Notation helps preserve placement, context, projection, reconstruction, transformation, and bridge mode. Notation does not create rigour by itself.

OneLine

A Level 1 practical calculus statement. OneLine gives the minimum useful placement and next move for an entity or claim.

Order profile

The set of orders an entity participates in. E7G-T usually prefers order profiles over single order labels.

Practical calculus

The operational layer of E7G-T. It answers: what do I do next with this object, claim, proof, model, observation, system, or output?

Preservation

The structure that survives a projection, transformation, translation, measurement, rendering, or representation.

Projection

A rendering, descent, compression, view, output, measurement, text, diagram, dashboard, interface, translation, or other lower-order appearance of a richer source or candidate source. Projection is not identity. Projection is not reconstruction.

Projection loss

The structure not preserved by a projection. Projection loss is normal and must be accounted for.

Proof-of-Path

A structured account of the admissible route by which a claim, model, proof, or output moves from entity placement through transformations, projections, checks, bridges, and stop conditions to a stated reliance tier.

Reality Bridge Discipline

The rule-set governing reality-facing claims. It requires bridge mode, standard substrate where relevant, observation protocol, measurement map, prediction or constraint, falsification path, evidence path, weakest-link review, and stop condition.

Reconstruction

An attempted ascent from a projection or trace toward a candidate source. Reconstruction is not projection in reverse. It is usually underdetermined unless supported by formal constraints, source access, empirical evidence, or rival-source elimination.

Reliance tier

The level of practical trust a claim or artefact can support. Reliance tier must not exceed completed checks.

Slice

A local, selected, partial, temporal, spatial, formal, interface, or contextual view of a richer entity. A slice is not the whole.

Source

The richer entity, system, structure, text, proof object, formal substrate, observation field, or candidate object from which a projection, view, trace, or representation may derive.

Standard substrate

The formal, empirical, computational, observational, or operational environment in which a stronger claim is checked.

Stop condition

The declared reason to pause, downgrade, reject, or refuse a claim.

Temporal regime

A modelling declaration about how time-like structure is treated. Temporal regimes include no-time, local-time, shared-time, branching-time, cyclic-time, meta-time, and timeless completion.

Transformation

A change under a declared rule, operator, procedure, tactic, function, translation rule, measurement protocol, or law-space.

Weakest link

The least-supported element in a bridge, proof path, empirical claim, or operational chain.

A.3 Revision use

In the next revision pass, chapters should refer back to these terms instead of redefining them repeatedly. This will reduce density while preserving rigour.

Appendix B — Notation Guide and Cleanup

B.1 Purpose of notation

E7G-T notation records modelling discipline. It does not replace explanation, proof, empirical evidence, or domain expertise.

Notation is optional in everyday use. A clear OneLine is often better than a cramped symbolic expression. Use notation when it prevents ambiguity, not when it merely makes the text look formal.

B.2 Clean presentation rule

A clean E7G-T expression should normally make visible:

entity;  
order;  
temporal regime;  
context;  
operation;  
output;  
preservation/loss where projection is involved;  
bridge mode where cross-domain language is involved;  
reconstruction status where source claims are involved.

The governing rule is:

Readable before compressed.

B.3 Placement notation

Compact form:

X : Dn @ TRi / C

Meaning:

X is treated as order Dn under temporal regime TRi within context C.

Placement notation does not say what X ultimately is. It says how X is being treated in this modelling move.

B.4 Boundary notation

Compact form:

∂X \= B

Meaning:

The boundary of X for the current operation is B.

Readable form:

Boundary(X / C) \= B

B.5 Incidence notation

Compact form:

A ⋈\_C B

Meaning:

A and B stand in a declared incidence relation under context C.

The relation kind should be named in prose: depends on, refers to, measures, translates, verifies, constrains, or projects from.

B.6 Slice notation

Compact form:

S\_C^s(X)

Meaning:

The slice s of X selected under context C.

Rule:

State the slice selector before making the whole claim.

B.7 Projection notation

Compact form:

π\_C^{n→k}(X) \= Y

Meaning:

Under context C, X is projected from order n to order k, producing Y.

Readable arrow form:

X : Dn @ TRi / C  \--π\_C^{n→k}--\>  Y : Dk @ TRj / C'

Full projection line:

X : Dn @ TRi / C  \--π\_C^{n→k}--\>  Y : Dk @ TRj / C'  \[preserves: P; loses: L; use: U\]

B.8 Transformation notation

Compact form:

τ\_T(X) \= Y

Meaning:

X is transformed into Y under transformation class T.

Rule:

No invariant without transformation class.

B.9 Invariant notation

Compact form:

Inv\_T(X) \= I

Meaning:

I is invariant in X under transformation class T.

B.10 Reconstruction notation

Candidate reconstruction form:

ρ\_C^{k→n}(Y) ⇝ X?

Meaning:

Under context C, Y is used to attempt reconstruction from order k to order n toward candidate source X. The question mark marks uncertainty.

Set-valued reconstruction form:

ρ\_C(Y) ⇒ {X\_i}

Meaning:

Y constrains a set of possible sources, not one guaranteed source.

Rule:

Compatible with source does not mean reconstructed from source.

B.11 Bridge notation

Compact form:

β\_M^{A↔B}

Meaning:

A bridge between domains or contexts A and B under mode M.

Example:

β\_formal-analogy^{QuantumMeasurement↔Projection}

Meaning:

Quantum measurement is being compared with projection as a formal analogy. This does not create an empirical physics claim.

B.12 Stop notation

Readable form:

Stop(reason)

Examples:

Stop(no empirical-testable bridge)  
Stop(reconstruction underdetermined)  
Stop(boundary mismatch)  
Stop(formal substrate not declared)  
Stop(interface fluency not evidence)

Stop is not failure. It is claim discipline.

B.13 OneLine pattern

Preferred public form:

E7-OneLine: X is treated as \[order/role\] under \[context\]; admissible move: \[next move\]; blocked overread: \[one phrase\].

B.14 Clean projection pattern

Source : Dn @ TRi / SourceContext  \--π\_ProjectionContext^{n→k}--\>  View : Dk @ TRj / UseContext  \[preserves: P; loses: L; distortion risk: R; reconstruction: S; use: U\]

B.15 Normalisation decisions for revision

Use X : Dn @ TRi / C as the standard placement form.

Use π\_C^{n→k}(X) \= Y as the compact projection form.

Use the readable arrow form for public examples.

Use ρ\_C^{k→n}(Y) ⇝ X? when reconstruction is candidate-directed.

Use ρ\_C(Y) ⇒ {X\_i} when reconstruction is set-valued.

Use τ\_T(X) \= Y for transformation.

Use Inv\_T(X) \= I for invariant claims.

Use β\_M^{A↔B} for bridge claims.

Use Stop(reason) for disciplined termination.

Do not invent new notation unless a repeated ambiguity requires it.

B.16 Anti-cramping rule

If a one-line expression becomes unreadable, expand it.

Bad compression hides the very projection loss E7G-T is meant to reveal.

Kernel v0.9 Extract — Draft

K9.1 Status

E7G-T v0.9 is a geometry-first modelling language and practical calculus under development.

It is not established mathematics.  
It is not empirical physics.  
It is not a proof system.  
It is not a replacement for mature formal, empirical, legal, medical, engineering, or domain-specific methods.  
It is not a Theory of Everything.

Its purpose is to make extension, projection, boundary, context, preservation, loss, reconstruction, bridge mode, and admissible use more visible.

K9.2 Core question

What do I do next with this concrete object, claim, proof, model, output, observation, system, or representation?

K9.3 Core discipline

Before applying an operation, declare:

entity of concern;  
order profile;  
temporal regime;  
context;  
boundary;  
operation;  
preservation/loss if projection is involved;  
transformation class if invariance is claimed;  
reconstruction status if source claims are involved;  
bridge mode if domains are crossed;  
admissible use;  
stop condition.

K9.4 D-order table

D0 — point-locality, local mark, scalar output, trace, pixel, accepted state, result.

D1 — line-extension, path, interval, sequence, procedure, timeline segment, proof-step path.

D2 — surface-extension, field, map, text surface, diagram, dashboard, screen, interface, visible proof text.

D3 — form-extension, object, body, artefact, local system, product, structured proof artefact when treated formally.

D4 — duration-extension, history, process, world-form, version history, event sequence, lived development.

D5 — variation-extension, possibility field, branch space, option set, design space, model alternatives, proof-search space.

D6 — transformation-extension, rule-space, operator, law, tactic, function, protocol, method, verification rule, permitted transformation class.

D7 — context-extension, interpretation frame, measurement context, user purpose, publication frame, bridge governance, admissible-use frame.

Rule:

D0–D7 are modelling orders, not literal physical dimensions by default.

K9.5 Temporal regime table

TR0 — no-time / atemporal treatment.

TR1 — local-time / local ordered sequence.

TR2 — shared-time / coordinated time between multiple local sequences.

TR3 — branching-time / alternatives, forecasts, options, proof search, scenario space.

TR4 — cyclic-time / recurrence, rhythm, periodic structure, iteration.

TR5 — super-time / meta-time / time about time / model-time governing object-time.

TR6 — timeless-completion / closed whole under a declared closure context.

Rule:

Temporal regimes are modelling declarations, not claims about ultimate physical time by default.

K9.6 Primitive moves

1\. Place

Declare the entity’s order, temporal regime, and context.

Notation:

X : Dn @ TRi / C

2\. Bound

Declare the operational boundary.

Notation:

∂X \= B

3\. Incide

Declare the relation between entities.

Notation:

A ⋈\_C B

4\. Extend

Move toward richer structure by adding declared extension.

Notation:

X ↑ n

5\. Slice

Select a local or contextual view.

Notation:

S\_C^s(X)

6\. Project

Render a lower-order view from a richer source or candidate source.

Notation:

π\_C^{n→k}(X) \= Y

7\. Preserve/Lose

Name what survives and what does not survive a projection or transformation.

8\. Transform

Apply a declared rule-space.

Notation:

τ\_T(X) \= Y

9\. Reconstruct

Attempt source recovery from a projection or trace, usually set-valued or tentative.

Notation:

ρ\_C(Y) ⇒ {X\_i}

10\. Bridge/Stop

Declare bridge mode or stop the claim.

Notation:

β\_M^{A↔B}  
Stop(reason)

K9.7 Practical laws

Law 1 — Typing Before Operation.

Place the entity before operating on it.

Law 2 — Extension Is Not Projection.

Building richer structure is not the same as rendering a lower-order view.

Law 3 — Projection Is Not Reconstruction.

A view of a source is not a recovery of the source.

Law 4 — Projection Loss Accumulates.

Loss in a projection stack cannot be silently regained.

Law 5 — Slice and Projection Need Not Commute.

Slicing before projection and projecting before slicing may produce different results.

Law 6 — Boundary Is Contextual But Not Arbitrary.

Boundary may vary by use, but it must match the claim.

Law 7 — Invariance Requires a Transformation Class.

No invariant claim is complete without declaring what transformations it survives.

Law 8 — Bridge Claims Inherit the Weakest Link.

A bridge cannot support a stronger claim than its least-supported component.

Law 9 — Empirical Language Requires Empirical Bridge.

Reality-facing claims require measurement, prediction, falsification, and evidence path.

Law 10 — The Next Move Is the Practical Test.

The value of the model is shown by the next admissible action it clarifies.

K9.8 Bridge modes

None

No bridge is claimed.

Contemplative what-if

Used for imaginative, reflective, fictional, or exploratory thinking.

Philosophical interpretation

Used for conceptual interpretation without formal or empirical force.

Formal analogy

Used where one structure resembles another under declared mapping, but without proof of identity or empirical support.

Formal compatibility

Used where the structure can be mapped into or compared with a recognised formal substrate under declared definitions.

Empirical-testable

Used only where there is a standard substrate, observation protocol, measurement map, prediction or constraint, falsification condition, and evidence path.

K9.9 Claim-strength ladder

1\. Intuition.

2\. Metaphor.

3\. Contemplative what-if.

4\. Philosophical interpretation.

5\. Formal analogy.

6\. Formal compatibility.

7\. Formal proof.

8\. Empirical-testable proposal.

9\. Empirically supported model.

10\. Validated operational method.

Rule:

Do not upgrade claim strength without completing the required bridge, proof, test, or validation.

K9.10 Practical levels

Level 1 — OneLine

Use for quick placement and overread blocking.

Template:

E7-OneLine: X is treated as \[order/role\] under \[context\]; admissible move: \[next move\]; blocked overread: \[one phrase\].

Level 2 — MiniCard

Use for modelling a claim or artefact before relying on it.

Fields:

entity;  
plain meaning;  
placement;  
boundary;  
projection account;  
preserved;  
lost;  
invariant;  
reconstruction status;  
bridge mode;  
next move;  
blocked overread.

Level 3 — FullCard

Use for reliance, publication, bridge claims, proof review, empirical-facing statements, or operational decisions.

Fields:

entity of concern;  
claim;  
order profile;  
temporal regime;  
context;  
boundary;  
extension account;  
projection path;  
preservation/loss;  
transformation class;  
invariant;  
reconstruction status;  
bridge mode;  
weakest link;  
admissible use;  
stop condition.

K9.11 Proof-of-Path kernel

Proof-of-Path is a structured account of the admissible route by which a claim, model, proof, or output moves from entity placement through transformations, projections, checks, bridges, and stop conditions to a stated reliance tier.

It is not a replacement for proof.

ProofOfPathCard fields:

id;  
claim;  
entity;  
substrate;  
assumptions;  
context;  
placement;  
boundary;  
definitions;  
transformations;  
projection events;  
preserved invariants;  
losses;  
checks;  
unresolved obligations;  
bridge mode;  
reliance tier;  
stop condition;  
source links.

Rule:

A Proof-of-Path can include a formal proof, but it does not become a formal proof merely by documenting a path.

K9.12 Reality Bridge kernel

Reality-facing language requires Reality Bridge Discipline.

RealityBridgeCard fields:

claim;  
source domain;  
target domain;  
bridge mode;  
standard substrate;  
observation protocol;  
measurement map;  
prediction or constraint;  
falsification condition;  
evidence path;  
weakest link;  
admissible use;  
non-admissible use;  
stop condition.

Rule:

Empirical-testable is the only bridge mode that can support empirical claims.

K9.13 Formal Neighbourhood kernel

FormalNeighbourhoodCard fields:

field;  
E7G-T element;  
resemblance;  
established tool;  
current bridge mode;  
what E7G-T can learn;  
what E7G-T must not claim;  
possible formalisation path;  
stop condition.

Rule:

Neighbourhood is not identity.

K9.14 Failure-mode checklist

Before relying on an E7G-T account, check for:

false identity;  
false reconstruction;  
hidden boundary shift;  
undeclared context;  
missing temporal regime;  
projection loss denial;  
slice mistaken for whole;  
invariant without transformation class;  
bridge inflation;  
formal-neighbourhood overclaim;  
empirical language without empirical bridge;  
proof text mistaken for proof object;  
AI fluency mistaken for evidence;  
interface accepted-state mistaken for explanation;  
replacement of mature tools;  
missing stop condition.

K9.15 Anti-replacement rule

E7G-T does not replace a mature formal, empirical, operational, legal, medical, engineering, translation, statistical, or domain-specific discipline where that discipline already has stronger tools.

E7G-T may help frame, inspect, document, teach, bridge, or lint reasoning around such tools. It may not bypass them.

K9.16 Public-use cautions

When presenting E7G-T publicly:

Do not call it established mathematics.

Do not call it new physics.

Do not claim that D0–D7 are literal physical dimensions.

Do not claim that holographic reading proves a holographic universe.

Do not treat contemplative usefulness as empirical evidence.

Do not treat metaphor as proof.

Do not treat proof-path documentation as formal proof.

Do not use E7G-T to avoid expert review.

Say instead:

E7G-T is a geometry-first modelling language and practical calculus for making projection, loss, reconstruction, bridge mode, and admissible use visible.

K9.17 Minimal examples

AI answer

E7-OneLine: An AI answer is treated as a D2/D7 textual projection under user-query context; admissible move: verify source and bridge mode; blocked overread: fluency is not evidence.

Business dashboard

E7-OneLine: A dashboard is treated as a D2 projection of a D4/D5 business system under reporting context; admissible move: inspect preserved metrics and lost causes; blocked overread: the chart is not the business.

Translation

E7-OneLine: A translation is treated as a target-language projection of a source text under purpose context; admissible move: identify preserved legal/semantic invariants; blocked overread: translation is not source identity.

Proof assistant session

E7-OneLine: A proof assistant session is treated as a D2/D7 interface projection of D6 formal rule work under kernel context; admissible move: inspect assumptions, proof state, elaboration, and accepted check; blocked overread: accepted interface state is not human explanation.

Speculative physics analogy

E7-OneLine: Quantum measurement-as-projection is treated as formal analogy unless empirical-testable bridge is declared; admissible move: state preservation/loss and stop condition; blocked overread: analogy is not new physics.

K9.18 v0.9 maturity statement

E7G-T v0.9 is mature enough to support disciplined modelling, practical analysis, educational framing, proof-path documentation, AI-output review, translation/accountability framing, dashboard critique, and bridge-mode control.

It is not mature enough to support claims of new mathematics, new physics, empirical confirmation, or replacement of expert methods.

The next maturity step is v1.0 only after:

terms are stabilised;  
notation is normalised;  
public examples are tested;  
formal-neighbourhood sources are added;  
bridge-mode rules are enforced consistently;  
at least one Application Atlas is drafted;  
at least one proof-assistant or software-interface experiment is specified.

K9.19 Next extraction actions

The next practical extraction actions are:

1\. Copy this Kernel v0.9 Extract into a standalone kernel document.

2\. Shorten it into a reference-style specification.

3\. Add examples only where they clarify a rule.

4\. Keep the monograph as explanatory source, not as the kernel itself.

5\. Build the first Application Atlas entry: AI output as projection.

6\. Build the first Proof-of-Path prototype: proof assistant session as accountable path.

7\. Build the first practical guide chapter: The chart is not the business.
